The Government bears the burden and cannot presume every FBI criminal-investigation source confidential, but may rely on a supported, rebuttable inference tied to particular circumstances or a properly defined category, including the crime and the source’s relationship to it.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statutory word confidential describes the circumstances of a source’s cooperation, not simply the identity of the investigating agency. A person may expect restricted law-enforcement sharing while reasonably expecting that the information will not become publicly available. The FBI’s sources nevertheless range too widely to support one inference for everyone. Reporters Committee approved categorical treatment under a different privacy provision and does not remove the factual predicate in Exemption7(D). At the same time, requiring separate direct testimony for every source would disregard circumstances that themselves support the inference. Payment to an informant, the violence of a crime, or a witness’s relationship to it may support an appropriately confined category when the agency explains the connection. The requester remains free to challenge that inference with nonprotected evidence. The Court reserves whether later public testimony waives protection and does not decide the prisoner’s guilt, Brady claim or entitlement to every withheld page.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Department of Justice v. Reporters Committee for Freedom of the Press, 489 U.S.749: distinguished; privacy categories do not establish every source’s confidentiality.
- John Doe Agency v. John Doe Corp., 493 U.S.146: compilation analysis preserved; it does not eliminate the confidentiality requirement.
- Department of State v. Ray, December16,1991: distinct Exemption6 privacy holding preserved.

#### Source identity and criminal-source information receive distinct statutory protection

**Holding and operative rule:** Exemption7(D) protects identity when law-enforcement disclosure could reasonably be expected to reveal a confidential source, including an agency, authority or private institution furnishing information confidentially; it also protects information furnished by a confidential source in a criminal investigation conducted by a criminal-law-enforcement authority. The statute separately covers a lawful national-security intelligence investigation; reasonably segregable nonexempt material remains subject to release, but redacting identity alone does not remove protection for covered source information.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the two statutory protections must be applied according to their different objects. One prevents disclosure that could reasonably reveal the confidential source; the other protects information furnished by such a source in the specified criminal-investigation setting. Landano therefore cannot obtain protected source information merely by proposing deletion of the source’s name. Nor can the FBI withhold an entire file simply because it contains material from one protected source: confidentiality must first be established and segregation must address material outside the exemption. John Doe Agency requires attention to the statutory law-enforcement compilation requirement rather than the original location of the paper, while Rose preserves FOIA’s disclosure duty for nonexempt material. The present remand concerns FBI criminal-investigation records, not the scope of the separate national-security clause. Protected submissions and in-camera examination may assist the court, but no universal requirement to use either procedure is imposed.

**Precedent treatment:**

- John Doe Agency v. John Doe Corp., 493 U.S.146: statutory compilation requirement applied.
- Department of Air Force v. Rose, 425 U.S.352: nonexempt segregable disclosure preserved, without overriding protected source information.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: confidentiality could be established expressly or by implication, but the proper scope of FBI presumptions was disputed. After: no universal presumption; reasonable, supported and rebuttable narrower categories remain available. Identity protection, criminal-source information protection and segregation remain separate. Public-testimony waiver, other exemptions and criminal merits remain unresolved here.

#### Lincoln v. Vigil — 1993-05-24

[Canonical Decision Record](records/lincoln_merits_1993-05-24.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0.

#### Allocation of this unearmarked lump sum is committed to agency discretion

**Holding and operative rule:** When Congress supplies a lump-sum appropriation without a binding program earmark or other meaningful allocation standard, an agency’s distribution among authorized statutory purposes is committed to its discretion under §701(a)(2). Legislative-history references and the general federal trust relationship do not require continued funding of this particular program; enforceable statutory boundaries and the separately presented constitutional claim remain distinct.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the appropriations leave this choice to the Service. Chaney explains why a decision requiring the balancing of resources, priorities and expertise may lack a judicially manageable standard, and Locomotive Engineers confirms that the APA preserves established categories of discretionary administration. The relevant enactments authorize health services broadly but do not require continuation of this regional program. References in committee materials describe expectations without becoming an enacted funding restriction. Nor does the general trust relationship with Indian beneficiaries choose one eligible group’s program over services for others. Overton Park remains different because an actual statutory restriction can supply law for a court to enforce. The Service must therefore remain within Congress’s authorized purposes, but courts may not replace its allocation judgment on the grounds advanced here. Under Webster, the independent due-process claim requires separate consideration; discretion under the APA does not itself answer that constitutional question.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Heckler v. Chaney, 470 U.S.821: discretionary resource-allocation rationale applied beyond the enforcement setting within the statutory limits stated.
- ICC v. Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers, 482 U.S.270: recognized discretionary administrative categories applied.
- Citizens to Preserve Overton Park v. Volpe, 401 U.S.402: distinguished; an actual statutory standard remains enforceable.
- Webster v. Doe, 486 U.S.592: separate constitutional review preserved.

#### This organizational and policy change does not require notice and comment

**Holding and operative rule:** Even assuming the program’s termination is a rule, this reallocation and organizational policy change falls within §553(b)(A)’s exceptions for agency organization and general policy statements and does not require notice and comment. The ruling concerns a change in service availability, not a new binding eligibility rule, and leaves the separate §552 publication question unresolved.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the procedural requirement depends on what the agency action does. The Service redistributed resources and reorganized its delivery policy; it did not promulgate a new binding eligibility standard disqualifying persons who otherwise qualified for benefits. Section553(b)(A) excludes the organizational and general policy choices at issue from notice and comment. Ruiz does not establish that every reduction in a program serving Indians requires that procedure. It addresses an agency’s obligation to follow its own applicable requirements and cannot supply a universal rulemaking command absent from the statute. The Court does not rely on the separate benefits exception in §553(a)(2), because the Department has undertaken to follow notice-and-comment requirements despite that exception. That undertaking does not eliminate the relevant §553(b)(A) exclusions. Whether another publication duty under §552 applies was not decided below and remains outside this holding, as does the separate constitutional claim.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Morton v. Ruiz, 415 U.S.199: distinguished; compliance with applicable agency procedures does not create a universal notice-and-comment duty.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: the APA’s review presumption coexisted with §701(a)(2) and specified rulemaking exceptions. After: this unearmarked allocation falls within discretionary administration and this organizational policy change needs no notice and comment. Independent enacted limits, constitutional claims, the §552 issue and distinct remedial questions are not extinguished.

#### Keene Corp. v. United States — 1993-05-24

[Canonical Decision Record](records/keene_merits_1993-05-24.md). Affirmed, 8–1; Stevens dissents.

#### Section1500 jurisdiction is measured when the action is filed

**Holding and operative rule:** Section1500 bars the reviewed Claims Court action when, at filing, a suit for or in respect to the same claim is pending elsewhere against the United States or a covered federal actor; later dismissal of that other suit does not cure the original action’s jurisdictional defect. Claims Court decisions inconsistent with that filing-time requirement cannot support jurisdiction here; the Court does not decide the reversed order of filing.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that jurisdiction is tested when the action begins. Mollan supplies that ordinary rule, and Corona Coal applies the statutory prohibition to an existing claim in another forum. The words describing a pending suit identify the condition that must be measured at that point, not a defect the plaintiff can retroactively erase. The dissent’s election approach addresses the practical hardship of divided jurisdiction, but it changes the statutory limit into a discretionary or continuing case-management rule. Congress’s choice may force difficult decisions about forum and timing; courts cannot supply a relation-back cure because those decisions are difficult. Brown and Hossein cannot survive to the extent they permit the inconsistent cure, while cases about a later-filed outside action address a sequence not presented. The Court affirms jurisdictional dismissal without reaching the underlying asbestos compensation theories or predicting the timeliness of a future action.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Mollan v. Torrance, 9 Wheat.537: filing-time jurisdiction applied.
- Corona Coal Co. v. United States, 263 U.S.537: statutory pendency bar applied.
- Brown v. United States, 175 Ct.Cl.343, 358 F.2d1002, and Hossein v. United States, 218 Ct.Cl.727: disapproved insofar as inconsistent with filing-time jurisdiction.
- Tecon Engineers, Inc. v. United States, 170 Ct.Cl.389, 343 F.2d943: reversed filing sequence reserved, not overruled here.

#### Overlapping facts and relief cannot be separated merely by changing legal theories

**Holding and operative rule:** For the claims presented, §1500 applies when the suits arise from substantially the same operative facts and seek overlapping relief, even if they plead different legal theories or those theories cannot be brought in the same forum. The Court leaves open suits seeking wholly different relief and does not adopt the Federal Circuit’s broader disposition of those precedents.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute cannot be avoided by assigning different legal labels to the same operative events and overlapping request for money. British American Tobacco supplies the established factual comparison, and the words for or in respect to a claim reject an artificially narrow identity requirement. Matson and the statutory history confirm that Congress addressed parallel routes against the Government and its agents, routes that often could not have been combined in one tribunal. Keene’s proposed claim-splitting limitation would therefore disable the statute in the circumstances it was enacted to reach. The factual overlap found below is not displaced by the different theories of compensation. Casman and Boston Five involved materially different requests for relief and need not be resolved to decide these suits. The Court neither rules that all factual overlap is sufficient regardless of relief nor adjudicates the merits of the monetary claims.

**Precedent treatment:**

- British American Tobacco Co. v. United States, 89 Ct.Cl.438: operative-facts construction adopted for overlapping relief.
- Matson Navigation Co. v. United States, 284 U.S.352: historical statutory setting applied; different causes do not negate the bar.
- Casman v. United States, 135 Ct.Cl.647, and Boston Five Cents Savings Bank v. United States, 864 F.2d137: wholly different relief question reserved, not overruled here.

#### The dismissals do not depend on a new rule requiring prospective-only treatment

**Holding and operative rule:** Keene’s reviewed suits fail under established filing-time and factual-identity principles; the Federal Circuit’s broader repudiation of precedents unnecessary to this result does not make the controlling rule new as applied here. The Court therefore rejects the requested prospective-only treatment without deciding the general availability of pure prospectivity.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Keene cannot obtain jurisdiction by characterizing this result as the retroactive announcement of an unexpected rule. The actual suits were filed while other actions on the same operative facts and overlapping relief remained pending. Corona Coal and British American Tobacco already supplied the necessary principles. The Federal Circuit also addressed cases involving a reversed filing order or wholly distinct relief, but those broader questions do not decide Keene’s actions and the Court does not adopt their resolution. Brown and Hossein are inconsistent with the filing-time rule, yet they do not supply a settled rule entitling these suits to proceed. Chevron Oil’s proposed prospective-treatment inquiry therefore need not be resolved: the premise of a new controlling rule for this case is absent. The Court likewise declines to answer hypothetical tolling questions about another complaint that has not been presented.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chevron Oil Co. v. Huson, 404 U.S.97: prospective-only request rejected at its new-rule premise; general availability not decided.
- Corona Coal Co. v. United States, 263 U.S.537, and British American Tobacco Co. v. United States, 89 Ct.Cl.438: existing governing principles applied.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before and after: filing-time jurisdiction and operative-fact comparison control these overlapping claims. Brown and Hossein lose force insofar as inconsistent with that rule. The Court does not adopt the en banc court’s treatment of the Claims Court-first or wholly different-relief cases. No general retroactivity replacement, tolling rule or merits liability is entered.

#### Sullivan v. Louisiana — 1993-06-01

[Canonical Decision Record](records/sullivan_merits_1993-06-01.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0; the separate sentencing relief is undisturbed.

#### The sentencing remand does not prevent review of this completed conviction ruling

**Holding and operative rule:** Under §1257 and Cox’s surviving-federal-question category, the separate sentencing remand does not prevent review of the finally resolved federal challenge to Sullivan’s conviction, which will remain whatever lawful sentence follows. Review does not reach the correctness of the penalty-phase relief or create a general exception for interlocutory criminal rulings.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the unresolved sentencing proceedings do not remove the federal conviction question from review. Cox permits review when further proceedings cannot eliminate the already determined federal issue that survives them. Louisiana conclusively sustained guilt against this constitutional challenge, while granting relief for a distinct failure of counsel in the penalty phase. A new sentencing hearing cannot supply the missing constitutionally adequate jury determination of guilt and cannot reconsider the federal ruling sustaining the conviction. The two components must therefore remain separate both in assessing finality and in defining the Court’s mandate. This application does not turn every intermediate criminal ruling into a final judgment: the conviction question here is concluded below and remains irrespective of the remaining sentence proceedings. The Court accordingly reaches the harmless-error issue while leaving the defendant’s independently obtained sentencing relief intact.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cox Broadcasting Corp. v. Cohn, 420 U.S.469, 480–481: surviving-federal-question finality applied to the distinct conviction and sentencing components.

#### A charge vitiating the reasonable-doubt standard leaves no valid verdict for harmless review

**Holding and operative rule:** When an unconstitutional reasonable-doubt charge vitiates the jury’s governing standard for all factual findings, the defendant has received no constitutionally valid jury verdict of guilt beyond a reasonable doubt. That structural defect requires reversal without harmless-error assessment; an appellate court’s hypothetical verdict cannot substitute for the required jury determination, and this rule does not classify every instructional mistake as structural.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the required finding belongs to a properly instructed jury. Winship supplies the beyond-a-reasonable-doubt standard, and Duncan assigns the criminal verdict to the jury. Chapman asks whether a constitutional error contributed to the verdict actually rendered; it does not authorize a reviewing court to make the missing verdict itself. Here the conceded Cage defect reaches the standard governing all findings, so no valid jury determination remains against which to measure the error. Rose involved a separable erroneous presumption and therefore does not establish harmlessness where the entire adjudicative foundation is missing. Fulminante identifies defects of that structure as outside ordinary harmless-error analysis. The strength of the evidence cannot transfer the jury’s constitutional responsibility to appellate judges. The Court reserves different instructional omissions and inaccuracies, accepts the conceded defect rather than reopening it under Estelle, and leaves Brecht’s separate preserved-Doyle habeas rule unchanged.

**Precedent treatment:**

- In re Winship, 397 U.S.358: proof-beyond-reasonable-doubt requirement applied.
- Duncan v. Louisiana, 391 U.S.145: jury’s constitutional role applied.
- Cage v. Louisiana, 498 U.S.39: conceded defect taken as the predicate, not relitigated.
- Chapman v. California, 386 U.S.18: actual-verdict inquiry preserved; no substitute hypothetical verdict permitted.
- Rose v. Clark, 478 U.S.570: separable presumption error distinguished.
- Arizona v. Fulminante, 499 U.S.279: structural-defect category applied.
- Estelle v. McGuire, December4,1991: contested-charge framework preserved; no reopening of the conceded defect.
- Brecht v. Abrahamson, April21,1993: preserved-Doyle habeas rule unchanged; not a Kotteakos constitutional-habeas rule.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: jury, burden-of-proof and harmless-error principles existed, while classification of this defect was open. After: the conceded vitiation of the jury’s reasonable-doubt standard is structural and cannot be cured by hypothesizing a verdict. Practical finality is applied to this particular completed conviction ruling. Other instructional errors, guilt sufficiency and collateral-review gates remain outside the holding.

#### Smith v. United States — 1993-06-01

[Canonical Decision Record](records/smith_merits_1993-06-01.md). Affirmed, 5–4.

#### Offering a firearm in exchange for drugs is use within the charged statute

**Holding and operative rule:** Offering a firearm as consideration in a drug exchange constitutes using the firearm under the applicable §924(c)(1), even though the firearm is employed as merchandise rather than fired, brandished or otherwise employed as a weapon. The separate during-and-in-relation-to requirement must also be met; the statute is not satisfied by possession alone under this holding.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a firearm is used when it is deliberately employed as the consideration offered to obtain drugs. Perrin directs attention to the ordinary meaning of the enacted word, and Timbers requires the surrounding statute to inform that reading. Use is not limited to the object’s most familiar purpose, and §924(d)’s treatment of firearm transactions confirms that Congress can describe employment in exchange as firearm use. That contextual confirmation does not import the forfeiture provision’s distinct elements or burden of proof. The dissent’s weapon-only reading would add a limitation absent from this criminal provision. The separate relation-to requirement supplies an additional limit rather than a reason to narrow use to discharge or threats. Bass and Moskal preserve lenity after genuine unresolved ambiguity; the Court finds none after these ordinary tools. This ruling concerns the offered exchange proved here, not uncharged carrying, mere possession or later statutory amendments.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Perrin v. United States, 444 U.S.37: ordinary meaning applied.
- United Savings Association v. Timbers of Inwood Forest, 484 U.S.365: whole-statute context applied.
- United States v. Bass, 404 U.S.336, and Moskal v. United States, 498 U.S.103: residual lenity preserved; no remaining ambiguity found.
- Deal v. United States, May17,1993: conviction sequence remains distinct; it does not decide use.

#### The offered firearm has the required connection to the trafficking crime

**Holding and operative rule:** The firearm must have a purpose or effect with respect to the trafficking crime, rather than being present by accident or coincidence; at minimum, it must facilitate or have the potential to facilitate that crime. Offering the firearm as the consideration for the charged drug transaction establishes that relationship here without deciding that facilitation alone is sufficient in every other setting.

**Authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the gun’s role in this transaction was direct. It was the item offered to procure the cocaine, not an object coincidentally present while a separate offense occurred. The phrase in relation to prevents an unrelated firearm use from triggering the mandatory penalty, and the transaction here meets that limit because the proposed exchange itself advances the charged trafficking. The statutory inquiry remains separate from whether the conduct counts as use; neither requirement may swallow the other. Stewart and Long describe the facilitative relationship as a useful minimum, but the Court need not decide its outer boundary when the firearm supplies the proposed payment. Nor does the officer’s failure to complete the barter defeat the nexus to the conspiracy and attempt actually charged. Blackmun’s concurrence confirms that no universal facilitation-only sufficiency rule is adopted. The decision does not sustain the conviction through carrying, which the indictment did not charge.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Stewart, 779 F.2d538: facilitation formulation used as a minimum connection, not a universal sufficiency rule.
- United States v. Long, 905 F.2d1572: facilitative relationship formulation applied within the proved exchange setting.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: this Court had not settled whether merchandise barter is firearm use. After: five Justices hold that the offered exchange is use and has the required trafficking relationship. The exact outer boundary of sufficient facilitation remains open. No carrying, mere-possession, later-amendment or constitutional-sentencing rule is entered.

#### Mertens v. Hewitt Associates — 1993-06-01

[Canonical Decision Record](records/mertens_merits_1993-06-01.md). Affirmed, 5–4, on the reviewed monetary-redress claim.

#### The reviewed compensatory award is not appropriate equitable relief

**Holding and operative rule:** Section502(a)(3)’s appropriate equitable relief encompasses traditionally equitable categories of relief, not compensatory damages merely because a court of equity could award them in trust litigation. The requested monetary compensation from this admitted nonfiduciary for the plan’s losses is therefore unavailable under that provision; the Court does not bar every monetary equitable remedy or decide the antecedent, expressly disclaimed cause-of-action question.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; Blackmun, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the enacted remedy limitation must retain independent force. Curtis and Terry distinguish compensation for loss, ordinarily legal relief, from traditionally equitable categories. Reading equitable relief to include everything an equity court could award in a trust controversy would deprive that limitation of its work and disregard ERISA’s use of different remedial language elsewhere. Firestone directs consultation of trust law where it fits the statute; it does not authorize replacing the statute’s express limit with the full remedial jurisdiction of historical equity. Clews establishes that trust courts could award money, but that fact does not determine the category Congress selected here. Russell reinforces respect for ERISA’s differentiated enforcement structure without itself settling this question. The participants seek compensation rather than a preserved restitution remedy. The Court rejects that claim while leaving the disclaimed antecedent action question, unreviewed remedies and the separate malpractice disposition untouched; no allegations become findings of liability.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Curtis v. Loether, 415 U.S.189: traditional legal compensation distinction applied.
- Chauffeurs, Teamsters & Helpers v. Terry, 494 U.S.558: remedy classification applied; trust forum alone does not decide the statutory category.
- Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. v. Bruch, 489 U.S.101: trust-law guidance preserved subject to express statutory limits.
- Clews v. Jamieson, 182 U.S.461: equity’s monetary powers acknowledged but distinguished from the statutory category.
- Massachusetts Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Russell, 473 U.S.134: enforcement-structure caution applied; its reserved question is now resolved only within this holding.
- United States v. Burke, May26,1992: its fractured judgment supplies no controlling general remedial-range test.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, February26,1992: appropriate-remedy presumption distinguished because Congress expressly limited this provision to equitable relief.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: the availability of this compensatory nonfiduciary remedy remained open. After: five Justices reject it under §502(a)(3)’s equitable-relief limit. The Court does not decide whether a cause against the nonfiduciary otherwise exists, does not prohibit every equitable monetary remedy and does not reopen restitution. Stone’s bounded statutory-coverage and restoration proposals remain noncontrolling. Burke’s actual fractured authority and Franklin’s cause/remedy distinction remain unchanged.

#### Musick, Peeler & Garrett v. Employers Insurance of Wausau — 1993-06-01

[Canonical Decision Record](records/musick_merits_1993-06-01.md). Affirmed, 6–3.

#### Contribution is available among persons jointly liable under the received Rule10b-5 action

**Holding and operative rule:** Persons jointly liable for the same violation under the established implied Rule10b-5 action may seek contribution, subject to proof of their shared underlying liability and the applicable allocation requirements. The rule is a bounded incident of that received action and does not create general professional liability, an automatic aiding-and-abetting rule, indemnity, or contribution under unrelated statutes.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that defining this incident of the established implied remedy calls for the same confined method used to define its other boundaries. Lampf looks to closely analogous express securities provisions, and §§9(e) and18(b) provide contribution among persons answerable for comparable misconduct. Blue Chip Stamps and Ernst & Ernst likewise show that the received action must have judicially identified limits rather than an undefined remedial scope. Northwest Airlines and Texas Industries concern attempts to add contribution to different congressionally supplied schemes; they do not settle every incident of this already judicially developed action. The dissent’s new-action objection has force, but the Court concludes that allocation among persons already liable is part of defining that existing liability, not a new substantive prohibition. The insurers must still establish the defendants’ own shared liability and a permissible allocation. Neither participation in a professional engagement nor payment of a settlement proves those predicates.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Lampf, Pleva, Lipkind, Prupis & Petigrow v. Gilbertson, 501 U.S.350: close express securities analogies applied to define an incident of the received action.
- Blue Chip Stamps v. Manor Drug Stores, 421 U.S.723: bounded judicial definition of the established action applied.
- Ernst & Ernst v. Hochfelder, 425 U.S.185: limits on implied securities liability preserved.
- Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. Transport Workers Union, 451 U.S.77: different express remedial scheme distinguished; no universal contribution presumption announced.
- Texas Industries, Inc. v. Radcliff Materials, Inc., 451 U.S.630: different statutory scheme distinguished.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, February26,1992: existing-action/remedy distinction preserved; no freestanding professional cause created.

**Current-law transition and limits:** Before: contribution within the received Rule10b-5 action was unsettled in this Court. After: six Justices recognize it through closely analogous securities remedies, subject to actual shared liability and allocation. No new substantive violation or universal federal contribution rule follows; the dissent’s authorization objection remains noncontrolling.

#### FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc. — 1993-06-01

[Controlling record](records/beach_merits_1993-06-01.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0 in the judgment.

#### Reenactment does not moot the live constitutional controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** Congress’s reenactment of the cable definition without changing the challenged distinction does not adopt a lower court’s constitutional invalidation or eliminate the live controversy defended by the FCC. The Court therefore reaches the reviewed equal-protection question.

**Authority:** Thomas’s threshold opinion; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the parties continue to dispute the validity of an operative statutory distinction. Congress’s failure to alter that distinction while revising other portions of the cable legislation does not turn the court of appeals’ judgment into an act of Congress. A legislative adoption inference sometimes used to understand statutory language does not establish that Congress enacted a judicial conclusion about constitutional validity. The FCC continues to defend the distinction, and reversal would alter the parties’ rights under the exemption. The amendment therefore leaves a concrete controversy rather than an advisory question. This conclusion decides only the proposed ground for mootness. It does not treat congressional silence as approval of every agency interpretation, decide the new legislation’s constitutionality, or resolve the separate speech objections. Those objections must be addressed through the questions and record actually presented for their consideration.

**Precedent treatment:**

- The lower court’s constitutional judgment is reviewed, not treated as legislation adopted by congressional silence; no earlier controlling decision is overruled.

#### Common-owner bargaining and regulatory costs sustain the economic line

**Holding and operative rule:** For an economic classification involving neither a suspect class nor a fundamental right under the question reviewed, a reasonably conceivable legitimate basis suffices; the challenger must negate such a basis, and formal legislative findings are unnecessary. Congress could rationally distinguish these commonly owned, controlled or managed dwellings from separately owned properties because bargaining arrangements and the relationship between regulatory cost and consumer protection differ; Moreno and Cleburne continue to forbid irrational or impermissibly motivated classifications.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Parts I and II-A; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress could make this ownership distinction without proving that it was the only satisfactory line. Fritz permits economic classifications supported by a conceivable legitimate relationship, and the entered Nordlinger decision confirms that imperfect fit does not itself defeat rationality. A common owner can bargain for service on behalf of the property and can choose among suppliers before committing the premises. Congress could also conclude that regulating small systems imposes costs disproportionate to the consumer protection obtained. Separate ownership changes those bargaining conditions and permits expansion without the same common control. The absence of a fixed subscriber cap and the existence of atypically large exempt facilities do not negate the general relationship. Neither legislative silence about an actual motive nor the absence of empirical findings shifts the challenger’s burden. Moreno and Cleburne still exclude a forbidden purpose or irrational prejudice; no such defect or settled contrary fact defeats this relationship here. Separate speech scrutiny remains undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States Railroad Retirement Board v. Fritz, 449 U.S.166: conceivable legitimate grounds and imperfect economic fit applied.
- Sullivan v. Stroop, 496 U.S.478: ordinary review of social and economic classifications applied.
- Nordlinger v. Hahn, June 18, 1992: the entered rational-basis holding applied without converting its particular tax grounds into universal immunity from review.
- Department of Agriculture v. Moreno, 413 U.S.528: illegitimate-purpose limit preserved.
- City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, 473 U.S.432: irrational-prejudice limit preserved.

#### Competitive entry independently supports the ownership distinction

**Holding and operative rule:** Congress could independently distinguish separately owned buildings because an incumbent provider can add nearby buildings at a lower marginal cost than a new entrant can incur to install its own reception facilities, creating a plausible first-mover advantage. This conceivable competition concern is sufficient under ordinary rational-basis review without proof that every regulated system possesses monopoly power.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Part II-B; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court also holds that the ownership line has a sufficient competition rationale independent of the regulatory-cost explanation. A provider already operating a reception facility may connect additional separately owned buildings more cheaply than a new competitor can construct the facilities necessary to serve them. Congress could regard that advantage as a reason to retain franchising oversight. In a commonly owned development, by contrast, a single owner can negotiate for the whole property before selecting a provider. Fritz permits Congress to address such differences through an administrable category even when particular systems do not display the predicted advantage. The argument that all systems avoid public rights-of-way identifies a common characteristic but does not eliminate this different competitive characteristic. The holding does not find actual monopoly power, decide an antitrust claim or require a regulator to prove market dominance in every application. Nor does it decide whether a different standard applies to a separately presented burden on protected speech.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Fritz, 449 U.S.166: a second conceivable legitimate economic basis independently sustains the line.
- Nordlinger, June 18, 1992: multiple sufficient rational grounds and tolerance for imperfect fit applied within ordinary review.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: ordinary rational-basis review and Nordlinger governed economic classifications, while the cable ownership line was contested. After: the line is sustained by eight Justices on bargaining/regulatory cost and seven on the independently sufficient competition rationale. Seven supply the threshold mootness rationale. No First Amendment holding or heightened-scrutiny resolution enters; no precedent is overruled.

**Operative relief:** The judgment invalidating the ownership distinction is reversed and the case remanded. No exemption is judicially extended to all private-property systems. The Court leaves separate speech claims, any properly presented heightened-scrutiny issue and challenges to particular applications for appropriate proceedings; it does not decide those claims or authorize public-right-of-way use under the exemption.

#### Nobelman v. American Savings Bank — 1993-06-01

[Controlling record](records/nobelman_merits_1993-06-01.md). Affirmed, 9–0.

#### Valuation does not remove the protected rights of this mortgage holder

**Holding and operative rule:** When a creditor retains a secured portion of an allowed claim secured only by a security interest in real property that is the debtor’s principal residence, §1322(b)(2) protects the holder’s rights in the mortgage claim as a whole, notwithstanding §506(a) valuation and bifurcation. The debtor may not modify the deficiency portion in a way that alters those protected repayment and enforcement rights; independent statutory restrictions and the express cure authority remain effective.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that valuation and protection of the mortgage holder’s rights answer different questions. Section 506(a) permits valuation of the bank’s collateral and confirms that the claim retains a secured portion. Butner supplies the state-law rights attached to the mortgage: repayment on the agreed terms, retention of the lien, acceleration, foreclosure and recovery of a permissible deficiency. Section 1322(b)(2) protects the holder of the described claim, rather than merely the dollars assigned secured status by valuation. Reducing the unsecured balance would change the repayment rights in the same note and thus modify the protected rights. The debtors’ last-antecedent reading is grammatically possible, but the full phrase and the focus on the holder’s rights supply the better reading. Ron Pair requires attention to that enacted text, not a transfer of Dewsnup’s different Chapter 7 rule. Section 362’s stay and §1322(b)(5)’s cure authority operate independently. Wholly unsecured liens and disputed additional collateral are not decided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Butner v. United States, 440 U.S.48: state law identifies the property and contract rights to which federal protection attaches.
- United States v. Ron Pair Enterprises, Inc., 489 U.S.235: the text of each Bankruptcy Code provision receives independent effect.
- Dewsnup v. Timm, January 15, 1992: Chapter 7 §506(d) rule distinguished; §506(a) valuation is not displaced.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: Dewsnup resolved a Chapter 7 lien question without deciding Chapter 13. After: §506(a) still values the claim, but §1322(b)(2) protects this holder’s rights in the partially secured principal-residence mortgage. Section 1322(b)(5) permits curing a default within a reasonable time and maintaining payments while the case is pending on a claim whose last payment comes due after the final plan payment; it is not a general permission to rewrite the protected obligation. The automatic stay remains independent. The Court does not classify the deed’s ancillary provisions, decide wholly unsecured liens or adopt later amendments.

**Operative relief:** The Fifth Circuit’s judgment is affirmed. This plan cannot strip the protected mortgage rights by treating the deficiency as a freely modifiable unsecured claim. The ruling does not prevent a new proposal complying with the statute, including the distinct express cure provisions, and orders no foreclosure or distribution.

#### Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/lambs_chapel_merits_1993-06-07.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0 in the judgment.

#### A religious perspective cannot be excluded from an admitted subject

**Holding and operative rule:** When a public school makes an after-hours forum available for private discussion of family life and child-rearing, it may not reject that otherwise eligible discussion solely because the viewpoint is religious. This prohibition applies even assuming a nonpublic forum; it does not require an unlimited public forum or invalidate neutral restrictions on subjects and activities outside the actual forum.

**Authority:** White’s speech portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the district’s stated reason for denial is viewpoint discrimination. Perry and Cornelius permit a government to define a limited forum but require neutrality among perspectives on a subject it admits. Family life and child-rearing were eligible topics, and the films addressed those subjects from a religious perspective. Describing religion itself as an excluded subject cannot change that relationship. Nor does exclusion of every religious denomination cure discrimination between religious and nonreligious views. The record does not establish that the application was denied for anticipated disorder or some independent neutral eligibility rule. The district’s later alternatives therefore cannot sustain the stated denial. The Court need not decide whether all of the property is a public forum or whether every religious activity must be permitted there. The earlier Sunday-service application is outside this case. On remand, genuinely neutral scheduling, safety and subject-matter rules remain available, but the same viewpoint distinction does not.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Perry Education Association v. Perry Local Educators’ Association, 460 U.S.37: viewpoint neutrality within a nonpublic forum applied.
- Cornelius v. NAACP Legal Defense & Educational Fund, 473 U.S.788: permissible forum limits distinguished from forbidden viewpoint exclusion.
- Widmar v. Vincent, 454 U.S.263: religious perspective does not by itself remove otherwise eligible private speech from equal-access protection.

#### Equal private access does not establish religion here

**Holding and operative rule:** Allowing this voluntary, publicly accessible, after-hours film presentation without school sponsorship does not violate the Establishment Clause. Equal access serves a secular forum purpose and neither primarily advances religion as governmental activity nor creates excessive entanglement; this holding authorizes access to private speech, not an official devotional program.

**Authority:** White’s establishment portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the district cannot justify viewpoint discrimination by invoking an establishment problem that equal access does not create. Widmar explains why a government’s neutral provision of space does not make every private speaker’s message its own. The proposed films would be shown after school, attendance would be voluntary, and the meetings would be open to the public rather than presented as school instruction. Under Lemon, the forum serves a secular community-use purpose; allowing a religious perspective on an admitted subject does not produce governmental advancement of religion or excessive entanglement. The entered Lee decision addresses official graduation worship and religious pressure within that school event, conditions absent here. The contrary argument confuses public ownership of the room with school sponsorship of the message. Nothing in this decision orders religious instruction, public financing of devotion or compulsory participation. It also leaves outside review the separate denial of Sunday worship services and other activities outside the forum’s lawful scope.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Widmar, 454 U.S.263: neutral access and private attribution applied.
- Lemon v. Kurtzman, 403 U.S.602: purpose, primary effect and entanglement applied, not replaced.
- Lee v. Weisman, June 24, 1992: the entered official-worship and pressure holdings distinguished and preserved.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: forum viewpoint neutrality, Widmar and the inherited establishment framework governed. After: religious family-life discussion cannot be excluded as a viewpoint within this admitted subject, and equal access here creates no establishment. Seven join the speech portion, six the Lemon-based application. Separate writings supply no replacement establishment test; neither the whole religious-use rule nor every possible worship restriction is facially adjudicated.

**Operative relief:** The Second Circuit judgment is reversed and the case remanded for relief against the viewpoint exclusion. Lower courts must secure nondiscriminatory treatment within the lawful after-hours forum and provide declaratory or injunctive relief as necessary. The school may retain genuinely neutral rules for comparable applicants. No damages award, public financing of devotional instruction or admission of the unchallenged Sunday-service application is ordered.

#### Minnesota v. Dickerson — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/dickerson_merits_1993-06-07.md). Affirmed, 7–2 on suppression; 9–0 on the live controversy and lawful tactile-recognition principle.

#### The legally consequential diversion record keeps the case live

**Holding and operative rule:** Completion and dismissal under Minnesota’s §152.18 diversion procedure do not moot this review where reversal could restore a retained record legally usable in later proceedings. The conclusion rests on those identified statutory consequences, not on an invented conviction or a universal presumption about every dismissed charge.

**Authority:** White’s Part I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that completion of probation does not eliminate the legal stake in this suppression judgment. Minnesota’s procedure withholds a judgment of conviction and dismisses the charge after successful diversion, so the Court does not rely on the ordinary consequences of a conviction that never occurred. The statute nevertheless retains a nonpublic record. Goodrich identifies its potential use in Minnesota proceedings, and Frank identifies its relevance to federal criminal-history treatment. Reversal could therefore alter a legally operative record rather than merely declare that a past search was proper. Sibron’s concern with continuing legal consequences supports adjudication on this concrete basis. Dickerson’s contrary argument correctly emphasizes dismissal, but overlooks the consequences the statute preserves despite dismissal. The Court does not decide that every expired diversion arrangement or dismissed prosecution necessarily remains live. The identified uses of this retained record, and the effect the requested judgment could have on them, are sufficient here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Sibron v. New York, 392 U.S.40: continuing legal consequences support a live controversy; the actual diversion scheme supplies those consequences.
- State v. Goodrich, 256 N.W.2d 506 (Minn.1977): retained diversion record’s state-law consequences applied.
- United States v. Frank, 932 F.2d 700 (8th Cir.1991): identified federal criminal-history use of the retained disposition considered.

#### Authorized touch can supply probable cause without an additional search

**Holding and operative rule:** An officer lawfully conducting a protective patdown may seize contraband when the authorized touching itself supplies probable cause of its incriminating character and the officer has lawful access to it. The officer may not manipulate or otherwise explore a nonthreatening object beyond the frisk to obtain that probable cause; immediate recognition requires probable cause, not certainty.

**Authority:** White’s Part II; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Constitution does not make sight categorically superior to touch when the original contact is lawful. Horton allows seizure of an immediately incriminating object encountered with lawful access, and Long permits seizure of contraband actually discovered within a legitimate protective search. A tactile perception satisfying the same conditions creates no additional search merely because it comes through a different sense. Terry nevertheless limits the patdown to its protective purpose. Hicks prevents an officer from undertaking a further search to establish the probable cause needed to justify the seizure. The State therefore cannot rely on an evidentiary manipulation to create its own authority. The contrary categorical rule overlooks cases in which the authorized contact alone supplies probable cause. Conversely, an officer’s bare assertion of immediate recognition does not erase the need for a supported account of the authorized contact. The holding does not license exploratory reaching into clothing or continuing a completed frisk on unparticularized suspicion.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Terry v. Ohio, 392 U.S.1: the protective purpose limits the scope of the frisk.
- Michigan v. Long, 463 U.S.1032: seizure of contraband found within an authorized protective search applied.
- Horton v. California, 496 U.S.128: lawful access and immediately apparent incriminating character extended to lawful touch.
- Arizona v. Hicks, 480 U.S.321: probable cause may not be manufactured through a separate unauthorized search.
- Soldal v. Cook County, December 8, 1992: independent possessory protection preserved; no enlargement of a frisk follows.

#### Manipulation after excluding a weapon requires suppression here

**Holding and operative rule:** When the accepted findings establish that the officer identified contraband only by squeezing and manipulating an object after determining that it was not a weapon, the examination exceeds Terry and cannot justify the seizure. That independent ground sustains suppression here despite the state court’s erroneous categorical rejection of tactile recognition.

**Authority:** White’s Parts III and IV; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the state court’s supported account resolves the legality of this seizure. The officer’s protective examination had established that the lump was not a weapon before further manipulation disclosed its identity as cocaine. Terry did not authorize that further investigation, and Hicks does not allow the information gained through it to justify the intrusion retrospectively. Rejecting the state court’s categorical view of touch therefore does not require reversal of its judgment. The remand position points to imprecision in the trial findings and asks whether the state court’s legal error influenced its reconstruction. The majority nevertheless accepts the state court’s supported finding of the additional examination, which independently answers the correct constitutional question. It does not demand a reconstruction of every passing subjective thought or reject the officer’s sensory competence in general. On this record the authorized search ended before the necessary identification occurred, and the cocaine remains suppressed without another evidentiary opportunity for the State.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Terry, 392 U.S.1: protective authority ended when the object was known not to be a weapon.
- Hicks, 480 U.S.321: information obtained by the additional exploration cannot validate it retrospectively.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: plain-view and protective-search doctrine supplied separate scope and seizure conditions. After: all nine apply those conditions to lawful touch and find a live controversy; seven affirm suppression because an additional evidentiary manipulation exceeded the frisk. No new warrant exception for exploratory touching and no general dismissed-charge mootness rule enters.

**Operative relief:** The Minnesota Supreme Court’s suppression judgment is affirmed. Its categorical objection to tactile recognition is rejected, but its independent finding of manipulation beyond the protective frisk sustains suppression. No remand or new opportunity to reconstruct the State’s proof is ordered. The completed diversion is described accurately, without entering or reinstating a criminal conviction.

#### Gilmore v. Taylor — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/gilmore_merits_1993-06-07.md). Reversed, 7–2.

#### Falconer announces a new rule for this collateral proceeding

**Holding and operative rule:** Falconer’s specific requirement governing the interaction of these murder and voluntary-manslaughter instructions was not dictated by precedent at Taylor’s finality and is therefore a new rule under Teague. The State’s concession of error under Falconer does not waive its preserved retroactivity objection, and this conclusion does not approve the charge on direct review or exclude all defense-instruction errors from due process.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a general protection against an unfair charge did not dictate Falconer’s particular result at finality. Cupp requires consideration of the whole instruction and trial, but does not settle every relationship between an offense instruction and a state affirmative defense. Patterson and Martin show that the constitutional burden on offense elements does not automatically govern every fact relevant to an affirmative defense. The strongest contrary argument is that these instructions permitted jurors to convict without considering the only meaningful route to manslaughter. That concern may support a constitutional rule; it does not establish that existing precedent compelled this specific rule. The entered Estelle decision preserves reasonable-likelihood review without supplying a finality-era Falconer holding. Graham’s capital-mitigation result rested on its own earlier capital authorities and does not make this noncapital sequencing rule old. Connecticut v. Johnson’s plurality concerns presumptions affecting elemental proof, not this defense sequence. Boyde’s later capital-instruction analysis does not establish that Falconer was dictated at 1987 finality. Judicial disagreement alone is not the test. The State preserved Teague despite conceding Falconer error, and the Court resolves only that threshold.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Teague v. Lane, 489 U.S.288: finality-era dictated-by-precedent inquiry applied.
- Cupp v. Naughten, 414 U.S.141: whole-charge fairness preserved but not treated as dictating this specific rule.
- Connecticut v. Johnson, 460 U.S.73: plurality discussion of elemental presumptions distinguished; it is not treated as a controlling holding compelling Falconer.
- Boyde v. California, 494 U.S.370: later capital-instruction reasonable-likelihood analysis distinguished; its date does not itself supply a 1987 finality-era source.
- Patterson v. New York, 432 U.S.197, and Martin v. Ohio, 480 U.S.228: offense-element burdens distinguished from affirmative-defense allocations.
- Estelle v. McGuire, December 4, 1991: the entered whole-charge reasonable-likelihood holding preserved; it neither decides Falconer nor supplies a source existing at 1987 finality.
- Graham v. Collins, January 25, 1993: the entered capital-mitigation holding distinguished on its earlier capital authorities.

#### Neither then-existing Teague exception permits this new instructional rule

**Holding and operative rule:** The Falconer rule regulates trial procedure rather than removing conduct or a class of persons from the State’s substantive power to punish, and it is not a watershed procedural rule essential to the fundamental accuracy and fairness of the criminal process. Neither exception recognized by Teague at this event’s date permits its retroactive use to obtain the reviewed habeas relief.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the character of the new rule prevents reliance on either Teague exception. The rule changes how the jury considers a defense within the trial; it does not make Taylor’s conduct constitutionally immune from punishment or exclude a class of persons from the penalty at issue. The substantive exception therefore does not apply. The watershed exception remains part of the governing law, but it is reserved for exceptional procedural changes fundamental to the accuracy and fairness of the criminal process. The possibility of a serious instructional error is not itself enough to place every new instruction rule within that category. The dissent emphasizes that jurors may have missed the only basis for a lesser offense. The majority does not dismiss that concern; it concludes that this specific sequencing rule does not reconstruct the process at the exceptional level Teague requires. No harmless-error ruling is necessary after the retroactivity bar, and the Court does not import any later elimination or narrowing of the exception.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Teague, 489 U.S.288: both the substantive and watershed exceptions applied in their event-date form.
- Sawyer v. Smith, 497 U.S.227: the demanding watershed category applied; importance of a procedural protection alone is insufficient.
- Brecht v. Abrahamson, April 21, 1993: the entered preserved-Doyle Chapman holding remains distinct; harmlessness is not reached here.
- Sullivan v. Louisiana, June 1, 1993: the absence of a valid reasonable-doubt verdict is a different instructional defect and does not bypass Teague in this case.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: Teague’s exceptions remained available, Estelle supplied whole-charge review and Graham required effective capital mitigation on its specific earlier authorities. After: seven Justices bar this Falconer claim as new and outside both exceptions. Neither the charge’s ultimate constitutional validity on direct review nor harmlessness is decided; the dissent’s watershed application is noncontrolling. No categorical due-process immunity for affirmative-defense instructions is adopted.

**Operative relief:** The Seventh Circuit judgment granting conditional habeas relief is reversed. Its 120-day retrial condition does not remain operative. This Court does not order a new trial, approve the instructions for direct review, decide harmless error or adjudicate the separate home-invasion sentence. No express implementing remand or special mandate date is added to the reversal.

#### Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/good_samaritan_merits_1993-06-07.md). Affirmed, 6–3.

#### Corrective adjustments operate within the authorized cost-method system

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1395x(v)(1)(A)(ii) requires suitable retroactive corrective adjustments when, for a provider and fiscal period, aggregate reimbursement produced by the methods proves inadequate or excessive; the Secretary permissibly construes that command as reconciliation of interim reimbursement to the amount payable under lawful reasonable-cost methods and limits. The clause does not require an individualized exception whenever actual costs exceed a ceiling, and authorized exceptions and independent challenges to unlawful methods remain intact.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the corrective-adjustment clause must operate within the reimbursement system Congress authorized. The statute permits cost methods and limits based on generalizations, while other provisions contemplate interim payments later reconciled to the amount lawfully due. Reading clause (ii) as an unconditional command to reimburse every provider’s actual excess costs would largely undo those limits and the specific exceptions accompanying them. The competing reading has textual force because the clause refers to methods and aggregate reimbursement rather than merely interim payments. That force establishes a real interpretive question; it does not make the hospitals’ construction compulsory. Chevron permits the Secretary’s reasonable resolution of that ambiguity, supported by the statutory arrangement and contemporaneous regulations. Georgetown’s rejection of unauthorized retroactive rulemaking does not independently decide this construction. The hospitals concede that no regulatory exemption or exception applies, and their challenges to the methods themselves are outside this review. Nothing here validates an independently unlawful limit or abolishes existing exceptions.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S.837: traditional tools followed by permissible construction of remaining ambiguity applied.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S.204: unauthorized retroactive rulemaking distinguished; its holding does not decide this clause’s meaning.
- Connecticut National Bank v. Germain, March 9, 1992: entered text/coexistence principle preserved; the dispute concerns what these provisions require, not implied repeal.

#### A changed agency interpretation is not automatically disqualified

**Holding and operative rule:** An agency’s change of statutory interpretation may reduce the weight accorded its view, but change alone does not make a considered, otherwise permissible construction unlawful. The Court must examine the text, delegated setting and reasons; an unsupported litigation position cannot substitute for the legally required agency explanation or obtain automatic controlling weight.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that consistency matters without making an agency’s first view irrevocable. FEC v. Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee recognizes that a reasonable agency construction may receive deference within its delegated field. Cardoza-Fonseca confirms that statutory meaning and the limits of the delegation remain judicial questions. Here the Secretary’s history includes changes in explanation, and the Court considers those changes rather than pretending the position has been uniform. The current interpretation nevertheless accords with the authorized cost-method system and has support in the contemporaneous regulatory arrangement. Georgetown denies controlling weight to unsupported litigation rationalizations and forbids unauthorized retroactive rules; neither proposition requires rejection merely because the agency has changed its view. The hospitals’ contrary argument would convert reduced persuasive weight into automatic invalidity. The Court rejects that conversion, not the requirement of reasoned agency action. No general deference regime replaces Chevron, and neither a defective explanation nor an independently unlawful method is insulated by this decision.

**Precedent treatment:**

- FEC v. Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee, 454 U.S.27: reasonable agency interpretation within the statutory delegation applied.
- INS v. Cardoza-Fonseca, 480 U.S.421: judicial interpretation and the significance of inconsistent agency positions preserved.
- Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S.204: unsupported litigation rationalizations receive no automatic controlling weight; change itself is a different issue.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: Chevron, the reasonable-cost provisions and Georgetown supplied distinct interpretation and retroactivity rules. After: the Secretary may use clause (ii) for reconciliation within valid methods, without a universal actual-cost escape from ceilings. An interpretive change affects weight but is not automatic invalidity. Neither specific exceptions nor independent method-validity review is eliminated. The dissent’s command to correct inadequate final methods is not controlling.

**Operative relief:** The Eighth Circuit judgment is affirmed on the statutory interpretation stated here. No new above-limit hearing or reimbursement award is ordered. Existing regulatory exceptions remain legally available to qualifying providers, but these petitioners conceded nonqualification. Their abandoned challenges to the methods are not remanded as live claims. The Court does not validate an independently unlawful ceiling or foreclose an independently proper future challenge.

#### Antoine v. Byers & Anderson, Inc. — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/antoine_merits_1993-06-07.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0.

#### The ministerial transcript function does not carry absolute judicial immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** Court reporters performing the statutory task of accurately recording or producing a verbatim transcript do not receive absolute judicial immunity merely because their work is important or integral to a judicial proceeding. A claimant must establish the historically and functionally justified immunity; this ministerial task does not involve the discretionary adjudication that supports judicial immunity.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that immunity follows the protected function rather than proximity to the judge. Butz requires the claimant to justify absolute protection, and Forrester distinguishes adjudicative decisions from administrative functions even when a judge performs them. Section 753 requires accurate verbatim preservation of the proceedings; it does not authorize a reporter to decide what the legal record ought to contain. The proposed analogy to common-law judges taking notes fails because those judges exercised discretion in deciding what to record for their own adjudication. A modern reporter’s skill and institutional importance do not transform the assigned task into judicial decisionmaking. Burns likewise requires actual historical and functional support rather than a general fear of litigation. The respondents have not established that support here. The Court removes only absolute judicial immunity. It does not determine whether an authorized federal damages action exists, whether Antoine suffered an actionable deprivation, or whether causation, other defenses or damages can be proved.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Forrester v. White, 484 U.S.219: functional distinction between adjudication and administration applied to reporting.
- Butz v. Economou, 438 U.S.478: burden of justifying absolute immunity and functional comparison applied.
- Burns v. Reed, 500 U.S.478: historical and functional justification required; importance alone is insufficient.
- Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S.409: immunity for adjudicative functions distinguished from the ministerial transcript task.
- Wyatt v. Cole, May 18, 1992: entered private-party qualified-immunity holding preserved without extension to the defense decided here.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: historical and functional immunity rules governed, but the lower courts extended absolute protection to the reporting function. After: accurate ministerial reporting and transcript production do not carry that immunity. No new Bivens cause, automatic liability or falsification exception is adopted; other defenses and jurisdictional requirements remain open.

**Operative relief:** The Ninth Circuit judgment is reversed and the case remanded. The reporter and company cannot obtain absolute judicial immunity merely because accurate reporting is integral to the judicial process. The Court makes no liability finding, establishes no constitutional damages cause, resolves no other immunity or defense and awards no damages. Remaining federal and state claims must be addressed under the applicable jurisdictional and remedial rules.

#### United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America, Inc. — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/national_bank_merits_1993-06-07.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0, in the consolidated cases.

#### The court may decide a necessary antecedent question of governing law

**Holding and operative rule:** In this live dispute, the court of appeals could consider whether the statute authorizing the challenged activity remained in force even though the parties initially assumed it did, after giving them a fair opportunity to brief the issue. This authority to identify governing law does not impose a general duty to raise every unargued issue or dispense with adversarial fairness.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the parties’ initial framing did not compel a court to assume the existence of the law on which their dispute depended. Kamen distinguishes a properly presented claim from every legal argument bearing on its resolution, and Arcadia recognizes the need to decide a question logically antecedent to the one the parties emphasize. The insurance agents and federal defendants remained adverse over the validity of the Comptroller’s action, so statutory existence was a governing-law question within a concrete controversy. The court of appeals requested argument and supplemental briefing, rather than resolving an unexpected premise without an opportunity to respond. Its consideration of the issue was therefore permissible even though its answer was wrong. The Court rejects neither party presentation in general nor the need for fair procedure. It does not decide whether a court would be required to raise this question in different circumstances or authorize advisory resolution of unrelated legal issues.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Kamen v. Kemper Financial Services, Inc., 500 U.S.90: a presented claim permits consideration of the governing legal arguments.
- Arcadia v. Ohio Power Co., 498 U.S.73: a necessary antecedent question may be resolved.
- Swift & Co. v. Hocking Valley Railway Co., 243 U.S.281: parties’ legal assumptions do not bind a court’s statement of governing law.

#### The enacted statutory structure establishes that the insurance authorization survived

**Holding and operative rule:** The 1916 insurance-agency provision amended §13 of the Federal Reserve Act rather than Revised Statutes §5202, notwithstanding the misplaced quotation marks in the printed text. The 1918 amendment of §5202 therefore did not repeal it; omission from a nonpositive-law Code edition cannot overcome the governing Statutes at Large.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the full enactment, rather than two isolated quotation marks, identifies the amended provision. The 1916 Act restated §13 of the Federal Reserve Act and carried forward language within that section referring to §5202. Its arrangement, neighboring Federal Reserve powers and consistent references to the Act establish where the operative amendment begins and ends. Treating the printer’s marks as controlling would detach the insurance authorization and neighboring provisions from the structure Congress enacted. Hammock permits punctuation to yield to that demonstrated statutory meaning, without creating a general judicial power to improve legislation. The 1918 Act amended §5202 by adding another exception and thus did not repeal the separate insurance provision in §13. The later Code omission supplies no contrary enactment because that title was not positive law. The enrolled-bill principle concerns authenticity, which nobody disputes here. The Court need not rely on legislative history, agency deference or the desirability of bank insurance sales to establish survival.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Hammock v. Loan & Trust Co., 105 U.S.77: punctuation read in the whole enacted context where that context establishes the legal meaning.
- Marshall Field & Co. v. Clark, 143 U.S.649: enrolled-bill authenticity distinguished from interpretation of an agreed authentic text.
- Posadas v. National City Bank, 296 U.S.497: a description of §5202 amendments does not decide this repeal question.
- Commissioner v. First Security Bank of Utah, 405 U.S.394: prior assumed validity is not treated as a holding resolving statutory survival.
- Germain, March 9, 1992: entered fidelity to operative statutory language and coexistence preserved.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: the statute’s continued existence was disputed because of printed punctuation and Code omission. After: all nine hold the 1916 provision survived the 1918 amendment and permit the antecedent inquiry made after briefing. Courts gain no general power to rewrite statutes for policy reasons. The geographic reach of the bank’s agency activity and the Comptroller’s construction remain for the remand.

**Operative relief:** The District of Columbia Circuit judgment is reversed and the consolidated cases remanded. The surviving insurance-agency provision supplies governing law for the remaining challenge to the Comptroller’s interpretation. The Court does not approve unlimited geographic insurance sales, decide that interpretation, or grant an additional petition. No substantive geographic rule is inferred from the statutory survival holding.

#### Rake v. Wade — 1993-06-07

[Controlling record](records/rake_merits_1993-06-07.md). Affirmed, 9–0.

#### Section 506(b) supplies interest on these oversecured arrearages

**Holding and operative rule:** For an allowed secured claim whose collateral value, after recovery under §506(c), exceeds the claim, §506(b) allows interest within that surplus even if the agreement does not provide interest on arrearages.
