In these plans the parties accept application through confirmation; reasonable fees, costs and charges remain subject to the separate requirement that the agreement provide for them.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statutory entitlement to interest does not depend on an interest-on-arrearages term in the notes. Ron Pair reads §506(b) to distinguish interest from the following category of reasonable fees, costs and charges provided by agreement. These claims are oversecured, and the arrearages form part of the allowed secured obligation. Interest is therefore available within the value remaining after the recovery contemplated by §506(c). The debtors’ cure argument does not remove that entitlement: §1322(b)(5) permits a method of treating default, but contains no exception displacing §506(b). Nobelman protects the holder’s mortgage rights while recognizing that independent Code provisions continue to operate; it does not make contract silence decisive here. The parties agree on the provision’s preconfirmation application for these plans, and the Court does not announce a universal temporal endpoint for every case. It awards no unagreed fee and decides no undersecured-claim question. Calculation must preserve the distinction between this accrual and later present-value protection.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Ron Pair Enterprises, Inc., 489 U.S.235: statutory interest distinguished from agreement-dependent reasonable fees, costs and charges.
- Nobelman v. American Savings Bank, June 1, 1993: protected mortgage rights coexist with independent Code provisions; contract silence does not displace §506(b).

#### Deferred cure payments must preserve present value under the applicable confirmation alternative

**Holding and operative rule:** When a plan provides for an allowed secured arrearage claim through deferred payments, the creditor objects and the debtor retains the collateral, §1325(a)(5)(B) requires lien retention and distributions worth at least the allowed claim as of the plan’s effective date. Cure and maintenance under §1322(b)(5) do not exempt those arrearages from that requirement; creditor acceptance or surrender remains a distinct statutory alternative.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that these plans provide for the arrearage claims by prescribing how they will be paid. Section 1325(a)(5) is not confined to claims whose contractual rights are otherwise modified. Wade objects and the debtors retain the collateral, so the applicable alternative requires retention of the lien and distributions having the necessary value as of the plan’s effective date. Deferred dollars do not alone supply present value; the payment stream must include the value protection the statute requires. The cure provision permits correction of default within a reasonable time while maintaining payments on obligations extending beyond the plan, but it does not suspend the confirmation requirements. The contrary view would turn the cure label into an exception absent from the text. Nobelman’s anti-modification holding addresses a different provision and does not supply that exception. The Court decides no rate, no treatment of all undersecured mortgages and no automatic entitlement to every fee. The calculation must not duplicate recovery for the same period.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Nobelman, June 1, 1993: the anti-modification rule and express cure authority retained without erasing the separate confirmation requirement.
- Ron Pair, 489 U.S.235: distinct Bankruptcy Code provisions receive their own operative effect; the present-value holding rests on §1325(a)(5)(B), not §506(b) alone.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: Ron Pair governed oversecured interest and the actual Nobelman decision protected home-mortgage rights while preserving independent statutory constraints. After: §506(b) accrual and §1325(a)(5)(B) present value apply separately to these deferred cure arrearages. Section 1322(b)(5) remains available where default is cured within a reasonable time and ongoing payments are maintained during the case on a claim whose final payment falls after the last plan payment. The Court sets no rate and decides no later statutory amendment or universal undersecured-mortgage rule.

**Operative relief:** The Tenth Circuit judgment is affirmed. Plans using deferred cure payments over Wade’s objection must satisfy the separate accrual and present-value requirements. The Court fixes no interest rate or dollar award and does not duplicate recovery for overlapping periods. Fees, costs and charges must independently satisfy their statutory conditions; no automatic attorney-fee award follows. The Hannons’ nonpetitioning status is preserved without inventing a separate grant.

#### Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah — 1993-06-11

[Controlling record](records/lukumi_merits_1993-06-11.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0 in the judgment; all four challenged ordinances are invalid as the scheme presented.

#### Objective religious targeting defeats neutrality

**Holding and operative rule:** A law burdening religious practice is not neutral when its text, definitions, exemptions and operation identify religious conduct for distinctive restriction, even if it avoids naming the religion. Hialeah’s interlocking scheme targets Santeria sacrifice on that objective showing; adverse impact alone and the words “ritual” or “sacrifice” alone do not establish the rule’s trigger.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Parts II-A-1 and II-A-3; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the city’s design, not a label of secular purpose, determines neutrality here. Fowler forbids discriminatory treatment of religious exercise, and Smith does not shield a law whose object is to suppress it. Ordinance 87-71 excludes killings primarily for food while capturing offerings to the orishas. Ordinance 87-52 reaches ritual killing with a food intent but spares the licensed food establishments described in its own exception. Ordinance 87-40’s necessity judgment treats religious reasons as insufficient while accepting comparable secular reasons. Those provisions leave almost only the Santeria practice prohibited. The city therefore cannot prevail merely by avoiding an express denominational name. Ordinance 87-72 reaches more secular conduct, but operates with the same scheme; its independent validity in another setting is reserved. The words ritual and sacrifice can have secular uses, and unequal impact alone is insufficient. The demonstrated definitions and operation supply the targeting conclusion without making subjective legislative motive an indispensable condition.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Fowler v. Rhode Island, 345 U.S.67: discrimination in the treatment of religious exercise prohibited.
- Employment Division v. Smith, 494 U.S.872: targeting falls outside the neutral-law rule; that rule is not reconsidered here.
- McDaniel v. Paty, 435 U.S.618: special religious disabilities distinguished from neutral regulation.
- Bowen v. Roy, 476 U.S.693: the individualized-exemption discussion supports requiring a compelling reason for denying religious hardship treatment where comparable secular reasons are assessed.

#### Substantial exemptions for comparable secular harm defeat general applicability

**Holding and operative rule:** A law is not generally applicable where it substantially leaves unregulated comparable secular conduct threatening the interests invoked to burden religious exercise. Each challenged ordinance fails that minimum requirement here, including 87-72’s express state-law exemption for small-number hog or cattle operations; the Court does not define every boundary of general applicability.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Part II-B; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a city cannot pursue these asserted harms only when they arise from religious conduct. Smith’s general-applicability boundary and Fowler’s equality principle require examination of the comparable activities left outside the prohibitions. Food slaughter, hunting, fishing, pest eradication and permitted euthanasia undermine the claim that the first three ordinances comprehensively prevent unnecessary animal killing. The public-health distinction is equally deficient: disposal of carcasses and consumption of uninspected meat create the same risks regardless of religious motivation, yet the scheme leaves comparable secular conduct alone. Ordinance 87-72 has its own defect, an express exception for small-number hog and cattle operations qualifying under state law. The city does not explain why those operations escape the asserted health and cruelty concerns. This is substantial underinclusion, not an inconsequential imperfection in a comprehensive law. The Court need not define a universal general-applicability test or hold that every exemption invalidates every regulation. The unequal pursuit of the identified harms resolves these ordinances.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Smith, 494 U.S.872: general-applicability requirement applied, without reopening its neutral-law holding.
- Fowler, 345 U.S.67: unequal treatment of religious exercise applied.
- Hobbie v. Unemployment Appeals Commission, 480 U.S.136: protection against unequal treatment of religious observers preserved.

#### Narrow tailoring independently fails for every ordinance

**Holding and operative rule:** A restriction of religious practice that is nonneutral or not generally applicable must satisfy compelling-interest scrutiny and narrow tailoring. These ordinances fail narrow tailoring independently of whether the asserted ends could be compelling because comparable harms are left unregulated and neutral, narrower controls on disposal, care, killing methods and location could address the city’s concerns.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Part III, narrow-tailoring ground; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the city’s chosen means are not narrowly drawn to its asserted ends. Yoder and McDaniel require scrutiny of the actual burden, not acceptance of an abstractly important objective. General disposal rules could address carcasses left in public places; care requirements could address the conditions in which animals are held; and rules about killing methods could address cruelty without classifying the religious reason as unnecessary. The district court’s view that secrecy makes narrower rules unenforceable does not explain why a ban on private sacrifice would work better than rules against publicly observable disposal. A slaughterhouse label likewise supplies no justification by itself, and the first three ordinances apply regardless of location. The exemption in 87-72 further defeats the fit asserted for its own location rule. Under Arkansas Writers’ Project, failure of narrow tailoring suffices to invalidate the challenged restriction. The Court does not decide entitlement to an exemption from a genuinely evenhanded anti-cruelty law.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Wisconsin v. Yoder, 406 U.S.205: rigorous justification of restrictions on religious exercise applied.
- McDaniel, 435 U.S.618: demanding scrutiny of religious disabilities applied.
- Arkansas Writers’ Project, Inc. v. Ragland, 481 U.S.221: lack of narrow tailoring independently suffices under strict scrutiny.
- Schneider v. State, 308 U.S.147: collateral public harms cannot justify unnecessary suppression when direct regulation is available.

#### The selectively pursued interests are not compelling in this application

**Holding and operative rule:** Where the government burdens protected religious conduct but leaves appreciable comparable harm unregulated despite feasible controls, it has not established that its asserted interest justifies the selective restriction at the highest constitutional level. Hialeah’s substantial underinclusion independently defeats its compelling-interest showing for this scheme; the decision does not declare animal welfare or public health inherently unimportant.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Part III, compelling-interest ground; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court also holds that the city has not established a compelling justification for the discrimination actually imposed. Strict scrutiny examines the interest in applying the particular restriction, not merely the importance of public health or humane treatment in general. The city permits many secular killings and fails to address comparable disposal and inspection risks while prohibiting the religious practice. Those choices leave appreciable harm of the same asserted kind outside the scheme. Florida Star illustrates why an interest cannot sustain an exceptional speech restriction when substantial equivalent harm remains unaddressed, and the same defect is decisive here. The city’s assertion that secular food use or pest control is obviously necessary does not explain why religious reasons alone must be discounted. This ground is independent of the narrower-means failure. It does not resolve the constitutionality of a comprehensive anti-cruelty law or deny the seriousness of the interests identified. It holds only that the selective scheme does not establish the required justification.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Florida Star v. B.J.F., 491 U.S.524: substantial unregulated comparable harm undermines the asserted exceptional justification; no broader rule is imported from a separate writing.
- Yoder, 406 U.S.205: the asserted interest must justify the particular restriction at the required level.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: Smith, Fowler and the inherited strict-scrutiny principles governed religious targeting. After: five directly adopt the objective-neutrality ground, six the minimum general-applicability application and seven each independent strict-scrutiny failure. All four ordinances are invalid as presented. The three-Justice enactment-history discussion is noncontrolling. No Marks synthesis is necessary, no free-exercise exemption regime replaces Smith, and no majority decides an exemption from a genuinely general anti-cruelty law. The Court’s June 7 private-access holding remains distinct.

**Operative relief:** The Eleventh Circuit judgment is reversed and the case remanded for declaratory and appropriate injunctive relief preventing enforcement of the four challenged ordinances as the discriminatory scheme presented. The Court does not invalidate Florida’s entire animal-cruelty chapter, decide damages, reopen the individual defendants’ immunity or grant immunity from neutral animal-welfare laws. Any severability determination must identify an independently lawful operative provision; it cannot preserve the same religious discrimination under a different label. Whether Ordinance 87-72 could survive as an independently enacted, differently situated law is not decided.

#### Wisconsin v. Mitchell — 1993-06-11

[Controlling record](records/mitchell_merits_1993-06-11.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0.

#### Federal constitutional effect is not settled by a state court’s characterization

**Holding and operative rule:** The Court accepts the state court’s authoritative construction of statutory terms, but independently determines the federal constitutional effect of the law so construed. Wisconsin’s characterization of the intentional-selection enhancement as punishment of thought therefore does not resolve the First Amendment question or remove the statutory requirement of an independently committed crime.

**Authority:** White’s Parts I and II; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that respect for state-law construction does not surrender the federal constitutional inquiry. Ferber and Terminiello require acceptance of authoritative state definitions that determine what a law prohibits. The Wisconsin court here did not redefine a statutory term to eliminate the underlying crime or intentional victim selection. It instead characterized the practical effect of considering discriminatory motive as punishment of thought. Whether that effect violates the First Amendment is the question this Court must decide. The State’s reply that the statute concerns conduct is not, by itself, a complete answer either: the enhanced penalty does depend on a particular motive. The Court must therefore examine whether that motive is constitutionally relevant to the criminal act rather than presume validity from a label. This method preserves binding state-law meanings while allowing independent federal review. It creates no license to rewrite the selection requirement or decide unrelated state-law sentencing questions.

**Precedent treatment:**

- New York v. Ferber, 458 U.S.747: authoritative state construction accepted.
- Terminiello v. Chicago, 337 U.S.1: binding construction of operative state-law terms distinguished from the federal characterization at issue here.

#### Intentional racial selection can aggravate an independently criminal battery

**Holding and operative rule:** The First Amendment permits an enhanced penalty for an independently criminal battery when the defendant intentionally selects the victim because of race under the applicable statutory predicate. The State may prove a relevant motive through lawful evidence, but may not punish abstract racist belief, expression or association without its legally relevant connection to the offense or another legitimate issue.

**Authority:** White’s Parts I and II; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the selection requirement identifies a feature of the criminal attack, not an independent offense of offensive belief. Barclay permits consideration of racial motivation connected to the murder there; the entered Dawson decision preserves that distinction while prohibiting irrelevant proof of abstract belief. A legislature may account for the distinct individual and community harms it reasonably attributes to deliberately discriminatory attacks. Hishon and Roberts likewise show that considering discriminatory purpose does not automatically convert regulation of conduct into punishment of protected expression. The entered R.A.V. decision invalidated substantial reach into protected speech and reserved the broader question of distinctions within unprotected speech categories. This statute instead requires an independently criminal act and intentional selection of its target. Nothing in that reserved question requires invalidation here. The State must prove the actual statutory nexus, and speech evidence remains subject to relevance and other evidentiary limits. No punishment for belief alone or automatic admission of offensive statements is authorized.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Dawson v. Delaware, March 9, 1992: entered connection-to-legitimate-issue limit applied; abstract belief remains protected.
- Barclay v. Florida, 463 U.S.939: racial motivation connected to the criminal act distinguished from abstract association evidence.
- R.A.V. v. City of St. Paul, June 22, 1992: entered substantial-overbreadth holding distinguished; noncontrolling content-selection reasoning is not imported.
- Hishon v. King & Spalding, 467 U.S.69, and Roberts v. United States Jaycees, 468 U.S.609: regulation of discriminatory conduct distinguished from suppression of a protected message.
- Runyon v. McCrary, 427 U.S.160: protection for belief does not confer a right to engage in unlawful discrimination.

#### Speculative future evidentiary use does not establish substantial overbreadth

**Holding and operative rule:** The possibility that a person might suppress protected views for fear that those views could later help prove intentional selection in a future crime does not establish substantial overbreadth of this enhancement. The First Amendment permits relevant statements to prove criminal intent, subject to evidentiary safeguards and Dawson’s prohibition on punishment for abstract belief.

**Authority:** White’s Part III; White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the proposed chilling chain is too speculative to invalidate the statute on its face. It assumes that a person will withhold protected expression because the person might later commit a covered crime and the expression might then prove the required discriminatory selection. The statute does not prohibit the present expression itself. Haupt establishes that earlier statements may be relevant evidence of criminal intent even though the statements could lawfully have been made. Dawson supplies the necessary boundary: irrelevant belief or association is not a legitimate basis for punishment. Ordinary rules of relevance, reliability and admissibility continue to operate, so the decision does not admit every offensive remark a prosecutor offers. The contrary argument would turn legitimate evidentiary use into a general constitutional obstacle to proving motive. That attenuated concern is unlike a law directly reaching a substantial range of protected speech. It therefore does not sustain this facial challenge to the intentional-selection enhancement.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Haupt v. United States, 330 U.S.631: relevant earlier statements may establish criminal intent subject to careful evidentiary scrutiny.
- Dawson, March 9, 1992: the entered limit on irrelevant abstract-belief evidence preserved.
- R.A.V., June 22, 1992: direct substantial reach into protected expression distinguished from speculative evidentiary chilling.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: R.A.V. supplied substantial-overbreadth law and Dawson required a legitimate connection for belief evidence. After: all nine sustain enhancement for this intentional-selection battery; eight reject the distinct speculative-chill ground in a controlling opinion. The reserved within-unprotected-speech question remains open. No later jury-allocation rule, independent speech crime or blanket admissibility rule enters.

**Operative relief:** The Wisconsin Supreme Court judgment is reversed and the case remanded for proceedings consistent with the enhancement’s First Amendment validity. The Court removes the constitutional obstacle but does not issue a new sentencing order or decide equal-protection and vagueness claims outside the granted question. The intermediate court found the former waived and rejected the latter; this Court does not alter those unreviewed determinations or erase ordinary evidentiary objections.

#### United States Department of Treasury v. Fabe — 1993-06-11

[Controlling record](records/fabe_merits_1993-06-11.md). Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded; 5–4 on protection for policyholders and necessary administration, with unanimous rejection of the challenged wage and general-creditor preferences over federal claims.

#### The first clause protects the policy-performance priority

**Holding and operative rule:** Where a conflicting federal statute does not specifically relate to insurance, §1012(b)’s first clause preserves a state liquidation priority to the extent it regulates fulfillment of policyholder obligations. Ohio’s policyholder protection therefore prevails over §3713 in that scope; neither the insurance label nor inclusion in a broadly named class protects every other claim.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor (5 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that performance of the insurance promise remains part of insurance regulation when the insurer becomes insolvent. National Securities identifies the insurer-policyholder relationship as the relevant focus and permits a statute with different functions to be evaluated by the function actually at issue. Ohio’s priority for policy obligations is directed to payment of those promises, not merely to the existence of an insurance company. Royal Drug and Pireno do not confine insurance to writing the contract while making its performance irrelevant; their antitrust settings also differ from the first clause’s laws enacted for the purpose of regulation. The government’s argument that every claimant has become an ordinary creditor overlooks the continuing policy obligation. The first clause supplies Congress’s own allocation between qualifying state law and a conflicting federal law not specifically addressing insurance. The Court protects that operation, not the entire liquidation code. It does not decide every antitrust use of the phrase or confer priority on every claim grouped in Ohio’s third class.

**Precedent treatment:**

- SEC v. National Securities, Inc., 393 U.S.453: policyholder relationship and function-specific treatment of a state statute applied.
- Group Life & Health Insurance Co. v. Royal Drug Co., 440 U.S.205: antitrust setting distinguished; insurance companies’ every business decision is not insurance regulation.
- Union Labor Life Insurance Co. v. Pireno, 458 U.S.119: risk-transfer and policy-relationship criteria considered without excluding actual policy performance.
- Prudential Insurance Co. v. Benjamin, 328 U.S.408: Congress’s support for state insurance regulation confirms the enacted allocation.
- United States v. Knott, 298 U.S.544: pre-Act priority setting distinguished; it does not nullify §1012(b)’s later operative text.

#### Necessary administration shares the protected function

**Holding and operative rule:** A liquidation-administration priority is preserved against §3713 to the extent the administration is reasonably necessary to implement the protected payment of policyholder obligations. Ohio’s administration preference serves that function here, but the ruling does not transform ordinary wage debts or any expense merely labeled administrative into a protected claim.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor (5 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that protecting policyholder payment would be ineffective if the process necessary to collect and distribute the insurer’s assets could not begin. The Ohio liquidator must preserve and recover assets, evaluate claims and administer the distribution. Administration reasonably necessary to carry out that work is connected directly to fulfillment of the policy obligations protected by National Securities. This is more than a general assertion that every creditor preference may improve an insurer’s reliability. Royal Drug rejects that unlimited indirect-benefit reasoning, and the Court does not revive it. The government’s all-creditors approach would deny protection to the very process that makes the specifically protected policyholder priority possible. Conversely, calling a debt an administration expense cannot settle whether it has the required function. On remand, disputed actual expenses must receive their proper legal classification. The separate ordinary wage preference does not become necessary administration merely because employees helped the insurer before liquidation, and the Court fixes no amount of allowable expense.

**Precedent treatment:**

- National Securities, 393 U.S.453: the protected policyholder relationship includes administration necessary to implement the policy-payment function.
- Royal Drug, 440 U.S.205: indirect benefit to insurer reliability alone remains insufficient.

#### Ordinary creditor preferences remain subject to federal priority

**Holding and operative rule:** Section1012(b)’s first clause does not preserve Ohio’s ordinary employee-wage and general-creditor preferences against §3713 merely because those priorities appear in an insurance-liquidation statute. Their indirect relationship to policyholder welfare is too tenuous; the United States retains federal priority over these unprotected preferences, subject to the actual statutory trigger.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court; the four dissenters agree in this disposition on their broader ground; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor (5 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a limited protection for insurance regulation cannot become an exception for every debt of an insurance company. National Securities directs attention to the protected relationship, and Royal Drug rejects the premise that every measure improving a company’s reliability necessarily regulates the business of insurance. Ordinary employees and commercial creditors claim in those capacities, not by enforcing the insurance promise. Their possible contribution to the insurer’s general financial health does not connect their priority sufficiently to payment of policyholder obligations. The broader Sixth Circuit approach would erase the statutory boundary between regulation of insurance and regulation of all an insurer’s affairs. Section 3713 therefore controls the competing priorities for these classes. The four dissenters would reach that same result through federal priority throughout, but their agreement in result does not enlarge the majority’s rationale. The effect on remaining state priorities and severability belongs on remand under state law; the Court does not supply an equitable reordering of the whole statute.

**Precedent treatment:**

- National Securities, 393 U.S.453: distinct shareholder/creditor functions are not protected merely because regulated entities are insurers.
- Royal Drug, 440 U.S.205: tenuous indirect benefits to policyholders do not establish protected insurance regulation.
- Stanton v. Stanton, 421 U.S.7: effects on remaining state-law provisions and severability left for remand rather than judicial redesign.

**Present operation and before/after:** Before: §3713 ordinarily controlled conflicting creditor priorities, subject to McCarran-Ferguson’s actual insurance exception. After: five preserve policyholder priorities and necessary administration under the first clause, while the wage and general-creditor preferences yield. Nine agree on that latter disposition but only five join the specific partial-protection rationale. The second clause’s antitrust exemption and the separate §1013(b) boycott provision remain unaltered; no Marks combination is needed.

**Operative relief:** The Sixth Circuit judgment is affirmed insofar as the state law protects policyholder obligations and administration reasonably necessary to that protection, reversed insofar as it places ordinary wage and general-creditor claims above federal claims, and remanded for further proceedings. Classification of actual expenses, effects on remaining state priority provisions and severability are left to lawful determination on remand. No complete payout schedule, distribution amount or blanket immunity for every claim in Ohio’s third class is ordered. The effect on remaining provisions is a state-law question; the Court does not itself redesign Ohio’s classes.

#### Northeastern Florida Chapter, AGC v. Jacksonville — 1993-06-14

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Northeastern_Florida_Chapter_AGC_v_Jacksonville_merits_1993-06-14.md). Reversed and remanded, 7–2.

#### Equal opportunity to compete is a concrete injury

**Holding and operative rule:** An association may challenge a governmental barrier preventing members who are able and ready to bid from competing on equal terms, without proving that a member would have won a contract, when a member has individual standing, the interest is germane to the association’s purpose, and neither the claim nor the requested relief requires individual-member participation. The unchallenged allegations accepted here establish the required connection; genuinely disputed elements remain subject to stage-appropriate proof.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Equal opportunity to compete is a concrete injury portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the injury is denial of equal treatment in competition, not eventual loss of a contract. Turner protects eligibility for consideration, and Bakke likewise distinguishes a chance to compete from a guaranteed benefit. Members regularly engaged in city contracting allege that they would bid on reserved work if allowed. The ordinance itself prevents that participation, and relief removing the barrier would redress the injury. Warth does not require proof of a successful bid in a competition the government closed to the bidder; it requires a concrete connection between the plaintiff and the challenged conduct. Hunt still governs the association’s representation of its members. Lujan’s evidentiary rules remain: the parties did not contest these allegations in their summary-judgment submissions, and the Court does not excuse missing proof when an element is genuinely disputed. Nothing in this threshold decision determines whether the preference is constitutionally justified.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Turner v. Fouche, 396 U.S.346 — equal eligibility applied.
- Bakke, 438 U.S.265 — opportunity injury distinguished from guaranteed selection.
- Warth, 422 U.S.490 — concrete causal connection retained.
- Hunt, 432 U.S.333 — association requirements retained.
- Lujan, June 12, 1992 — concrete injury and stage-specific proof preserved.

#### The replacement continues the controversy

**Holding and operative rule:** Repeal does not moot this dispute because the successor’s sheltered-market method continues to exclude the association’s members from particular competitions on the challenged basis. Changes in groups, percentages and methods do not remove that injury; a replacement actually ending it could produce a different result.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, The replacement continues the controversy portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the operative program must be examined for the injury actually alleged. City of Mesquite rejects automatic mootness from voluntary repeal when the challenged conduct continues or can recur. Jacksonville’s new program changes details but retains a sheltered market in which specified firms alone may compete. The association continues to confront the unequal opportunity that gives it standing. The city’s argument that the old ordinance no longer exists identifies a change in legal instrument, not disappearance of the injury. Diffenderfer involved a change removing the operative statutory basis for the challenged treatment; it does not make every legislative revision conclusive. The conclusion is confined to the replacement mechanism shown here. On remand the courts must address the current program and relief capable of redressing the injury, rather than automatically restoring every provision of an injunction against the old ordinance. The constitutional merits of either preference remain undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- City of Mesquite, 455 U.S.283 — voluntary cessation applied.
- Diffenderfer, 404 U.S.412 — distinguished because the challenged injury continues.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds an unequal-opportunity application of Article III and a case-specific continuing-controversy holding. No equal-protection merits standard changes.

#### South Dakota v. Bourland — 1993-06-14

[Canonical Decision Record](records/South_Dakota_v_Bourland_merits_1993-06-14.md). Reversed and remanded, 6–3.

#### Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation

**Holding and operative rule:** The Flood Control Act and Cheyenne River Act displaced the treaty right of exclusive occupation on the taken former trust lands and the non-Indian hunting-and-fishing regulation derived from that exclusion right. Retained tribal access and personal hunting-and-fishing privileges do not themselves preserve authority to license or regulate non-Indian public users.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Acquisition displaces exclusion-derived regulation portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress’s treatment of these lands supplies the clear indication required by Dion. The Fort Laramie Treaty originally protected exclusive use and occupation, including control of non-Indian access. Congress acquired the project lands, authorized public recreation under federal management, settled the rights taken, and retained shoreline access and hunting and fishing subject to the regulations governing corresponding public uses without retaining exclusion of the public. Montana explains the relationship between exclusive occupation and the regulatory authority claimed from it; Brendale confirms the importance of the land’s legal status. Tribal use rights remain meaningful without carrying power to condition the public access Congress provided. Menominee does not compel the opposite result, because this conclusion rests on the particular acquisition and access enactments together, not an assumption that every federal project extinguishes treaties. The Court requires no particular verbal formula for abrogation. It does not decide reservation boundaries, tribal criminal jurisdiction or every possible regulation on acquired acreage.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Dion, 476 U.S.734 — clear congressional choice required, without an invariable express-words requirement.
- Montana, 450 U.S.544 — exclusion-derived authority and effects of alienation applied.
- Brendale, 492 U.S.408 — land-specific setting considered without inventing a universal rule from its fracture.
- Menominee, 391 U.S.404 — protection against casual implied abrogation preserved; particular enactments distinguished.

#### Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry

**Holding and operative rule:** Loss of treaty-based exclusion does not decide whether a Montana exception or properly established federal authorization permits particular regulation. Remand preserves the exceptions for consensual commercial dealings, contracts, leases or other arrangements with the Tribe or its members, and for conduct threatening or directly affecting tribal political integrity, economic security, health or welfare, within Montana’s limits. General inherent sovereignty alone does not restore the abrogated regulatory power.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Residual authority remains a distinct inquiry portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the source rejected today must not be confused with every possible source of tribal civil authority. Montana generally limits regulation of nonmembers on non-Indian land but identifies two exceptions requiring attention to actual relationships and conduct. The court of appeals relied on treaty-based control for the former trust acreage while preserving a separate inquiry for formerly non-Indian fee land. The district court found neither exception satisfied on either land category, but the court of appeals did not review those findings for the former trust lands; that review remains. The Corps-regulation theory was not raised below, was omitted from the Government’s brief and conflicted with record evidence of the Corps’ view. The Court declines deference to that undeveloped litigating position. The State therefore receives no declaration that every non-Indian activity throughout the reservation is exclusively subject to state law. Any claimed authorization and concrete conflict with superior federal rules must be assessed on their own terms. Remand permits that inquiry without reviving the displaced exclusion right or collapsing the two land categories into a single historical ownership premise. No result on those undeveloped questions is directed.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Montana, 450 U.S.544 — both consensual-relationship and serious tribal-interest exceptions expressly retained.
- United States v. Wheeler, 435 U.S.313 — retained internal tribal powers do not themselves establish nonmember regulation after the abrogation found here.

**Current operation and transition:** Treaty-derived control is removed as a sufficient source on the taken former trust lands. Residual authority remains unresolved; no general state exclusivity or boundary ruling.

#### Local 144 Nursing Home Pension Fund v. Demisay — 1993-06-14

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Local_144_Nursing_Home_Pension_Fund_v_Demisay_merits_1993-06-14.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0 in the judgment.

#### Section 302 restrains prohibited transactions

**Holding and operative rule:** Section302(e) authorizes restraint of violations of the payment, demand, receipt and related prohibitions in §302(a) and (b); §302(c)(5) identifies qualifying trusts rather than a general supervisory remedy for later administration. Retaining these reserves supplies no transfer remedy without an actual prohibited transaction; payment to a nonqualifying trust remains subject to the statute.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, Section 302 restrains prohibited transactions portion; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the remedial grant must be tied to conduct Congress prohibited. Subsection(a) addresses employer payments, and subsection(b) their request and receipt. The exception describes a trust established for designated employee benefits and money held for that purpose; subsequent misconduct may violate fiduciary or contractual duties without becoming a prohibited receipt. Arroyo’s actual distinction between qualifying receipt and later diversion supports that reading. Its broader supervisory language, like Amax’s related discussion, did not decide this question and cannot enlarge subsection(e). Robinson rejected an additional reasonableness requirement and reserved supervisory scope. The employers’ promise that successor funds would recognize prior service neither bound the existing funds to a new agreement nor transferred their assets. The contrary structural-defect theory substitutes a desired allocation for an operative statutory violation. The Court leaves the separately pleaded ERISA transfer and fiduciary claims for the court of appeals and grants no immunity from independently established duties.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Arroyo, 359 U.S.419 — lawful receipt distinguished from later diversion; contrary supervisory language is dictum.
- Amax, 453 U.S.322 — fiduciary independence retained; supervisory language does not establish jurisdiction.
- Robinson, 455 U.S.562 — reasonableness-review rejection retained; reserved supervisory scope resolved.
- Mertens, June 1, 1993 — unchanged; its remedy limit does not resolve these unreviewed ERISA claims.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds the limit on §302(e) supervisory authority. The three-Justice pooled-fund rationale is not a second Court holding. No ERISA asset entitlement is decided.

#### Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California — 1993-06-14

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Concrete_Pipe_and_Products_of_California_Inc_v_Construction_Laborers_Pension_Trust_for_Southern_California_merits_1993-06-14.md). Affirmed, 9–0 in the judgment.

#### The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication

**Holding and operative rule:** The interested plan sponsor’s withdrawal-liability assessment and demand are enforcement acts, not an adjudication. Due process permits that preliminary demand because the dispute first receives adjudication before a neutral arbitrator, under the lawful arbitral burdens stated separately below.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the constitutional requirement of impartial adjudication attaches to the process that decides the dispute, not every preliminary act asserting a claim. Tumey and Ward require an impartial decisionmaker and reject the notion that ordinary appellate review necessarily cures an earlier biased adjudication. Those principles do not make the plan sponsor an adjudicator merely because it calculates an amount, demands payment and performs preliminary review. Withrow distinguishes preliminary functions from the eventual decision of a contested claim. The sponsor acts as a claimant whose assertion can be disputed in neutral arbitration. That distinction answers the employer’s objection to the sponsor’s institutional interest without treating the interest as irrelevant or permitting it to control the arbitrator. Whether the statutory presumptions compromise the neutral proceeding remains a separate question. The Court sustains the preliminary structure; it does not authorize a plan to replace independent adjudication with review that merely accepts its own determination.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Tumey, 273 U.S.510 — interested adjudication forbidden; the sponsor is not the adjudicator here.
- Ward, 409 U.S.57 — appellate review does not automatically cure initial biased adjudication; that premise is absent here.
- Withrow, 421 U.S.35 — preliminary and adjudicative functions distinguished.

#### The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion

**Holding and operative rule:** In neutral arbitration, §1401(a)(3)(A) places on the employer the burden to disprove the challenged factual determination by a preponderance. It does not add appellate deference to the sponsor’s factual determinations, a clear-and-convincing requirement or a conclusive presumption.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the factual presumption must be read in the context of independent arbitration. The statute combines preponderance language with unreasonable or clearly erroneous terminology. Those expressions ordinarily serve different functions, so the text presents a genuine construction problem rather than an unmistakable command to defer to the sponsor. Crowell supports resolving that difficulty through a permissible construction avoiding a serious constitutional question. An ordinary burden of persuasion gives the statutory presumption effect while preserving the arbitrator’s independent resolution of contested facts. The employer may show that the sponsor’s factual determination is wrong by the ordinary preponderance standard; it need not demonstrate the additional defect that an appellate tribunal would demand from a reviewed adjudication. Section1401(c)’s separate presumption in judicial review of the arbitrator’s findings remains distinct and is not imported into arbitration. The arbitrator’s treatment of the withdrawal-date dispute complied with this construction, so the Court requires no second arbitration on this record.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Crowell v. Benson, 285 U.S.22 — constitutionally sound construction used within the interpretive choice the text permits.
- Section1401(c) — judicial review presumes arbitral findings correct unless rebutted by a clear preponderance; no transfer of that standard into arbitration.

#### Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes

**Holding and operative rule:** Under §1401(a)(3)(B), a contesting employer may rebut the actuarial calculation by a preponderance showing either that assumptions and methods were unreasonable in the aggregate, considering plan experience and reasonable expectations, or that the actuary made a significant error in applying them. The professional and statutory constraints make this presumption constitutionally permissible here; fraud need not be proved.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that actuarial estimation differs from a sponsor’s assertion of historical fact. Under the applicable §1393(a)(1) route, the assumptions and methods must be reasonable in the aggregate, account for plan experience and reasonable expectations, and together offer the actuary’s best estimate of anticipated plan experience. Professional responsibilities and the need for coherent valuation limit arbitrary selection against a withdrawing employer. A range of reasonable estimates is possible, so demonstrating another permissible estimate does not establish aggregate unreasonableness. But the employer also retains the distinct statutory route based on a significant application error; that route does not disappear because the assumptions themselves are reasonable. Nor does either route require proof of corruption or fraud. The Court therefore rejects the claim that the calculation is effectively unreviewable. On this record the neutral arbitrator applied the actuarial presumption and the employer failed to establish the required defect. This conclusion does not authorize sponsor control inconsistent with the statute, ratify every actuarial choice, or replace the independent factual inquiry with professional deference on matters outside actuarial judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Section1393 — statutory constraints on actuarial assumptions and methods applied.
- Section1401(a)(3)(B) — aggregate unreasonableness OR significant application error preserved as separate routes.
- Connolly, 475 U.S.211 — pooled-plan structure provides context, not an irrebuttable actuarial presumption.

#### This withdrawal allocation is rational

**Holding and operative rule:** Allocating a proportionate share of unfunded vested-benefit costs to this withdrawing employer is rational in light of its participation in the pooled pension undertaking. Neither private contribution limits nor the absence of a one-to-one match between its contributions and benefits to its own employees defeats this substantive-due-process application.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, This withdrawal allocation is rational portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress could connect withdrawal liability to participation in a common pension arrangement. Usery permits rational allocation of costs associated with an enterprise’s past activity, and R. A. Gray sustains the legislative response to withdrawal incentives threatening pension security. Concrete Pipe received the advantages of a pooled arrangement in which contributions and service credits were not segregated employer by employer. Its share can therefore reflect pooled obligations rather than only benefits earned while employees worked for it. Private contractual limits do not determine the reach of otherwise constitutional federal economic legislation. The employer’s concern about retrospective burdens warrants examination, but it does not establish irrationality in the allocation actually imposed. Connolly confirms the relevance of participation and the system’s protective purpose without foreclosing every as-applied challenge. The Court does not decide a preserved challenge to classification as a defined-benefit plan, because that classification was not presented for review, or establish liability based on an employer’s wealth alone.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Usery v. Turner Elkhorn, 428 U.S.1 — rational retrospective economic allocation applied.
- PBGC v. R. A. Gray, 467 U.S.717 — pension-protection allocation sustained within actual statutory setting.
- Connolly, 475 U.S.211 — pooling and contractual limits treated consistently with its constitutional holding.

#### The asserted impact does not establish a taking

**Holding and operative rule:** This withdrawal liability is not a taking when its economic impact, reasonable investment-backed expectations and the character of the governmental action are considered together. The substantial monetary burden alone does not establish appropriation or a constitutional percentage ceiling; broader reliance on the former 30%-of-net-worth limit is not independently decided.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The asserted impact does not establish a taking portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the taking claim requires its own analysis rather than an inference from procedural validity. Connolly applies the Penn Central considerations to this adjustment of pension obligations. The Government has neither physically occupied property nor appropriated particular assets for its own use; it has allocated economic burdens arising from a regulated undertaking. The asserted financial impact is substantial, but diminution alone does not establish a taking, and the employer has not shown an impact constitutionally disproportionate to its relationship with the plan. Its contractual limitations cannot themselves prevent legislation within Congress’s authority. The regulatory setting informs reasonable expectations without creating immunity for all retrospective pension laws. The Court does not announce a fixed permissible percentage of net worth, decide a distinct unbriefed entitlement to the former thirty-percent cap, or adjudicate the status of employers entering plans before ERISA. Those broader questions are unnecessary to rejecting the claim actually presented and sustaining this award.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Connolly, 475 U.S.211 — as-applied pension takings analysis followed.
- Penn Central, 438 U.S.104 — economic impact, reasonable expectations and governmental character considered together.
- Village of Euclid, 272 U.S.365 — diminution alone insufficient; no numerical safe harbor derived.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds a binding independent-factfinding construction of §1401(a)(3)(A), preserves both actuarial rebuttal routes and sustains this liability under distinct constitutional analyses. The judicial-review presumption remains separate. No categorical 30%-cap ruling or universal anti-taking immunity.

#### Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills School District — 1993-06-18

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Zobrest_v_Catalina_Foothills_School_District_merits_1993-06-18.md). Vacated and remanded, 5–4; no Establishment Clause merits holding.

#### Antecedent legal grounds must be considered on this remand

**Holding and operative rule:** The live reimbursement claim should return for adversarial consideration of its statutory and regulatory basis and any independently dispositive funding limits before this Court decides establishment. The lower courts must expressly decide whether to entertain the district’s forfeited defenses, considering the family’s opportunity to respond and prejudice; avoidance does not automatically revive abandoned grounds.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Antecedent legal grounds must be considered on this remand portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that an antecedent-law remand is appropriate in this particular posture. Graduation does not extinguish the family’s claim for expenses, and the parties’ exhaustion stipulation leaves that dispute available for review. But the district litigated below on the federal constitutional objection and indicated it otherwise would have furnished the interpreter. Adickes therefore gives the preservation objection real weight. Singleton nevertheless recognizes discretion to address an unpassed-on issue where the circumstances justify it, and Ashwander counsels against unnecessary constitutional adjudication. The IDEA, reimbursement authority and the federal funding regulation present identifiable questions affecting the requested relief. They require adversarial attention, not an assumption that the family lacks an entitlement or that every alternative defense was preserved. A regulation merely implementing establishment limits may not avoid the constitutional question at all. The Court orders that examination while leaving both reimbursement and the Establishment Clause merits unresolved, and without reviving the Free Exercise issue omitted from the petition.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Adickes, 398 U.S.144 — ordinary preservation rule acknowledged, not bypassed silently.
- Singleton, 428 U.S.106 — discretion exercised for this antecedent-law remand.
- Ashwander, 297 U.S.288 — constitutional-necessity principle applied without an automatic forfeiture exception.
- Burlington, 471 U.S.359 — statutory reimbursement context retained; no automatic award.

#### Lack of a duty and an affirmative prohibition are different

**Holding and operative rule:** On remand, absence of a federal mandate to supply the interpreter cannot by itself establish that local provision or reimbursement is prohibited. The courts must determine the actual operation of IDEA, 34 C.F.R.§76.532(a)(1) and any properly entertained state-law defense, distinguishing a bar on religious worship, instruction or proselytization funding from a regulation implementing only the constitutional limit.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Lack of a duty and an affirmative prohibition are different portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that identifying the source of relief requires more precision than asking whether federal law compels the district to provide this service. The district’s local policy and its litigation representations make authorization distinct from obligation. Burlington establishes a statutory setting for reimbursement, but the Court has not adjudicated entitlement under it on this record. Section76.532(a)(1) restricts use of federal funds for religious worship, instruction or proselytization; its scope and the Government’s contention that it implements the Establishment Clause must be examined rather than presumed. An interpretation simply restating the constitutional restriction may leave that question unavoidable. Any Arizona constitutional defense also requires a lawful route consistent with preservation and the federal claim. The district cannot obtain an unexplained merits victory by changing theories late, and the family must have a fair opportunity to answer. This remand neither orders a monitoring scheme nor decides that the interpreter is religious instruction attributable to government.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Burlington, 471 U.S.359 — reimbursement authority distinguished from an automatic entitlement.
- Mueller, 463 U.S.388; Witters, 474 U.S.481; Meek, 421 U.S.349; Wolman, 433 U.S.229; Ball, 473 U.S.373 — no merits treatment by the Court; competing aid precedents remain available.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds only a case-specific discretionary remand and its legal distinctions. Establishment funding remains unresolved; Lee, Lamb’s Chapel, Lemon, Mueller, Witters, Meek, Wolman and Ball are neither replaced nor silently extended.

#### Helling v. McKinney — 1993-06-18

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Helling_v_McKinney_merits_1993-06-18.md). Affirmed and remanded, 7–2.

#### The appellate court could reach the future-effects claim

**Holding and operative rule:** The attempted future-effects proof and related appellate objections gave the court of appeals a sufficient record-based basis to treat future health risk as part of this pro se litigation. That conclusion acknowledges ambiguity and does not authorize new claims on appeal or reopen the independent immediate-symptom and damages-immunity rulings.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, The appellate court could reach the future-effects claim portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court rejects the officials’ contention that the court of appeals invented a wholly new claim. The record is not perfectly clear, and the Court does not manufacture an express amended pleading. McKinney sought to establish actual and potential effects of the smoke exposure, encountered exclusion of potential-effects evidence, and challenged the relevant rulings on appeal. That course gives a sufficient basis for the appellate court’s reading of the case. The seriousness of custodial safety alone would not cure a failure to present a claim; it is the attempted proof and related objections that matter here. The returned proceeding remains narrower than the entire original suit. The directed verdict concerning deliberate indifference to existing symptoms and the separate protection against damages were left intact below and are not reopened. Affirming this procedural scope permits consideration of the future-risk theory under governing substantive law, without deciding that the current facts establish either a constitutional violation or a continuing need for an injunction.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Ordinary claim-scope and appellate-review principles — applied to the actual attempted proof; no new exception for important claims.
- Wilson v. Seiter, 501 U.S.294 — governing substantive requirements retained on the returned claim.

#### Preventive protection does not require existing disease

**Holding and operative rule:** A prisoner may establish an Eighth Amendment violation by proving deliberate indifference to involuntary exposure posing an unreasonable risk of serious future harm. He must prove both the objective gravity of the exposure—including seriousness, likelihood and contemporary society’s unwillingness to tolerate the risk—and the separate subjective requirement under Wilson, assessed against current conditions.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, Preventive protection does not require existing disease portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the State’s duty of custodial safety is not postponed until preventable disease develops. Estelle requires protection against serious medical neglect, while Hutto demonstrates that unsafe confinement conditions can require correction before their full harm occurs. Rhodes supplies the objective seriousness requirement, and Wilson separately requires deliberate indifference. Scientific evidence of risk is necessary to the factual inquiry but is not the entire constitutional standard: McKinney must also establish that society does not tolerate forcing unwilling persons to face this danger. The officials’ proposed categorical present-injury requirement would prevent relief precisely while prevention may still work. That does not make every exposure or negligent response unconstitutional. Transfer to a different institution and the new smoking policy may change exposure, culpability and prospective need; actual implementation must be examined. The Court affirms an opportunity to prove the claim, not a finding of violation, a damages entitlement, or a judicial command to ban smoking throughout Nevada’s prisons.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Estelle, 429 U.S.97 — serious custodial needs and culpability retained.
- Hutto, 437 U.S.678 — protection from unsafe conditions applied.
- Rhodes, 452 U.S.337 — objective seriousness retained.
- Wilson, 501 U.S.294 — deliberate indifference remains a separate requirement.
- DeShaney, 489 U.S.189 — custodial safety obligation supplies context.
- Hudson, February 25, 1992 — nontrivial malicious beating rule remains distinct; no universal conditions standard imported.

**Current operation and transition:** Recognizes a future-risk conditions claim while preserving Wilson’s independent culpability requirement, society-based objective gravity and current prospective-relief limits. No later medical findings or Farmer formulation enters.

#### Reno v. Catholic Social Services, Inc. — 1993-06-18

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Reno_v_Catholic_Social_Services_Inc_merits_1993-06-18.md). Vacated and remanded, 6–3 in the judgment.

#### The individual-determination channel does not foreclose all rule challenges

**Holding and operative rule:** Section1255a(f) channels review of individual legalization determinations but does not categorically eliminate district-court review of a challenge to generally applicable regulations where no individual determination supplies an effective review route. A regulatory label alone establishes neither ripeness nor a right to district-court reconsideration of an individual denial.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The individual-determination channel does not foreclose all rule challenges portion; Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the object of the challenge matters to the statutory channel. McNary distinguishes a systemic challenge from review of an individual application determination under a related legalization statute. Section1255a(f) likewise does not clearly prohibit every challenge to a regulation. But an applicant whose claim depends on a formal denial ordinarily must follow the limited administrative and judicial path Congress specified, including review connected to a deportation order. A plaintiff cannot avoid that structure merely by calling an individual eligibility dispute a policy challenge. Conversely, refusal to accept papers can prevent an application record and formal denial from ever arising. Reading that refusal as necessarily foreclosing all review would require an indication Congress has not supplied. Michigan Academy’s presumption favors a review-preserving construction in that circumstance; it does not prove the facts of any claimant’s case. The Court leaves eligibility, substantive validity of the challenged rules and the ultimate remedy outside this threshold disposition.

**Precedent treatment:**

- McNary, 498 U.S.479 — systemic-review distinction applied; individual denials remain distinct.
- Bowen v. Michigan Academy, 476 U.S.667 — presumption of review applied, not converted into a factual burden.
- Section1255a(f)(2) — late-filing denial bar preserved; no general permission to evade it by never filing.

#### A concrete application barrier must be established

**Holding and operative rule:** An actual refusal to accept a timely application under the challenged rule supplies concrete application sufficient for a ripe district-court challenge where front-desking blocks the individual-review route. Mere awareness of an adverse rule is insufficient on this record; further facts may establish that the front-desking policy substantially caused a comparable inability to apply even without a personal front-desk rejection.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, A concrete application barrier must be established portion; Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a benefits regulation does not necessarily injure every person who reads it. Abbott Laboratories asks whether the controversy is fit for decision and what hardship postponement creates. Here some persons might not otherwise qualify or might have used designated assistance that bypassed front-desking. The existing record does not identify particular class members actually refused acceptance with sufficient precision. Those who were refused encountered the regulation directly and lost the administrative record needed for ordinary review. The Government cannot create that absence and then insist on a formal denial as the only judicial doorway. The Court nevertheless does not equate every decision not to apply with a government refusal. Further evidence might show that the policy substantially caused nonapplication in a sufficiently concrete way; that possibility remains open. The district courts must establish the affected claimants and adequate class representation before choosing relief. No present finding establishes legalization eligibility for every class member.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Abbott Laboratories, 387 U.S.136 — fitness and hardship applied to concrete effects.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S.871 — concrete application and immediately operative burdens distinguished.
- McNary, 498 U.S.479 — effective-review concern applied to refused filings.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds the review-channel distinction and concrete-application requirement with an express indirect-barrier reservation. Vacatur rejects the present undifferentiated relief; no majority decides every equitable-tolling issue or automatically extends the statutory period. Processing an actually timely tender is distinct from extending a deadline.

#### Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation — 1993-06-18

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Harper_v_Virginia_Department_of_Taxation_merits_1993-06-18.md). Reversed and remanded, 8–1.

#### An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review

**Holding and operative rule:** When this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs all still-open direct-review cases concerning events within its substantive scope, whether those events predate or postdate announcement. A State cannot use selective prospectivity to withhold the rule; Chevron Oil is displaced to the extent it permits that practice, without reopening final judgments or deciding every conceivable prospective disposition.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that similarly situated pending litigants receive the same governing federal law. Griffith rejects selective application on criminal direct review, and Beam supplies the civil foundation when the Court already applied the rule to its own litigants. Davis applied federal nondiscrimination law to the pension taxation before it; Michigan’s willingness to refund did not reserve the rule’s temporal operation. Virginia therefore cannot use Chevron Oil to preserve the same discrimination for other pending claimants. Reliance and fiscal disruption are substantial practical concerns, but they cannot make the substantive federal rule vary among contemporaneous open cases. The state refund interpretation here depends on the rejected prospectivity premise and is not an independent state ground insulating that error. The Court distinguishes finality and remedy from choice of governing law. It does not reopen closed judgments, calculate refunds, or decide a hypothetical case in which the Court expressly reserves application to the parties before it.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Davis, 489 U.S.803 — nondiscrimination rule applied to pending claims.
- Griffith, 479 U.S.314 — equal direct-review application extended to this civil setting.
- Beam, 501 U.S.529 — its rejection of selective application clarified through a direct majority rule.
- Chevron Oil, 404 U.S.97 — displaced only insofar as it permits selective prospectivity after application to the original litigants.

#### Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate

**Holding and operative rule:** The state courts must apply Davis and determine the correction state law and federal due process require. If the State compelled payment before an adequate opportunity to contest the tax, meaningful backward-looking relief is required; lawful refunds, retroactive nondiscriminatory assessments or a combination may suffice, while a meaningful prepayment opportunity presents a different remedial setting.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that application of the federal rule does not mechanically select a refund amount. McKesson requires meaningful backward-looking correction when a State collects first and provides no adequate prepayment opportunity to challenge an unconstitutional discriminatory tax. The State may have more than one lawful means of correcting the discrimination, including refunds, constitutionally permissible assessment of previously favored taxpayers, or a combination. Davis similarly distinguishes the nondiscrimination command from the appropriate corrective mechanism. The present record requires the state courts to examine their procedures and actual remedial authority rather than assume either an automatic full refund or unconditional retention of the taxes. Genuine procedural bars and remedial considerations may operate consistently with federal law, but they cannot merely reintroduce selective prospectivity under another name. The Court does not impose liability on absent taxpayers without lawful process or invent a state appropriation. It reverses the federal-law premise and remands the remedy for determination under the proper sources.

**Precedent treatment:**

- McKesson, 496 U.S.18 — pay-first systems require meaningful backward-looking relief; alternatives preserved.
- Davis, 489 U.S.803 — correction of unequal taxation distinguished from a particular refund formula.
- Beam, 501 U.S.529 — procedural and remedial questions kept distinct from temporal governing law.

**Current operation and transition:** Six directly adopt the general rule; eight adopt the remedial holding and reversal. No Marks synthesis. Finality and genuine remedial rules survive; selective prospectivity does not.

#### Sale v. Haitian Centers Council — 1993-06-21

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Sale_v_Haitian_Centers_Council_merits_1993-06-21.md). Reversed, 7–2.

#### Section 243(h) does not govern this offshore return operation

**Holding and operative rule:** Section243(h)’s restriction on deporting or returning an alien to threatened persecution does not govern the challenged interdiction and repatriation beyond the territorial sea. The 1980 removal of the words within the United States does not extend this domestic immigration provision to the coordinated offshore operation.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, Section 243(h) does not govern this offshore return operation portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the provision’s scope must be determined from its statutory setting as well as its protective language. Aramco supplies the presumption against extraterritorial application absent a sufficient indication of congressional intent. Section243(h) addresses the Attorney General’s authority in the domestic immigration system, including exclusion and deportation. Removing the earlier territorial phrase extends protection within that procedural setting without clearly regulating the Coast Guard’s high-seas conduct. The word return has work to do in that setting and does not alone overcome the contextual limitation. Stevic and Cardoza-Fonseca distinguish mandatory withholding from discretionary asylum but do not establish offshore reach. The contrary textual argument is substantial, particularly given the amendment, yet it does not show the required geographic extension. This is a construction of the enacted protection, not an executive emergency exception to an applicable statute. The Court does not determine whether any individual qualifies for refugee protection or whether another source limits a different operation.

**Precedent treatment:**

- EEOC v. Arabian American Oil Co., 499 U.S.244 — geographic presumption applied.
- INS v. Stevic, 467 U.S.407 — withholding standard distinguished from territorial reach.
- INS v. Cardoza-Fonseca, 480 U.S.421 — asylum/withholding distinction preserved.
- Youngstown, 343 U.S.579 — no power to disregard an applicable statute inferred; coverage is the issue decided.

#### Article 33 does not independently limit the high-seas operation

**Holding and operative rule:** Article33 of the Refugee Convention, as undertaken through the 1967 Protocol, does not extend its return prohibition to this high-seas repatriation operation. That territorial construction resolves the asserted treaty restraint without deciding whether the Convention otherwise supplies an independently enforceable cause of action. Article33(2) withholds its benefit where reasonable grounds identify a danger to the security of the country of presence, or a refugee convicted by final judgment of a particularly serious crime constitutes a danger to that country’s community.
