**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, Article 33 does not independently limit the high-seas operation portion; White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the treaty text must be read as a whole in its setting. The prohibition on expelling or returning a refugee to threatened territory is central to Article33, but its language and the context of the territorial refuge system do not establish a rule governing every offshore encounter. The security exception’s reference to the country in which the refugee is present and the negotiating materials support the territorial understanding of the obligation undertaken. The contrary reading gives broad force to return and to the humanitarian object, but purpose alone cannot substitute for the geographic scope of the actual undertaking. The Court does not treat the treaty as meaningless: it preserves its prohibition in the setting it covers while rejecting its application to this operation. Because the asserted substantive restriction does not reach the conduct, the Court need not decide independent self-execution or construct a new remedial action. Nor does this holding authorize return from domestic custody contrary to applicable law.

**Precedent treatment:**

- 1951 Refugee Convention Article33 and 1967 Protocol — construed geographically for this operation, without a self-execution holding.
- Stevic, 467 U.S.407 — treaty/statutory relationship considered without importing a general offshore duty.

**Current operation and transition:** The two coverage holdings independently reject the asserted statutory and treaty restraints on this operation. No humanitarian-policy judgment or general executive exemption from law. Stone’s contrary statutory construction remains noncontrolling.

#### Brooke Group v. Brown & Williamson — 1993-06-21

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Brooke_Group_v_Brown_and_Williamson_merits_1993-06-21.md). Affirmed, 5–4; three dissenters would reverse and Stone would vacate and remand for further review.

#### Below-cost pricing must carry a prospect of recoupment

**Holding and operative rule:** A primary-line predatory-pricing claim requires pricing below an appropriate measure of the defendant’s costs and a reasonable prospect of recovering the predatory investment through anticompetitive returns. Tacit oligopolistic coordination is a legally possible mechanism; actual success, an express conspiracy or a single-firm monopoly is not indispensable, but intent, concentration and injury to a rival alone are insufficient.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, Below-cost pricing must carry a prospect of recoupment portion; O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute protects competition rather than insulating a competitor from low prices. Brown Shoe supplies that distinction, and Matsushita and Cargill explain why below-cost losses injure competition only if a plausible later return can make the predatory strategy worthwhile. Robinson-Patman’s primary-line standard requires a reasonable prospect of that recovery, not proof that recoupment already occurred or automatic importation of the Sherman Act’s dangerous-probability formulation. Utah Pie permits supported circumstantial proof of competitive injury; it does not establish liability merely from discriminatory prices and harm to a smaller rival. Oligopolistic coordination is not categorically excluded, because durable supracompetitive returns may in principle arise without one firm obtaining a monopoly. But the evidence must connect the strategy to recovery of the defendant’s own losses, including the consequences of sharing subsequent gains with rivals. The Court need not select an exclusive accounting measure of cost because the separate recoupment requirement resolves this appeal.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown Shoe, 370 U.S.294 — competition/competitor distinction applied.
- Matsushita, 475 U.S.574 — recoupment logic applied without requiring an express conspiracy here.
- Cargill, 479 U.S.104 — low prices distinguished from actionable predation.
- Utah Pie, 386 U.S.685 — read consistently with competitive injury; not treated as rival-protection liability.
- Spectrum Sports, January 25, 1993 — intent cannot replace competitive danger; Sherman Act elements not transplanted wholesale.

#### This record lacks the necessary recoupment showing

**Holding and operative rule:** Even crediting below-cost pricing and predatory purpose and drawing reasonable inferences for Liggett, the evidence does not permit a reasonable jury to find the required prospect of Brown & Williamson recouping its losses through the alleged coordinated pricing mechanism. The failure of that necessary element sustains judgment as a matter of law without a categorical oligopoly exemption.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, This record lacks the necessary recoupment showing portion; O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the evidence must establish the links in the proposed market mechanism, not merely a hostile plan. Liggett points to parallel profitable pricing, internal discipline documents, later list-price increases and expert testimony about costs and promotions. Those materials support intent and a proposed strategy, but the required inference also concerns durable anticompetitive returns sufficient to repay Brown & Williamson’s own investment. Expanding generic output, excess capacity, lower-price entry, discounts separating list from net prices and Reynolds’ contrary incentives undermine that connection. The predator would bear its own losses while sharing any coordinated gains. Matsushita permits scrutiny of whether the proposed mechanism has evidentiary support; it does not authorize choosing between witnesses merely because judges prefer one. On the whole record, the missing recovery showing cannot be supplied by intent or concentration. The Court therefore affirms on competitive injury and does not decide the district court’s independent antitrust-injury and causation grounds or declare that oligopoly recoupment can never be proved.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Matsushita, 475 U.S.574 — economically plausible mechanism must have evidentiary support.
- Cargill, 479 U.S.104 — rival losses do not alone establish competitive harm.
- Utah Pie, 386 U.S.685 — circumstantial proof permitted, but necessary recovery inference absent here.

**Current operation and transition:** Five directly adopt the primary-line rule and this sufficiency holding. Oligopolistic recoupment is legally possible; the case fails on proof. Robinson-Patman remains distinct from attempted monopoly, secondary-line discrimination and other exclusionary conduct.

#### Darby v. Cisneros — 1993-06-21

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Darby_v_Cisneros_merits_1993-06-21.md). Reversed and remanded, 9–0.

#### Section 704 controls exhaustion of superior-agency review

**Holding and operative rule:** In an APA action, an otherwise final agency decision is reviewable without an optional superior-agency appeal unless a statute expressly requires exhaustion or an agency rule both requires the appeal and makes the challenged action inoperative pending it. Courts cannot add their own exhaustion requirement beyond those alternatives; remaining APA finality, reviewability and adequate-remedy conditions still apply.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, Section 704 controls exhaustion of superior-agency review portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress has specified the effect of additional administrative review on finality. Section704 makes otherwise final action final despite reconsideration or an available appeal, subject to an express statutory requirement or an agency rule combining mandatory appeal with inoperativeness. HUD’s optional review provision does not satisfy that rule-based exception. McKart explains institutional reasons for exhaustion but cannot authorize courts to rewrite a governing statute. McCarthy preserves congressional control while addressing judicially imposed exhaustion in a different setting, and McNeil enforces an express FTCA prerequisite rather than a free-standing exhaustion policy. The Government’s expertise and efficiency arguments do not supply the missing statutory condition. The Court’s procedural holding does not establish that the debarment was substantively wrong or that every agency action is reviewable. Lincoln’s distinct reviewability limits remain. The judgment is reversed so the remaining issues can be addressed under the correct APA framework, without requiring this optional appeal.

**Precedent treatment:**

- 5 U.S.C.§704 — statutory requirement OR mandatory-and-inoperative agency rule; alternatives and conjunction preserved.
- McKart, 395 U.S.185 — exhaustion policy subordinate to enacted conditions.
- McCarthy, March 4, 1992 — express statutory primacy retained; different damages-only setting.
- McNeil, May 17, 1993 — express FTCA exhaustion distinguished.
- Lincoln, May 24, 1993 — distinct reviewability limits preserved.

**Current operation and transition:** Adds a binding APA limit on extra judicial exhaustion. No repeal of express statutory exhaustion, review of nonfinal action or universal common-law exhaustion holding.

#### Johnson v. Texas — 1993-06-24

[Canonical Decision Record](records/Johnson_v_Texas_merits_1993-06-24.md). Reversed as to the death sentence and remanded, 5–4; guilt determination untouched.

#### Youth’s independent culpability significance needs an effective sentencing route

**Holding and operative rule:** Considering the charge, evidence and trial as a whole, there is a reasonable likelihood that the special issues prevented Johnson’s jury from giving effect to his youth-related reduced culpability when it truthfully found deliberateness and future danger. Under Graham, Eddings, Penry and Boyde, admission of the evidence and partial relevance to future danger do not supply the missing route; mere possibility of a restrictive reading would be insufficient.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Youth’s independent culpability significance needs an effective sentencing route portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that its decision in Graham governs this application. Eddings recognizes that immaturity can reduce culpability independently of a prediction about later conduct. Johnson was nineteen, and his father’s account of troubled adolescence, substance use and family losses supplies a concrete mitigating argument. Texas correctly observes that youth can be transient and can reduce a prediction of future violence. But the general instruction to consider mitigating evidence remained tied to answering deliberateness and future-danger questions; it did not authorize a lesser sentence when both answers remained yes. Penry distinguishes admitting evidence from allowing its mitigating force, and Boyde requires a reasonable likelihood of preclusion in the charge and trial as a whole. That standard is satisfied, rather than merely conceivably implicated, on this record. Jurek’s conditional general approval remains, but cannot displace Graham’s effective-consideration rule. No categorical age prohibition, favorable evidentiary weight or unrestricted mercy instruction is required.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Graham v. Collins, January 25, 1993 — independent youth-culpability holding applied as binding merits law.
- Eddings, 455 U.S.104 — immaturity’s independent culpability significance applied.
- Lockett, 438 U.S.586 — material mitigation cannot be legally excluded from effect.
- Penry, 492 U.S.302 — admission distinguished from meaningful sentencing effect.
- Boyde, 494 U.S.370 — reasonable likelihood, not mere possibility, applied.
- Jurek, 428 U.S.262 — conditional facial approval preserved; this application fails.

#### Direct review permits the necessary sentence correction

**Holding and operative rule:** Because this is direct review of the preserved mitigation claim, Teague does not bar relief. The death sentence must be reversed for an effective sentencing vehicle permitting the material youth mitigation to affect punishment without a false answer to a factual special issue; Texas may provide adequate capital resentencing or a lawful noncapital sentence, while the guilt judgment remains intact.

**Authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Direct review permits the necessary sentence correction portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the remedy must correct the defective sentencing mechanism without adjudicating guilt anew. Teague governs collateral use of new procedural rules, not a case still on direct review. Moreover, Graham held that the relevant effective-mitigation duty was established before its own finality date; that settled duty supplies the governing rule here. The defect here concerns what the jury was legally able to do with supported mitigation, not a judicial determination that youth necessarily outweighs aggravation. A lawful instruction or special issue can permit the relevant judgment without requiring jurors to deny deliberateness or predicted danger they actually find. The State retains authority to structure its sentencing process consistently with that duty. The Court neither prescribes exact future wording nor automatically selects life imprisonment regardless of state law. It reverses only the judgment sustaining death and remands for a constitutionally adequate capital proceeding or a lawful noncapital sentence.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Teague, 489 U.S.288 — collateral-review restriction inapplicable to this direct appeal.
- Graham, January 25, 1993 — merits and remedy principles followed; collateral-review limitations do not bar this direct appeal.
- Jurek, 428 U.S.262 — authority to structure sentencing retained subject to effective mitigation.

**Current operation and transition:** Applies and confirms Graham’s effective-mitigation rule on direct review of a nineteen-year-old’s concrete youth evidence. No categorical age bar, facial invalidation or right to favorable mitigation weight. The conviction survives.

#### Godinez v. Moran — 1993-06-24

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Godinez_v_Moran_merits_1993-06-24.md).

#### A common capacity standard and a separate actual waiver

**Holding and operative rule:** Dusky supplies the constitutional mental-capacity standard for standing trial, pleading guilty and choosing to waive counsel: sufficient present ability to consult with counsel with rational understanding, and rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings. A competent defendant may relinquish the rights only through a knowing, intelligent and voluntary waiver; competence does not establish that the particular choice was informed and uncoerced.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that mental capacity and the validity of a choice are separate questions. Dusky requires rational understanding, not a bare awareness of the courtroom or the charges. Faretta protects a competent defendant’s informed choice of self-representation without requiring the legal skill of a lawyer. That distinction defeats the claim that the decision to waive counsel necessarily requires professional ability to conduct a defense. It does not relieve the judge of examining the choice actually made. Boykin requires an informed guilty plea, and Zerbst requires a valid relinquishment of counsel. Westbrook’s additional inquiry is preserved through those waiver requirements rather than a second, higher constitutional capacity threshold. The Ninth Circuit therefore cannot sustain its conditional writ on the premise that ordinary trial competence is constitutionally insufficient as a capacity standard. States remain free to provide further safeguards. The Court decides neither competence for execution nor a distinct rule for the later conduct of a trial without counsel.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Dusky v. United States, 362 U.S.402: the functional competence standard governs each presented choice.
- Faretta v. California, 422 U.S.806: legal skill is distinct from competence to choose representation.
- Boykin v. Alabama, 395 U.S.238, and Johnson v. Zerbst, 304 U.S.458: actual informed and voluntary waiver remains separately required.
- Westbrook v. Arizona, 384 U.S.150: additional waiver inquiry preserved; a higher constitutional capacity quantum is not required.

#### Existing findings do not eliminate the distinct inquiry on remand

**Holding and operative rule:** A further competence determination is required when circumstances give the court reason to doubt present competence; no automatic hearing is required for every plea or waiver. Reversal of the higher-standard ruling leaves the existing claims about adequate inquiry and informed, voluntary relinquishment for consideration under the correct standards and the applicable pre-AEDPA treatment of state findings.

**Authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that rejecting the Ninth Circuit’s legal standard does not adjudicate every factual and remedial issue in Nevada’s favor. The trial court expressly found competence and valid waivers, and a later state hearing considered the challenge. Those findings must be addressed, not described as nonexistent. Pate and Drope nevertheless require attention to circumstances suggesting present incapacity, including relevant changes after an earlier examination. The medication and other circumstances therefore bear on the questions the proper doctrines ask; they are neither automatic proof of incompetence nor matters conclusively answered by older reports. Medina leaves that inquiry duty intact, and the entered Riggins decision does not equate medication with incapacity. The reviewing court must also consider whether any necessary retrospective determination can reliably be made and whether each relinquishment was informed and voluntary. The unraised Teague defense is not introduced here. The conditional-writ direction resting on the higher standard falls; no already-reduced death sentence is restored.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Pate v. Robinson, 383 U.S.375, and Drope v. Missouri, 420 U.S.162: reasonable doubt concerning present competence triggers inquiry.
- Medina, June 22, 1992, and Riggins, May 18, 1992: their entered limited rules are preserved, not enlarged into conclusive findings here.
- Collins v. Youngblood, 497 U.S.37: the Court may decline to introduce an unraised Teague defense.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 24, the common Dusky capacity standard and independent waiver obligation govern the presented choices. Inquiry and application remain distinct; the higher-capacity basis of the Ninth Circuit’s conditional writ is rejected. No automatic validation of convictions or sentences, no restoration of the already-reduced sentence, and no new general retrospective-hearing rule follows.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Remove the conditional-writ direction based on the higher standard; consider the existing inquiry and actual-waiver claims under the proper doctrines and pre-AEDPA treatment of state findings. Blackmun and Stevens would preserve conditional relief. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Heller v. Doe — 1993-06-24

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Heller_v_Doe_merits_1993-06-24.md).

#### The lower overall proof protection lacks a rational relation to the disadvantage imposed

**Holding and operative rule:** Kentucky’s clear-and-convincing burden for involuntary commitment of adults with intellectual disability, compared with proof beyond a reasonable doubt for mental illness, violates equal protection on the preserved rational-basis challenge: the offered diagnostic, prediction and treatment differences do not rationally justify assigning this class the greater risk of mistaken confinement. Addington’s federal minimum remains unchanged; the remedy must address lawful equalization and severability rather than impose a national reasonable-doubt standard or order every class member released.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statutory distinction must relate to the particular procedural disadvantage. Cleburne permits relevant distinctions between conditions but does not allow a label to substitute for that relationship. A longer history may make diagnosis or some predictions easier. Commitment, however, also requires dangerousness, likely treatment benefit and a least restrictive placement; the interests at stake remain physical liberty and protection from erroneous confinement. Addington explains the allocation of that error risk and supplies a constitutional minimum, not immunity from an equality challenge when a State provides greater protection to another class. The asserted difference in the intrusiveness of treatment likewise does not adequately explain the lower overall safeguard against confinement. Beach does not demand formal findings or perfect empirical fit, and the Court imposes neither requirement. It rejects the proffered relationship, not legislative generalization as such. The appropriate limited remedy must respect severability and the State’s lawful choices, while every continuing confinement still needs an independent lawful basis.

**Precedent treatment:**

- City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, 473 U.S.432: rational relationship to the actual disadvantage required; no new scrutiny tier adopted.
- Addington v. Texas, 441 U.S.418: the clear-and-convincing minimum survives; unequal safeguards remain separately reviewable.
- FCC v. Beach Communications, June 1, 1993: no formal-findings or empirical-certainty requirement imposed; its specific economic justification does not decide this classification.
- Foucha, May 18, 1992: current lawful confinement predicates preserved; noncontrolling equal-protection reasoning is not promoted to law.

#### Family-party participation has a distinct rational basis

**Holding and operative rule:** Kentucky may distinguish the two commitment proceedings by allowing guardians and immediate family members to participate as parties for adults with a lifelong developmental disability. Continuing family knowledge rationally supports that participation; the availability of testimony or a less extensive form of participation does not by itself defeat ordinary rational-basis review.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that gathering useful information is different from assigning a person less protection against erroneous confinement. A developmental condition and sustained family involvement plausibly give relatives and guardians knowledge of abilities, daily functioning and available care that assists the proceeding. Parham recognizes the value of family information without giving a relative authority to decide whether an adult should be confined. Cleburne requires a rational relationship, and that informational relationship supports the participation distinction here. Kentucky need not show that every family has useful knowledge or that no less extensive procedure could work. Nor does the existence of mental illness within some families eliminate the permissible general distinction. The Court does not presume that family members always share the adult’s interests. Their preferences or convenience establish none of the statutory grounds for confinement, and the independent adjudicator must evaluate their evidence under the lawful burden. This holding sustains party participation as a category, not every application of it.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cleburne, 473 U.S.432: relevant differences may support a particular procedural distinction.
- Parham v. J.R., 442 U.S.584: family information may be useful; adult confinement is not delegated to relatives.
- Schweiker v. Wilson, 450 U.S.221: ordinary review does not require the least restrictive legislative alternative.

#### Party participation does not itself deprive the adult of fair adjudication

**Holding and operative rule:** Allowing relatives or guardians to participate as parties does not categorically violate procedural due process where the adult has counsel and an effective opportunity to contest adverse evidence before an independent decisionmaker. Relatives may neither supply a substitute for the statutory commitment grounds nor control the decision or suppress the adult’s position.

**Authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the challenged party status must be examined within the actual procedural safeguards. Mathews requires consideration of the private interest, the risk of error and value of safeguards, and the government’s interests. The liberty interest is substantial. Kentucky nevertheless provides counsel, professional examination, an opportunity to testify and present evidence, cross-examination and independent adjudication. Relevant family information can improve accuracy when tested through those procedures. The possibility of adverse family interests requires such testing; it does not establish that adding a party inherently makes an accurate decision impossible. Parham does not permit the State to call an adult’s involuntary placement voluntary merely because a relative requested it. The adjudicator must find the statutory grounds under the lawful burden, regardless of the family’s preference. Proceedings that defeat effective contest or independent decisionmaking remain challengeable. The Court reverses the categorical due-process invalidation without determining that every class member’s hearing was fair.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Mathews v. Eldridge, 424 U.S.319: private interest, error risk and safeguards, and government interests applied separately from equality review.
- Parham, 442 U.S.584: informational participation distinguished from final decisional control.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 24, the proof distinction is invalid under rational-basis review, with severability and lawful equalization left for remand. The family distinction and party status survive the categorical challenges subject to independent adjudication and effective contest. Addington, the substantive confinement predicates and the unresolved heightened-scrutiny question remain distinct. Kentucky’s repealed voluntary-admission rule and other unreviewed grounds are not decided.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Determine severability and lawful state choices to equalize safeguards; no national reasonable-doubt rule or blanket release. Party participation may continue with counsel, effective contest and independent adjudication. Blackmun, Stevens and Souter would invalidate on equal protection and do not reach the independent due-process question. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — 1993-06-24

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Buckley_v_Fitzsimmons_merits_1993-06-24.md).

#### Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** A prosecutor who performs ordinary investigative work to obtain evidence identifying a perpetrator before probable cause has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for alleged fabrication in that function; later courtroom use does not transform the earlier work into advocacy. Genuine evaluation and preparation in an advocate’s role remain protected, and neither all pre-indictment acts nor all post-indictment acts receive a categorical classification.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the prosecutor must justify immunity for the function actually performed. Imbler protects initiating and presenting a prosecution and genuine preparation for those acts. Burns, Forrester and the entered Antoine decision reject immunity based merely on official title or an activity’s importance to litigation. The complaint describes a joint effort with detectives to secure a boot identification during the preliminary investigation of an unsolved crime. On those allegations the prosecutors were searching for evidence needed to build a case, not preparing an established advocate’s presentation. Their later use of the evidence before a grand jury or at trial does not change the earlier function. The competing view correctly observes that advocacy can include out-of-court preparation, but that principle does not establish the protected function alleged here. The Court does not decide qualified immunity, ultimate constitutional injury or damages. Unreviewed immunity for the actual in-court presentations remains undisturbed, and unresolved allegations about paid or coerced witness statements require initial treatment below.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S.409: protected advocacy and genuine preparation preserved; ordinary investigation distinguished.
- Burns v. Reed, 500 U.S.478: function-specific limits applied to investigation.
- Forrester v. White, 484 U.S.219, and Antoine, June 7, 1993: function and claimant’s burden control; importance to litigation is insufficient.

#### A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity

**Holding and operative rule:** A prosecutor’s statements to the press announcing an indictment have no absolute prosecutorial immunity merely because they concern a pending prosecution or cause injury through later judicial proceedings. The ordinary qualified-immunity inquiry and the requirement of an actionable constitutional deprivation remain separate.

**Authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that informing the public is not the judicial advocacy function protected by Imbler. The challenged press conference neither initiates a prosecution in court nor presents the State’s case to an adjudicator. Burns requires historical and functional support for absolute immunity, and none supports this out-of-court press function. The Seventh Circuit’s focus on whether injury depends on a later judge’s decision confuses the merits of a damages claim with immunity for the challenged act. A prosecutor’s title cannot convert public statements into judicial advocacy when other executive officials making comparable statements receive no such absolute protection. Tower also forecloses creating a new immunity simply because the Court considers it desirable policy. Rejecting absolute immunity does not establish that defamation alone violates the Constitution, that these statements caused compensable harm, or that qualified immunity is unavailable. Those questions remain for the appropriate proceedings after reversal of the categorical immunity ruling.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Imbler, 424 U.S.409, and Burns, 500 U.S.478: press activity lacks the protected advocacy function.
- Tower v. Glover, 467 U.S.914: policy alone cannot create a new §1983 absolute immunity.
- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S.800, and Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S.635: separate qualified-immunity law left unchanged.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 24, the pleaded investigative fabrication and press statements lack absolute prosecutorial immunity. Genuine advocacy preparation and unreviewed in-court presentation immunity survive. No constitutional liability, damages award, witness-immunity change or qualified-immunity revision results.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Remand for remaining elements and defenses; allegations are not findings. No absolute prosecutorial immunity for the press function; constitutional injury and other defenses remain unresolved. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Shalala v. Schaefer — 1993-06-24

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Shalala_v_Schaefer_merits_1993-06-24.md).

#### Sentence-four judgment ends the action and confers prevailing status

**Holding and operative rule:** A sentence-four judgment reversing the Secretary’s denial of benefits with a remand ends the §405(g) civil action and makes the successful claimant a prevailing party; the court must enter judgment rather than retain the merits action pending an administrative award. Sentence-six remands instead retain jurisdiction for the statutorily specified proceedings and later return to court.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the statute supplies distinct remand routes with different procedural consequences. Sentence four authorizes a judgment affirming, modifying or reversing the Secretary’s decision, with or without remand. Finkelstein and Melkonyan give effect to that judgment and distinguish it from sentence six, which allows a pre-answer remand on the Secretary’s good-cause motion or a remand for new material evidence with good cause for its earlier omission. A claimant who obtains reversal through sentence four has secured judicial relief, not merely a favorable statement during ongoing litigation. Garland’s prevailing-party rule, consistent with the entered Farrar eligibility holding, therefore applies without waiting for the agency to award benefits. That status does not establish every EAJA requirement or the reasonable amount. Administrative success cannot replace the court judgment as the statutory filing event. The Court preserves the different sentence-six sequence, including return to court and a final judgment after the further proceedings.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Sullivan v. Finkelstein, 496 U.S.617, and Melkonyan v. Sullivan, 501 U.S.89: the two statutory remand routes applied.
- Texas State Teachers Assn. v. Garland Independent School District, 489 U.S.782, and entered Farrar, December 14, 1992: enforceable judicial relief establishes prevailing status, not automatic fee amount.

#### The missing separate judgment prevents the fee deadline from expiring

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the then-effective rules, EAJA’s thirty-day application period follows a properly entered judgment and expiration of the applicable sixty-day appeal period; the absence of the required Rule 58 separate judgment prevents that deadline from expiring. Schaefer’s application is timely although the sentence-four remand was itself a final merits disposition.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that substantive finality and the formal entry starting appellate time must not be collapsed. Section 2412(d) measures its thirty days from a judgment that is final and no longer appealable. Rule 4(a) gives sixty days to appeal in this action involving a federal officer and requires entry in compliance with Rule 58. The district court’s April 4, 1989 remand resolved the judicial merits but lacked the separate judgment document. Indrelunas gives that formality effect. Mallis permits an appeal without insisting on the document in appropriate circumstances; it does not deem the unstarted appeal period expired against a party seeking fees. The Secretary therefore cannot establish untimeliness from the remand date or the later administrative decision. Proper entry remains necessary where still absent. This conclusion sustains the award reviewed here without importing a later deemed-entry deadline, enlarging recoverable services or reopening EAJA issues outside the appeal.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Indrelunas, 411 U.S.216: separate-document requirement applied as then in force.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Mallis, 435 U.S.381: ability to take an appeal without the document distinguished from expiration of appellate time.
- Melkonyan, 501 U.S.89: final court judgment, not agency decision, governs the EAJA clock.

#### Hudson does not preserve administrative-fee coverage after an ordinary terminated sentence-four action

**Holding and operative rule:** Hudson’s integrated administrative-proceeding fee rationale does not apply to a sentence-four remand properly entered without retained jurisdiction, or to retention of jurisdiction that is challenged. It remains applicable to qualifying sentence-six proceedings in which the court retains the action, subject to EAJA’s other requirements.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Hudson must be read with the statutory structure clarified in Finkelstein and Melkonyan. Hudson involved a remand treated, without objection, as part of a continuing judicial action. Its fee rationale depended on that continuing jurisdiction and the administrative work’s relationship to the judicial case. Sentence four, however, requires a terminating judgment; a court cannot preserve the action simply to postpone the fee clock until benefits are awarded. The argument that effective representation on remand favors broader coverage identifies a policy concern but does not supply authority to retain an action the statute ends. The qualifying sentence-six route remains different because the Secretary returns to the court after further proceedings. The holding neither eliminates Hudson in that setting nor awards new administrative fees in this appeal. Prevailing status after a sentence-four reversal and entitlement to compensation for later administrative work remain separate questions.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Sullivan v. Hudson, 490 U.S.877: retained-jurisdiction rationale limited in light of the statutory distinction; sentence-six coverage preserved.
- Finkelstein, 496 U.S.617, and Melkonyan, 501 U.S.89: sentence-four termination controls; statutory retention is reserved for sentence six.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 24, sentence-four reversal/remand confers prevailing status and ends the action, while then-required separate judgment entry and expiration of appellate time govern EAJA timeliness. The Hudson limitation has six direct votes. No later Rule58 deemed-entry provision or universal administrative-fee entitlement is adopted.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Application was timely; sustain the reviewed award and complete proper judgment entry where still necessary. No new administrative-service award or reopening of unreviewed fee questions. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### United States v. Edge Broadcasting Co. — 1993-06-25

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/United_States_v_Edge_Broadcasting_Co_merits_1993-06-25.md).

#### Meaningful reduction and reasonable fit in a border broadcaster’s application

**Holding and operative rule:** Assuming truthful advertising of lawful activity, the licensing-State lottery restriction satisfies Central Hudson as applied to Edge because it meaningfully reduces lottery promotion reaching North Carolina residents and reasonably accommodates the different policies of lottery and nonlottery States. Complete exclusion of cross-border advertising is unnecessary, but the actual advancement and reasonable-fit requirements remain; no categorical exception for vice advertising or bar on as-applied challenges follows.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the local effect of this restriction is substantial enough to justify its application. Edge obtains most listeners and advertising revenue from Virginia, but it also accounts for eleven percent of radio listening in the affected North Carolina counties. Excluding lottery promotion from that listening time materially serves the federal accommodation of North Carolina’s policy. Central Hudson requires advancement of the asserted interest; it does not require complete eradication of advertising from every other source. Fox requires reasonable fit and therefore permits consideration of an administrable licensing line connected to the community served. Discovery Network involved a distinction unrelated to the physical harms asserted there, while this distinction tracks differing state lottery policies. Edenfield’s requirement of real support remains satisfied by this audience record. The Virginia audience’s lawful interest is weighed rather than ignored. The Court does not decide whether merely marginal advancement would suffice, whether gambling advertisements lack protection categorically, or any unresolved alternative equality claim.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission,447 U.S.557: four-part scrutiny applied, with the first inquiry assumed in Edge’s favor.
- Board of Trustees v. Fox,492 U.S.469: reasonable fit applied; no least-restrictive-means requirement.
- Entered Discovery Network, March24, and Edenfield, April26,1993: unrelated classifications and unsupported harms distinguished, their scrutiny retained.
- Posadas,478 U.S.328: no overruling and no adoption of the greater-power/lesser-power argument.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 25, Edge supplies a five-Justice record-specific Central Hudson application. Actual meaningful reduction and reasonable fit control; no majority establishes a no-as-applied-review rule, categorical vice exception or automatic permission to suppress speech for federalism alone.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Remove the First Amendment ground; any genuinely unresolved preserved equal-protection or other alternative claim remains below. No facial approval of every application. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### TXO Production Corp. v. Alliance Resources — 1993-06-25

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/TXO_Production_Corp_v_Alliance_Resources_merits_1993-06-25.md).

#### The particular judicial review is constitutionally sufficient

**Holding and operative rule:** The jury process followed by the trial judge’s hearing and review and the state supreme court’s review of this punitive award satisfy procedural due process on this record. The trial judge’s failure to provide a separate written explanation does not by itself invalidate the award; this decision supplies neither a universal procedural checklist nor a substantive permissible ratio.

**Authority:** Stevens’s plurality procedural discussion, expressly agreeing on this proposition with Scalia’s concurrence joined by Thomas, this proposition; Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Stevens, Blackmun and Kennedy adopt the procedural discussion; Scalia and Thomas expressly concur that the instructed jury and actual judicial reasonableness review provided here satisfy due process. This common case-specific proposition has five votes; no formal whole-opinion join or Marks substantive rule is inferred.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds, at the level shared by five Justices, that the process actually afforded this award is sufficient. Haslip identifies meaningful judicial review as a safeguard against arbitrary punitive awards without prescribing its Alabama procedures as the only constitutional form. Here the trial judge heard the parties’ postverdict arguments and expressed agreement with the appraisal of deliberate misconduct. The state supreme court examined the punitive challenge, the relevant precedents and the conduct, threatened harm and deterrence considerations. Its colorful terminology does not eliminate that review. The opposing view fairly questions the sparse explanation and the risk that wealth displaced wrongdoing, but the absence of a written trial ruling alone does not establish a constitutional denial of process. This shared procedural conclusion does not settle the separate disagreement about substantive excessiveness, adopt the plurality’s potential-harm estimates, approve every instruction as a reusable model, or determine that any later award with a similar ratio is lawful.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Haslip,499 U.S.1: actual procedural safeguards applied; no mandatory reproduction of every Alabama feature.
- Garnes v. Fleming Landfill,186 W.Va.656,413 S.E.2d897: state review framework examined as part of this record, not transformed into a federal checklist.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 25, the judgment sustains this award by five votes. Five expressly support the adequacy of the particular process, but no majority adopts potential-harm arithmetic, Kennedy’s entire substantive test, Scalia’s rejection of substantive review, a ratio or an instruction-waiver rule. Haslip’s existing governing force remains; no later punitive-damages framework enters.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. The $10 million award stands. Stone/White/O’Connor/Souter would vacate the punitive component for constitutionally adequate review; O’Connor and White additionally favor a new punitive trial on the bias ground. No reopening of $19,000 compensation, title ownership or state-law tort liability. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### St. Mary's Honor Center v. Hicks — 1993-06-25

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/St_Mary_s_Honor_Center_v_Hicks_merits_1993-06-25.md).

#### Pretext alone does not command judgment, and circumstantial evidence need not be supplemented by a special category of proof

**Holding and operative rule:** After an employer produces admissible evidence of nondiscriminatory reasons, a proven prima-facie case and rejection of the offered explanations do not automatically command judgment for the employee. Those circumstances may support a finding of intentional discrimination without a mandatory additional category of direct or independent proof; the Eighth Circuit’s automatic-liability mandate is vacated, while no majority here adopts a replacement persuasion allocation.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the appellate mandate cannot stand merely because the employer’s reasons were disbelieved. Title VII addresses the prohibited cause of an employment decision, not false explanation as an independent statutory wrong. McDonnell Douglas structures indirect proof, and Burdine requires the employer to produce admissible evidence rather than persuade at the ordinary explanation stage. That structure does not justify eliminating every opportunity to consider whether discrimination actually caused the action. At the same time, Aikens recognizes the practical force of circumstantial proof: a prima-facie showing and rejection of the employer’s material account may establish discrimination without eyewitness testimony or a separate kind of evidence. The Court therefore removes the compulsory-liability direction and remands. Five Justices do not agree on a new allocation after established pretext. The inherited rules remain governing to the extent not displaced by this narrow holding; neither Stone’s persuasion shift nor the dissent’s automatic-result rule gains controlling force. No finding that personal hostility actually motivated these decisions is made.

**Precedent treatment:**

- McDonnell Douglas Corp. v. Green,411 U.S.792: indirect framework retained; no automatic liability holding adopted.
- Texas Department of Community Affairs v. Burdine,450 U.S.248: production stage retained; no five-Justice replacement of its persuasion rule.
- Postal Service Board of Governors v. Aikens,460 U.S.711: actual discrimination and circumstantial evidence remain central.
- Entered Hazen, April20,1993: prohibited motive remains distinct from general unfairness; no added evidence category follows.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 25, the automatic-liability mandate is removed and the narrow five-Justice holding governs. Stone’s proposed trigger, shifted burden and extra remedial instructions remain published noncontrolling positions. No majority changes Burdine’s persuasion allocation or establishes a universal Title VII/§1983 equivalence. The appellate court proceeds under existing governing law consistent with the limited mandate; the Court does not decide clear error, retaliation, remaining defenses, relief or applicability of the 1991 Act.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** The appellate liability mandate is vacated and the matter returns to the Eighth Circuit. Existing district findings are not independently erased by this Court; review and any further proceedings follow governing law and the narrow mandate. The previously unaddressed retaliation issue remains for proper consideration below, not resolved by silence. The unappealed §1981 dismissal is unchanged. No Supreme Court docket component remains pending; ordinary mandate procedure applies.

#### Shaw v. Reno — 1993-06-28

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Shaw_v_Reno_merits_1993-06-28.md).

#### Territorial racial design alone does not state the asserted deprivation

**Holding and operative rule:** Alleged deliberate or predominant consideration of race in drawing territorial constituencies, even with departures from ordinary districting criteria, does not by itself state this equal-protection claim where no racial exclusion, intentional dilution, materially unequal participation, legally inferior civic membership or other independently cognizable constitutional deprivation is alleged. This is a substantive disposition of the design-only theory, not heightened pleading, a universal requirement of additional downstream loss, or an added constitutional-injury prerequisite for an independent statutory claim.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the alleged design alone does not deny these voters equal membership in their territorial constituency. UJO supports the limited distinction between racial awareness and electoral discrimination, although its separate rationales establish no comprehensive test. Croson protects each person against racial allocation of competitive opportunities; statewide racial strength therefore cannot answer a personal deprivation. Here the alleged demographic design does not itself prescribe racial eligibility or limit the representative’s constituency to one race. Anderson forbids official racial ballot cues, and Gomillion forbids boundaries used to expel a racial population from municipal voting; both remain enforceable without this theory. The contrary view reasonably identifies a danger of racial stereotyping, but that danger does not make every deliberate demographic boundary an official rule of unequal membership. The Court accepts the allegations rather than inventing benign geography. Conley supplies ordinary pleading rules, while the asserted substantive theory fails. No finding of federal compulsion, final compliance with other law, or right to a particular racial composition follows.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United Jewish Organizations v. Carey, 430 U.S.144: its fractured reasoning supports a limited distinction, not a comprehensive controlling test or a statewide-white-representation defense.
- Wright v. Rockefeller, 376 U.S.52: distinguished; racial purpose was not established there, so it did not decide the present assumed-purpose question.
- City of Richmond v. Croson, 488 U.S.469, and Shelley v. Kraemer, 334 U.S.1: personal equality retained; the Court declines to equate territorial design alone with Croson’s racial allocation of opportunities.
- Anderson v. Martin, 375 U.S.399: official racial ballot labeling remains prohibited without proof that an election winner changed.
- Gomillion v. Lightfoot, 364 U.S.339: racial municipal disenfranchisement remains prohibited; Whittaker’s broader concurrence is not adopted as a rule covering every racial district adjustment.
- White v. Regester, 412 U.S.755, and McLaurin v. Oklahoma State Regents, 339 U.S.637: impaired participation may be unconstitutional despite formal access; no universal downstream-loss requirement.
- Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S.41; entered Leatherman, March 3, 1993: ordinary notice pleading retained, no demand for expert proof or a replacement map.
- Entered Growe and Voinovich: statutory prerequisites, state primacy and unresolved coalition/influence questions remain distinct; no additional constitutional-injury gate imposed on § 2.

#### Federal preclearance dismissal remains separate

**Holding and operative rule:** The district court outside the District of Columbia cannot use this action to review the Attorney General’s preclearance determination under the Voting Rights Act; § 14(b)’s designated channel and Morris’s restriction on review leave dismissal of the federal defendants undisturbed. That disposition does not immunize the State’s plan from an otherwise cognizable constitutional or statutory challenge.

**Authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court leaves the federal defendants’ dismissal undisturbed on grounds independent of the state claim. Section 14(b) channels actions restraining execution or enforcement of the Voting Rights Act to the District Court for the District of Columbia. Morris explains why the Attorney General’s preclearance determination is not an ordinary reviewable administrative adjudication and why substantive challenges to a state plan remain separate. The plaintiffs cannot obtain review of that determination in this North Carolina action by describing their objection as one to the resulting map. Conversely, preserving the federal dismissal says nothing about whether the State’s plan satisfies every independent constitutional or statutory command. Beer’s nonretrogression principle does not compel maximization of majority-minority districts, and preclearance is no universal certificate of legality. Because the designated channel and nonreviewability rules resolve this component, the Court does not decide an alternative merits defense for the federal officials or direct them to approve different boundaries.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Morris v. Gressette, 432 U.S.491: restriction on reviewing the Attorney General’s preclearance determination applied; independent challenges to the state enactment remain available under their own rules.
- Beer v. United States, 425 U.S.130: nonretrogression distinguished from maximization or certification of compliance with every law.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 28, a five-Justice Court rejects this territorial racial-design-only constitutional theory. Independent exclusion, dilution, unequal participation, civic-status and statutory claims retain their own elements. No generally applicable replacement for Croson or Metro Broadcasting, predominance scrutiny rule, coalition/crossover entitlement, or requirement to prove federal compulsion enters. Federal-official dismissal rests on the separate channel. No existing simulated Shaw holding is overruled.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. No injunction, new map or trial on this rejected theory; other properly presented claims and any properly sought amendment remain governed by ordinary procedural and preclusion rules. The separate statutory review channel and nonreviewability ground remain; no federal merits injunction. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### United States v. Dixon — 1993-06-28

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/United_States_v_Dixon_merits_1993-06-28.md).

#### Nonsummary punitive contempt triggers double-jeopardy protection

**Holding and operative rule:** A completed nonsummary criminal-contempt prosecution places the accused in criminal jeopardy; private initiation and an order’s protective purpose do not categorically remove that protection. Whether a later prosecution is for the same offense remains a separate inquiry; coercive civil sanctions, summary courtroom contempt and separate sovereigns are not decided.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that punishment for violating a court order can implicate the protection against renewed criminal prosecution. Bloom recognizes criminal contempt as a crime for procedural protection, while Gompers distinguishes a fixed punitive sanction from a civil sanction avoidable through compliance. Foster underwent criminal adjudication, including acquittals and imprisonment, under the court’s authority. The fact that his wife’s counsel initiated enforcement does not make that completed punitive trial a private civil remedy. The Government’s institutional distinction identifies why an order matters, but cannot eliminate the constitutional character of the punishment already imposed. Young recognizes the judiciary’s authority to enforce its orders; that authority does not itself answer the separate identity-of-offenses question. The Court does not impair prospective enforcement of protective orders or decide every civil or summary contempt setting. The later counts must therefore be examined under the applicable double-jeopardy rules, with their different outcomes and rationales stated separately.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Bloom v. Illinois, 391 U.S.194: criminal character of punitive contempt applied.
- Gompers v. Bucks Stove & Range Co., 221 U.S.418: punitive/coercive distinction applied.
- Young v. United States ex rel. Vuitton, 481 U.S.787: independent judicial enforcement authority retained, not a categorical exception from criminal protection.

#### Previously prosecuted necessary offenses cannot be tried again

**Holding and operative rule:** When a criminal-contempt adjudication necessarily prosecutes the substantive offense forbidden by the order, the Government cannot prosecute that same substantive offense again. Dixon’s drug offense and Foster I’s November assault were necessarily prosecuted in the respective contempts and are barred; this shared proposition does not supply a common analysis of Foster II–V.

**Authority:** White’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Government cannot retry the substantive offenses already necessarily established in these contempts. Brown protects against successive prosecution for greater and included offenses, and Harris prevents an abstract description of a greater offense from concealing the substantive crime necessarily prosecuted within it. Dixon’s order required compliance with the criminal law, and his contempt adjudication established the same possession with intent to distribute later charged. Foster’s November assault similarly supplied the offense prosecuted and punished under his protective order. The existence and knowledge of the order do not erase the previously prosecuted substantive crime. The opposing abstract-elements view gives insufficient effect to the necessary content of these particular charges. The six Justices supporting this rule differ on the broader comparison for additional elements and on Grady’s application, but agree directly on this limited protection. No other contempt conviction is vacated, and the Court does not resolve the remaining counts by treating every act in a single episode as one offense.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown v. Ohio, 432 U.S.161: successive greater/included-offense protection applied.
- Harris v. Oklahoma, 433 U.S.682: necessary substantive offense cannot be erased by an abstract description of the first charge.
- Blockburger v. United States, 284 U.S.299: elements inquiry retained; disagreement over its broader application separately recorded.

#### Grady remains governing successive-prosecution law

**Holding and operative rule:** Grady is not overruled: beyond Blockburger’s elements protection, a later prosecution is barred when establishing an essential element requires proof of conduct constituting an offense for which the defendant has already been prosecuted. Mere reuse of evidence or a common episode does not suffice, and Felix’s independently punishable conspiracy holding remains; no five-Justice rationale determines Grady’s application to Foster II–V in this fractured decision.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court retains the distinction between another prosecution of already prosecuted criminal conduct and the reuse of evidence about that conduct. Grady states that distinction, while Felix applies it to a separate conspiracy agreement and rejects a same-evidence rule. The Government’s concern about administering the conduct inquiry does not establish that this recent protection has become unworkable. Brown’s concern with finality after trial and the successive-prosecution line examined in Vitale support maintaining protection beyond a purely formal comparison of statutory language. The contrary proposal would discard Grady rather than merely clarify Felix; five Justices decline that change. Retention does not make all charges arising from one episode inseparable. Blackmun’s distinct view of contempt as vindication of judicial authority prevents a majority from applying this retained rule to bar Foster’s remaining counts, while four other Justices would overrule it. That fractured application must not be transformed into either an overruling or a new universally controlling contempt exception. Ashe remains separately available where an acquittal necessarily resolved an issue.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Grady v. Corbin, 495 U.S.508: expressly retained; no overruling by this decision.
- Entered United States v. Felix, March 25, 1992: conspiracy and evidentiary-overlap distinctions preserved, not recast as an overruling.
- Illinois v. Vitale, 447 U.S.410: successive-prosecution concern beyond formal elements supports retention.
- Brown v. Ohio, 432 U.S.161: finality and greater/included-offense protection retained.
- Ashe v. Swenson, 397 U.S.436: independent issue-preclusion rule preserved for the remanded threat charges.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 28, nonsummary criminal contempt receives criminal-jeopardy protection, and Dixon/Foster I are barred 6–3. Grady survives by five votes. Foster II–V may proceed under a 5–4 judgment, subject to Ashe where applicable, but no majority adopts either the four-Justice Blockburger-only/overruling view or Blackmun’s special judicial-order distinction. These are incompatible rationales, not logical subsets; no Marks synthesis creates a general contempt exception or overrules Grady. Later courts retain the existing rule and the case-specific judgment without inventing a five-vote test.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Substantive indictment remains dismissed; original contempt conviction undisturbed. No renewed trial on this simple-assault count. Prosecution may proceed subject to any properly established Ashe issue-preclusion bar from the actual acquittals and other preserved defenses; no finding resolving those issues here. This distinct charged offense may proceed under the judgment; no authorization for a constitutionally barred simple-assault conviction or displacement of the original contempt disposition. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals — 1993-06-28

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Daubert_v_Merrell_Dow_Pharmaceuticals_merits_1993-06-28.md).

#### The enacted Rules displace mandatory general acceptance

**Holding and operative rule:** Frye’s mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite does not govern admission of scientific expert evidence in federal trials under the Federal Rules of Evidence. Acceptance remains potentially relevant under Rule 702, but its absence does not alone require exclusion and its presence does not dispense with the Rules’ other requirements.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the enacted Rules supply the federal admissibility standard. Rule 402 generally admits relevant evidence subject to applicable exceptions, and Rule 702 addresses qualified expert knowledge that assists the factfinder without imposing a consensus condition. Beech Aircraft requires interpretation of these Rules as enacted law, while Abel permits compatible common-law guidance rather than a competing prerequisite. Bourjaily illustrates that an incompatible earlier doctrine does not survive merely because it was familiar before the Rules. Frye’s rigid condition therefore cannot exclude a proffer simply because it is new, unpublished or disputed within the relevant community. The contrary assertion that the Rules silently preserved Frye finds no support in their text or the identified drafting history. This displacement does not require admission of unsupported expertise, resolve the children’s causation claims, or establish that every scientific disagreement belongs before a jury. The Court vacates the judgment based on the wrong screening rule and leaves the actual proffers for proper evaluation below.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Frye v. United States, 293 F.1013: displaced as a mandatory prerequisite in federal trials; not a Supreme Court Rule 702 holding being overruled.
- Beech Aircraft v. Rainey, 488 U.S.153: statutory interpretation of enacted Rules applied.
- United States v. Abel, 469 U.S.45: compatible common-law guidance retained, incompatible prerequisite rejected.
- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S.171: Rules supersede incompatible common law.

#### Reliable scientific foundation and fit require flexible preliminary screening

**Holding and operative rule:** Under original Rules 104(a) and 702, the proponent must establish by a preponderance the preliminary foundation for scientifically reliable knowledge that fits the disputed factual question and will assist the factfinder. The judge examines principles, methodology and valid application using relevant nonexclusive considerations—testability and testing, peer review/publication, meaningful error information and operational standards, and acceptance—without making any one consideration an invariant prerequisite; admissibility does not determine ultimate causation or sufficiency.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that scientific knowledge requires more than credentials or unsupported assertion. Rule 702’s knowledge and assistance requirements demand a defensible scientific foundation and a valid connection to the offered inference. Bourjaily supplies the preliminary preponderance burden under Rule 104(a), distinct from the substantive trial burden. Testing, peer scrutiny, error information, standards and acceptance can illuminate reliability, but publication is neither indispensable nor conclusive. A method valid for one purpose may not support another: the lower courts must examine the link between the offered studies or reanalysis and the human-causation proposition. The contrary concern that judges must become final arbiters of scientific truth mistakes preliminary admissibility for ultimate factfinding. Cross-examination and contrary evidence remain appropriate for shaky but admissible proof; they do not replace the judge’s screening duty. Rules 403, 703 and 706 and the ordinary judgment rules remain available. The Court neither decides which experts qualify here nor extends this scientific discussion to every technical specialty, legislative fact or appellate review question.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S.171, 175–176: preponderance for preliminary admissibility applied, not a substantive causation burden.
- Rock v. Arkansas, 483 U.S.44: adversarial testing remains appropriate for admissible evidence; no excuse for unsupported foundation.
- United States v. Downing, 753 F.2d 1224: helpful fit and flexible reliability discussion informs interpretation, not a binding Supreme Court multifactor checklist.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 28, Frye’s federal consensus prerequisite is displaced 9–0; reliability/fit and flexible preliminary screening govern by eight direct joins. Rules 104(a)/702 burden is preponderance, not scientific certainty. No later Rule 702 text, universal technical expertise rule, appellate review standard or substantive civil rights evidence test enters.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Reevaluate material expert proffers under original Rules and reconsider summary judgment; further fair foundational submissions may be received. No finding of causation or direction to admit all experts. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Austin v. United States — 1993-06-28

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Austin_v_United_States_merits_1993-06-28.md).

#### These statutory forfeitures are covered fines

**Holding and operative rule:** Forfeiture to the United States under 21 U.S.C.§§ 881(a)(4) and(a)(7) constitutes payment in kind to the sovereign at least partly as punishment and is subject to the Excessive Fines Clause. Its civil designation, proceeding against property, and concurrent remedial aims do not remove coverage; coverage does not establish excessiveness or import all criminal-procedure safeguards.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that these statutory takings cannot be characterized as solely remedial. Browning-Ferris distinguishes private awards from governmental extraction; here the United States prosecutes the action and receives the property. The statutory connection to drug offenses and the separate owner defenses make culpability relevant, while deterrence and punishment are evident in the provisions’ operation. Halper recognizes that civil proceedings can serve punitive purposes, but its distinct double-jeopardy accounting analysis is not the test adopted here. The Government’s instrumentality argument does not make ordinary lawful property equivalent to contraband: Plymouth Sedan rejects that equivalence for a vehicle. Nor do widely varying property values function simply as calibrated reimbursement of enforcement expenses. The Court therefore rejects a categorical civil-forfeiture immunity from the Clause. It does not decide every historical forfeiture, a constitutional innocent-owner defense, taxation or compensatory recovery, state incorporation, or the ultimate amount that may lawfully be taken from Austin.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Browning-Ferris v. Kelco Disposal, 492 U.S.257: government prosecution/receipt distinguishes this fine from private punitive award.
- United States v. Halper, 490 U.S.435: partly punitive function recognized by analogy; separate Double Jeopardy analysis not imported.
- One 1958 Plymouth Sedan v. Pennsylvania, 380 U.S.693: lawful property is not contraband merely because used in crime.
- One Lot Emerald Cut Stones v. United States, 409 U.S.232, and United States v. One Assortment of 89 Firearms, 465 U.S.354: remedial customs/contraband characterizations distinguished, not abolished.
- Entered Buena Vista, February 24, 1993: statutory owner defenses remain provision-specific; no § 881(a)(7) or constitutional innocent owner holding imported.

#### Historical in rem form does not negate punishment

**Holding and operative rule:** The historical treatment of statutory in rem forfeiture includes punishment and does not establish categorical exemption from the Excessive Fines Clause for these forfeitures. The traditionally limited common-law innocent-owner defense does not prove that forfeiture was solely remedial; the constitutionality of forfeiting a truly innocent owner’s property remains unresolved.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that historical forfeiture practice supplies no categorical civil-property exception. Peisch described forfeiture as punishment of an owner and declined to treat unauthorized removal by strangers as the owner’s forfeiting act. The Palmyra allowed condemnation without a prior personal conviction, but did not thereby establish a purely remedial purpose. Goldsmith-Grant and Calero-Toledo connect traditional forfeiture to negligence, care in entrusting property, and deterrence, while reserving serious questions involving a truly innocent owner. These authorities answer the argument that the property’s formal status as defendant necessarily excludes punishment of its owner. The history permits concurrent remedial purposes; it does not require their absence before a sanction can be punitive in part. The actual drug forfeiture provisions reinforce that understanding through their offense predicates and express owner protections. The Court leaves the reserved constitutional innocence question unresolved and does not turn every historical statement into a rule that all governmental acquisition of property is a fine.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Peisch v. Ware, 4 Cranch 347: punitive understanding andstranger-removal qualification applied.
- The Palmyra, 12 Wheat 1: no prior personal conviction required, not an absence of punishment holding.
- J.W. Goldsmith, Jr.-Grant Co. v. United States, 254 U.S.505, and Calero-Toledo v. Pearson Yacht, 416 U.S.663: punitive/deterrent features recognized; truly innocent owner question reserved.

#### Remand leaves excessiveness distinct from statutory connection

**Holding and operative rule:** The lower court must evaluate whether Austin’s actual forfeiture is excessive; statutory facilitation and coverage as punishment do not themselves decide that question.
