The Court adopts no comprehensive excessiveness test and does not limit the remand to instrumentality alone: the property’s connection to the offense may matter, and other relevant considerations may also be examined.

**Authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that correcting the coverage error requires further consideration of the particular forfeiture. The court below expressed concern about the penalty but believed itself barred from applying the Excessive Fines Clause, so its comments are not a completed constitutional determination on developed findings. Browning-Ferris identifies governmental punishment as the coverage concern without supplying a comprehensive measure for this forfeiture. Calero-Toledo’s account of property connected to wrongdoing likewise does not decide the separate excessiveness inquiry. The proposed exclusive focus on instrumentality may identify a relevant consideration, but the Court leaves other considerations available rather than treating any sufficient statutory connection as conclusive. First-instance examination is appropriate because neither established values nor an applied constitutional standard are before this Court. No automatic return of the properties, numerical ratio, fixed monetary cap or ruling that the forfeiture necessarily survives follows. The Government retains its opportunity to establish lawful forfeiture within the statute and the constitutional limit.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Browning-Ferris, 492 U.S.257: fine coverage principle applied without inventing a comprehensive proportionality formula.
- Calero-Toledo, 416 U.S.663: statutory/historical connection not converted into a final excessiveness answer.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 28, § 881(a)(4)/(a)(7) forfeitures are covered fines unanimously. Five adopt the historical partly punitive explanation; six expressly leave other factors available beyond instrumentality. No comprehensive excessiveness formula, mandatory ratio, automatic return, state incorporation or Double Jeopardy holding enters. Stone’s factor proposal and Scalia’s exclusive test remain noncontrolling.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Federal § 881(a)(4)/(a)(7) forfeitures are covered; no lower court final excessiveness finding adopted. Determine excessiveness on a developed record; no automatic return or cap. Scalia would confine analysis to instrumentality; the Court leaves other factors available. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Hartford Fire Insurance v. California — 1993-06-28

Authority: [Canonical Decision Record](records/Hartford_Fire_Insurance_v_California_merits_1993-06-28.md).

#### Insurance exemption follows activity rather than association

**Holding and operative rule:** Otherwise qualifying business of insurance regulated by state law does not lose McCarran-Ferguson § 2(b) antitrust protection merely because domestic insurers agree with foreign reinsurers. Whether the activity itself qualifies and is state-regulated remains essential; neither domestic nor foreign nationality establishes that condition categorically.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the exemption’s object is the activity Congress described. Royal Drug distinguishes the business of insurance from all business done by insurance companies; its discussion of agreements with outsiders does not create an entity-based contamination rule. Pireno likewise examines risk transfer, the policy relationship and whether the practice remains within the insurance industry. The alleged agreements here concededly concern the business of insurance, and foreign reinsurers are not pharmacies or other parties wholly outside that industry. Even assuming their own activities lack state regulation, the domestic activity does not automatically lose otherwise established protection through association. The contrary analogy to exemptions for designated agricultural producers overlooks different statutory language. This ruling removes one appellate rationale without foreclosing the boycott exception or deciding the actual regulatory status of every reinsurer. Fabe’s separate first-clause protection of state insurance-regulatory law remains distinct. Qualifying activity, actual regulation and applicable statutory exceptions must still be evaluated.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Group Life & Health Insurance v. Royal Drug, 440 U.S.205: activity/entity distinction applied; loose outsider analogy confined to its context.
- Union Labor Life v. Pireno, 458 U.S.119: practice-specific criteria retained.
- SEC v. National Securities, 393 U.S.453: regulation of insurance business distinguished from regulation of companies.
- Case-Swayne v. Sunkist, 389 U.S.384, and United States v. Borden, 308 U.S.188: producer/entity-based exemptions distinguished.
- Entered Fabe, June 11, 1993: separate first-clause holding not expanded into the antitrust proviso.

#### Collateral refusals distinguish boycott from agreement on terms

**Holding and operative rule:** A concerted refusal of collateral or unrelated transactions to compel desired terms in a targeted transaction can constitute a McCarran-Ferguson § 3(b) boycott; a mere joint insistence on terms of the refused transaction does not, without more, do so. The pleaded broader withdrawals permit California I–IV/Connecticut I–II to proceed, subject to proof and whether other business genuinely affects the reinsured risk; California VII/Connecticut VI allege no refusal mechanism and cannot defeat domestic exemption on the reviewed grounds.

**Authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the boycott exception must leave room for the activity exemption Congress enacted. Barry involved withholding alternative insurance to force acceptance of another insurer’s terms, while Eastern States used unrelated dealings to compel a collateral trade restriction. Those examples distinguish coercive leverage from a collective demand about the very risk or transaction offered. Reinsurance terms depend on the underlying coverage, so refusing to reinsure an objectionable primary policy is not automatically collateral. The complaints nevertheless allege broader withdrawal even from otherwise acceptable business and linked demands that may cross that boundary. McLain and Conley require crediting that permissible pleaded account at this stage, not deciding the operational facts against the plaintiffs. Defendants may show that the other business genuinely affected the proposed risk. The model umbrella/excess counts contain no corresponding refusal allegation. The Court decides neither actual conspiracy nor antitrust injury, and supplies no categorical definition of every independent coercion or intimidation claim.

**Precedent treatment:**

- St. Paul Fire&Marine v. Barry, 438 U.S.531: collateral-pressure application retained; not limited to boycotts of competitors.
- Eastern States Retail Lumber v. United States, 234 U.S.600: unrelated trade used as leverage illustrates the boundary.
- United States v. South-Eastern Underwriters, 322 U.S.533: coercive exclusion distinguished from mere agreement on terms; not abrogated.
- McLain v. Real Estate Board, 444 U.S.232, and Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S.41: then-operative pleading standard applied; no actual liability finding.

#### The asserted British permission does not bar these American-market claims

**Holding and operative rule:** The pleaded London conduct intended to produce, and producing, substantial effects on American insurance coverage may be adjudicated under the Sherman Act; British permission without an incompatible duty or another established ground requiring nonapplication here does not warrant the asserted comity dismissal. The Court reserves a universal comity rule and §6a’s precise applicability, while preserving its qualifying-effects and claim-connection requirements wherever applicable.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that these allegations connect the challenged foreign conduct to the American market the statute protects. Alcoa and Matsushita recognize the Sherman Act’s reach to intended substantial domestic effects, and Continental Ore does not equate foreign legality with immunity from that law. The London defendants allegedly coordinated coverage restrictions aimed at American buyers; those alleged changes supply both the domestic effect and the asserted injury connection. Assuming §6a applies and adds requirements, the alleged direct, substantial and reasonably foreseeable domestic effect gives rise to these asserted claims. The Court does not decide the provision’s wider reach or every interaction with import commerce. British regulation and sovereign interests deserve consideration, but the defendants identify no British command inconsistent with American compliance. The contrary prescriptive-comity approach would displace established statutory reach on these allegations without a sufficient legal basis. The Court leaves broader comity questions open and requires proof below. Federal subject-matter power, substantive coverage and ultimate antitrust liability remain separate; none is established merely by labeling foreign practices objectionable.

**Precedent treatment:**

- United States v. Aluminum Co.of America, 148 F.2d 416, and Matsushita, 475 U.S.574: intended substantial domestic effects standard applied.
- Continental Ore v. Union Carbide, 370 U.S.690: foreign permission does not itself immunize conduct.
- SociétéNationale Industrielle Aérospatiale, 482 U.S.522: actual foreign/domestic conflict considered; Blackmun’s separate analysis identified as not the whole Court’s holding.
- Charming Betsy, 2 Cranch 64: interpretive canon retained; does not require the asserted dismissal here.
- Aramco, 499 U.S.244, and entered Sale, June 21, 1993: source-specific geographic scope preserved; different statutes do not erase established Sherman Act reach.

**Current force and limits:** Effective June 28, activity-specific McCarran-Ferguson exemption is unanimous; collateral boycott boundary is adopted by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; foreign claims continue under the case-specific effects/comity rule adopted by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter. No universal comity-impossibility test, automatic reinsurer exemption, or proven antitrust liability enters. Both dockets and count-specific remedies remain separate.

**Operative remedy and next stage:** Supreme Court review of this merits event is complete. Reverse the categorical exemption rationale; this does not by itself dismiss sufficient boycott claims. Continue pleading-stage claims; broader-pressure allegations permit proof, not a finding of boycott or liability. Reinstate dismissal on reviewed grounds: model-form drafting has no alleged refusal to deal; properly unresolved state-regulation issues remain below. Reject asserted comity dismissal; remand for proof and unadjudicated defenses. Dissent would dismiss for failure to state a claim, not lack of subject-matter jurisdiction. California IX–XI and Connecticut VII are not decided; no injunction or damages awarded. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date, later filing or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established. The Court’s roster, allotments and standing practices remain unchanged by this decision.

#### Alexander v. United States — 1993-06-28

[Controlling record](records/Alexander_v_United_States_merits_1993-06-28.md).

### 1. This postconviction forfeiture is not a traditional prior restraint

**Holding and operative rule:** A postconviction RICO forfeiture of identified assets linked to adjudicated racketeering does not constitute a traditional prior restraint merely because it impairs the owner's future ability to publish, where it neither forbids future publication nor requires permission to publish. That classification does not dispose of an independent First Amendment challenge to removal of protected works.

**Authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas; six Justices adopt this proposition. Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy do not join the classification. The independent First Amendment question has a different coalition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the forfeiture's financial consequences do not turn it into an order forbidding future speech. Near invalidated a restraint on future publication; Vance likewise involved a continuing restraint on films not yet adjudged obscene. Alexander faces no comparable command and no contempt sanction merely for returning to publishing with lawfully available assets. Fort Wayne Books required adjudication before the removal of presumptively protected materials on the record there; here the obscenity and racketeering charges have been tried. That difference defeats the categorical contention that any resulting loss of publishing assets is necessarily a prior restraint. It does not establish that every part of the forfeiture is constitutional. Arcara distinguishes the incidental effect of a neutral sanction from governmental regulation implicating expressive activity. The Court preserves that distinction and the separate inquiry into the treatment of lawful works. Neither the criminal judgment nor this classification licenses a future-publication ban or a seizure of protected stock on probable cause alone.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Near v. Minnesota, 283 U.S.697, and Vance v. Universal Amusement Co., 445 U.S.308: distinguished as restraints on future expression; their protection remains in force.
- Fort Wayne Books, Inc. v. Indiana, 489 U.S.46: its pretrial protection is preserved; the present adjudicated RICO case differs, and its reserved postconviction question is not treated as settled.
- Arcara v. Cloud Books, Inc., 478 U.S.697: applied to distinguish an ordinary sanction's incidental consequences from regulation implicating expression; no universal asset exemption follows.

### 2. The forfeiture approval requires an independent First Amendment examination

**Holding and operative rule:** Where obscenity predicates produce forfeiture of an expressive enterprise and removal of lawful inventory, the judgment sustaining that forfeiture cannot rest solely on the existence of valid convictions and the absence of a traditional prior restraint. The judgment must be vacated for an independent First Amendment examination of the challenged expressive burden, distinguishing the judicial forfeiture from subsequent administrative destruction and confining correction to a reviewable injury and available relief.

**Authority:** Souter's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy; five Justices expressly adopt this limited rule and remand. Their separate positions do not establish a majority rule either categorically immunizing expressive inventory or adopting a comprehensive scrutiny framework for every postconviction forfeiture.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that punishment after conviction remains subject to the First Amendment. Arcara sustained a sanction for nonexpressive misconduct and distinguished sanctions triggered by expressive conduct or singling out expression. Here obscenity offenses trigger the sanction, and lawful books and films are removed with the enterprise's other assets. Fort Wayne Books did not resolve that postconviction question. Marcus and Quantity of Books explain why suppressing protected inventory presents a concern distinct from collecting an ordinary monetary sanction. The Government correctly observes that a publisher cannot shelter criminal assets merely by investing in books; Simon & Schuster preserves neutral forfeiture while forbidding selective burdens on protected expression. But statutory coverage alone does not answer whether this sanction impermissibly suppresses lawful works. The lower court must separate the interests forfeited, the judicial injury and the Government's later destruction, and determine what correction this proceeding can supply. The Court neither orders return of destroyed stock nor creates a damages action, and it leaves the ultimate constitutional treatment of the distinct assets unresolved.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Arcara, 478 U.S.697: its nonexpressive-conduct rationale does not dispense with examination of this expression-triggered sanction; ordinary neutral sanctions remain enforceable.
- Fort Wayne Books, 489 U.S.46: the reservation concerning postconviction forfeiture is respected; its pretrial safeguard remains unchanged.
- Marcus v. Search Warrant, 367 U.S.717, and A Quantity of Copies of Books v. Kansas, 378 U.S.205: their protection against indiscriminate removal of expressive works supplies the need for distinct examination, without mechanically converting their pretrial remedies into this mandate.
- Simon & Schuster, Inc. v. Members of the New York State Crime Victims Board, December 10, 1991, simulated decision: content-selective financial burdens remain subject to its rule; lawful publishing does not immunize assets from neutral forfeiture.

### 3. Punitive criminal forfeiture requires Excessive Fines review

**Holding and operative rule:** A criminal RICO forfeiture extracting property as punishment is subject to the Excessive Fines Clause independently of the validity of the convictions or the permissibility of the prison term. The lower court must examine the extent of the exaction in relation to the offenses supporting it; it may not reject that inquiry simply because the sentence is less than life imprisonment without parole.

**Authority:** Souter's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas; six Justices adopt coverage and remand. Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy accept the alternative excessiveness remand without joining Part III or adopting a proportionality formula.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a punitive transfer of property to the Government is within the constitutional protection against excessive fines. Browning-Ferris distinguishes private civil damages from governmental extraction as punishment; this criminal forfeiture falls on the governmental side of that distinction. The court below treated the monetary objection as though a limitation on prison-sentence review resolved it. That approach fails to give the separate clause effect. The existence of valid RICO convictions establishes a basis for punishment, not the permissible extent of every forfeiture. Review must account for the forfeited interests and the offenses that support them. The inquiry is not reduced to counting the seven titles adjudged obscene, and it may not presume that every asset or dollar of lawful business is itself criminal activity. The Court leaves the actual excessiveness determination to the lower court, including appropriate record development. It establishes neither a numerical cap nor a complete proportionality formula, and it leaves the independently sustained convictions undisturbed.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Browning-Ferris Industries v. Kelco Disposal, 492 U.S.257: its distinction between private awards and governmental punishment supports coverage of this criminal forfeiture; its private-award holding remains intact.
- Solem v. Helm, 463 U.S.277: no rule about imprisonment displaces the separate Excessive Fines inquiry; the Court adopts no general prison-sentence holding here.
- Austin v. United States, June 28, 1993, is an uncoordinated same-day decision, not an earlier source of this holding. No logical priority or adoption of its separate writings is inferred.

**Before and after/current force:** The same-day group has no internal priority. Alexander adds the three direct-majority rules above; Austin's civil-forfeiture holding operates alongside them prospectively. No majority adopts categorical inventory immunity, a comprehensive First Amendment scrutiny method or an excessiveness formula. Supreme Court merits review is complete. The Eighth Circuit judgment is vacated insofar as it sustains the challenged forfeiture without the required independent First Amendment and Excessive Fines examinations, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with those holdings. The independently sustained convictions remain undisturbed. The court below must address statutory nexus and the reviewable injury attributable to the judgment; subsequent administrative destruction is not automatically a judicial command or a basis for relief outside this appeal. No return of destroyed stock, new damages cause of action, dollar cap, final excessiveness finding, mandatory business restoration or new Supreme Court filing is ordered. Ordinary mandate procedure applies; no special issuance date or retained Supreme Court proceeding is established.

#### DeBoer v. DeBoer, A-64 — 1993-07-26

[Record](records/DeBoer_v_DeBoer_A-64_application_1993-07-26.md). Unanimous denial by order without opinion creates no substantive holding, precedent treatment or reusable-law change. Existing review and custody law remains unchanged.

### 4. Material Published Noncontrolling Positions

- **Martin v. McDermott, No. 92-5618, November 2, 1992** — Stevens dissents from Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela and Blackmun. They would grant leave in No. 92-5618 and deny that petition in the ordinary course. The judicial-disqualification question is a separate claim requiring its own assessment; it cannot be rejected merely as another aspect of the bar-admission litigation. The petition's failure to establish a basis for Supreme Court review does not, for these Justices, warrant withholding access without prepayment. They accept no recusal allegation as true and decide no statutory or constitutional merits question. Their disagreement concerns the fee disposition and its resulting paid-filing condition, not an entitlement to certiorari. They join Parts I and III, including the grant of leave in No. 92-5584 and the refusal of any prospective restriction. This dissent supplies no controlling proposition and does not withdraw Zatko's present force. Source: [Decision Record](records/Martin_v_District_of_Columbia_Court_of_Appeals_fee_motions_and_petition_1992-11-02.md).

- **Montana v. Imlay, 1992-11-03** — Stevens concurs on lack of demonstrated practical relief from the reviewed judgment; White dissents, relying on Cox finality, possible parole consequences and nonbinding immunity representation. Source: [Decision Record](records/Montana_v_Imlay_dismissal_1992-11-03.md).
- **Hadley v. United States, 1992-11-16** — Stone-Zsela dissent, Nov16: would vacate/remand for case-specific Rule403 explanation of independent value of remote other-victim testimony given closer same-complainant proof and unaccepted intent concession; maintain substantially-outweighs threshold and harmless-error review. Source: [Decision Record](records/hadley-v-united-states-dig-1992-11-16.md).
- **Parke v. Raley, 1992-12-01** — Blackmun judgment concurrence, Dec1: accepts allocation only as sentencing; would require affirmative validity proof if recidivism constituted separate crime. Hypothetical separate-crime rule noncontrolling. Source: [Decision Record](records/parke-v-raley-merits-1992-12-01.md).
- **Richmond v. Lewis, 1992-12-01** — Thomas concurrence preserves Stringer Teague objection but follows binding law; Scalia dissent maintains eligibility narrowing alone sufficient and no constitutional mitigation-weighing duty. Source: [Decision Record](records/richmond-v-lewis-merits-1992-12-01.md).
- **Farrar v. Hobby, 1992-12-14** — Thomas, joined by O’Connor, Scalia and Kennedy: immediate zero fees is justified by this record despite eligibility; only limited success need not trigger a full lodestar calculation. O’Connor separately: extent of relief, legal significance and actual public purpose explain whether nominal recovery is merely technical; not all nominal awards require zero. Neither position controls the remand. Source: [Decision Record](records/Farrar_v_Hobby_merits_1992-12-14.md).
- **District of Columbia v. Greater Washington Board of Trade, 1992-12-14** — Stevens’s dissent would preserve independently administered workers’ compensation replacement benefits despite reference to covered-plan compensation where the law does not regulate or require changed administration of the ERISA plan. It supplies no exception to the majority’s operative-reference rule. Source: [Decision Record](records/District_of_Columbia_v_Greater_Washington_Board_of_Trade_merits_1992-12-14.md).
- **Republic National Bank of Miami v. United States, 1992-12-14** — Blackmun, Stevens and O’Connor’s appropriation-free constitutional return theory has three votes. White’s preference to defer the payment issue is noncontrolling. Thomas’s application of §1355(c) to this pending pre-enactment transfer/appeal is a solo temporal construction. Stevens’s separate statutory agreement supports the controlling proposition without constituting a formal opinion join. Source: [Decision Record](records/Republic_National_Bank_of_Miami_v_United_States_merits_1992-12-14.md).

#### 1993-01-12 — Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc.

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/puerto-rico-aqueduct-v-metcalf-eddy-merits-1993-01-12.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

Blackmun joins the Court’s procedural decision while retaining his view, expressed in his Atascadero dissent, that the Constitution does not support the full breadth of the Court’s state-immunity doctrine. He accepts immunity in the narrower class of state-law actions by another State’s or a foreign country’s citizen in federal court; that protection is enough to justify immediate review, independently of whether this particular claimant ultimately qualifies.

Stone-Zsela concurs only in the judgment. Cohen and Mitchell supply the procedural result because a substantial, conclusively denied claim to protection from suit cannot be restored after trial. Sovereign-dignity reasoning is unnecessary to that conclusion. Frivolous or nonconclusive assertions remain subject to ordinary screening. He leaves substantive immunity, PRASA’s status, waiver, abrogation, prospective-officer relief and alternative contract remedies open.

Stevens dissents. Under Van Cauwenberghe and Catlin, an objection to federal jurisdiction ordinarily awaits final judgment. He regards this state-immunity claim as a choice-of-forum defense, unlike officials’ protection against the distracting threat of liability that supports Mitchell. Allowing piecemeal review imposes costs on the claimant and courts that a governmental dignity interest does not justify. He would affirm dismissal without resolving immunity.

#### 1993-01-12 — Commissioner v. Soliman

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/commissioner-v-soliman-merits-1993-01-12.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

Blackmun joins the Court and emphasizes that a deduction must satisfy the statutory conditions. Soliman’s only office may be important without being his principal business place; the bulk of professional service, time and remuneration arises at the hospitals. Papers and telephone calls do not themselves establish the patient-meeting branch. His concurrence creates no separate Court rule.

Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurs in the judgment. He would ordinarily locate the principal place where the taxpayer delivers the services or goods for which he is paid. Here all anesthesiology services occur at hospitals, so that inquiry would end. When the home itself is one of multiple places of delivery or income production, he would use a totality inquiry guided by relative importance and time. His approach thus has an escape valve, but he regards the Court’s general two-consideration inquiry as unnecessarily uncertain where the service-delivery facts already resolve the case.

Stevens dissents. He would treat the sole office maintained for management of this self-employed practice as its principal place of business. Exclusive regular use, the necessity of administration and absence of other office space satisfy the statute’s antiabuse purpose. He considers the three statutory alternatives independent and objects that heavy service-delivery emphasis risks collapsing principal business location into the patient-meeting branch. He would affirm the deduction; his headquarters construction does not control.

#### 1993-01-12 — Rowland v. California Men’s Colony, Unit II Men’s Advisory Council

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/rowland-v-california-mens-colony-merits-1993-01-12.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

Kennedy joins the Court. He considers it permissible to ask whether the Dictionary Act’s broad meaning can work within the particular statute; that contextual inquiry need not be illegitimate policymaking. But authorized affidavits, an actual resources inquiry and separate counsel rules supply a workable application here. He therefore agrees that practical difficulties do not overcome the inclusive definition or justify categorical exclusion. His concurrence does not guarantee the Council relief on remand.

Souter, joined by White, O’Connor and Scalia, dissents. He regards four features together as sufficient contextual indication that §1915 addresses natural persons: the possibility of proceeding without requested counsel, language of personal poverty, the applicant’s own sworn assertion and its perjury deterrent, and the absence of an organizational means standard. A permissive counsel provision fits individuals able to appear on their own, while entities require licensed counsel. Ordinary personal-indigency measures do not resolve which corporate or member resources count, and agency affidavits do not remove all authorization and deterrence concerns. He would reverse the Ninth Circuit and require denial of this entity’s motion. His natural-person restriction does not control.

#### 1993-01-13 — Nixon v. United States

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Nixon_v_United_States_merits_1993-01-13.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

**Stevens, concurring, no joins.** Stevens joins the Court’s opinion and emphasizes the constitutional decision to place impeachment in the Legislative Branch. That allocation deserves respect in evaluating this ordinary procedural objection. He sees no occasion to assume that the Senate would abandon its constitutional responsibility. His additional institutional emphasis supplies no holding concerning a future violation of an express command.

**White, joined by Blackmun, concurring in the judgment.** They would decide the claim on the merits. Powell requires courts to interpret the limits of a constitutionally assigned power; assigning the Senate the trial does not necessarily make the Senate the final interpreter of the duty to conduct one. Review does not require imposing every criminal-trial formality. The committee developed a record, the full Senate received it and heard the parties, and the full Senate made the decision. In their view that process satisfies the constitutional requirement to try Nixon. They reject the premise that possible disruption or interbranch disagreement independently removes ordinary constitutional interpretation from the courts. Their ground supports affirmance but commands only two votes.

**Souter, concurring in the judgment, no joins.** Souter regards the present allocation of evidentiary work as falling within the Senate’s broad responsibility for impeachment trials. The institutional disruption of judicial review reinforces restraint here. He would leave room for judicial intervention in an extraordinary proceeding whose arbitrariness seriously threatens the integrity of the result, such as conviction unrelated to adjudication. This reservation is a separate, noncontrolling position; the Court itself leaves that outer boundary undecided.

#### 1993-01-13 — Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Bray_v_Alexandria_Womens_Health_Clinic_merits_1993-01-13.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

**Scalia Part III, joined by White, Kennedy and Thomas; plurality only.** These four would additionally find no class-based invidious animus here. A group defined merely by the activity the alleged tortfeasor opposes is insufficient; opposition to abortion differs from targeting women because of sex. They leave open whether women can be a qualifying class. Their purpose analysis uses Griffin, Scott and Feeney’s distinction between action because of a group’s disadvantage and action despite its effect. They also favor a broader exclusion of purely local obstruction applied without discrimination against interstate travelers. Neither additional ground has five votes.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment, no joins.** The independent private-right defect suffices. Opposition to abortion alone neither proves nor disproves sex discrimination; Griffin’s full class boundary need not be decided. He joins jurisdiction, fees, state remand and hindrance nonreach, but does not endorse the plurality’s categorical local-obstruction formulation. Those reservations prevent attribution of the additional grounds to the Court.

**Kennedy, concurring, no joins.** Kennedy joins Scalia throughout. Limited federal coverage does not remove state responsibility to enforce ordinary trespass and obstruction laws. Respect for separate federal and state authority cannot justify tolerating serious unlawful conduct. This observation adds no enforcement order or remand direction.

**Souter, concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part, no joins.** Souter accepts Griffin/Scott as settled statutory constructions and rejects the present deprivation theory and its fee basis. He would consider hindrance separately. A purpose to impede constituted state authorities supplies an additional limiting element and, in his view, need not carry the deprivation clause’s entire class-animus and privately protected-right restrictions. Overwhelmed police warrant further examination, not automatic liability. He disputes exclusion of that opportunity from affirmative remand directions. His view establishes no purpose finding and no successful federal claim.

**Stevens, joined by Blackmun, dissenting.** They would affirm federal relief. Deliberate exclusion from abortion services targets a choice available only to women, and organized blockades impair actual access by interstate patients. Griffin and travel protection reach this purposeful deprivation; equal obstruction of local patients is not a complete answer. Stevens also reads hindrance to reach organized interference with state protection. They would uphold relief or at least allow development of that theory. Neither their interpretation nor the asserted inference of purpose controls.

**O’Connor, joined by Blackmun, dissenting.** They regard organized blockades as deliberate denial of equal legal protection to women seeking lawful medical care. O’Connor’s position, consistent with her Scott dissent join, treats collective coercion preventing a protected class from enjoying its rights as the statute’s concern. They reject characterization of the exclusion as incidental disadvantage and would uphold federal relief; the distinct hindrance provision reinforces their position. This does not convert Casey’s governmental-regulation framework into a freestanding constitutional prohibition on private acts.

#### 1993-01-13 — Crosby v. United States

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Crosby_v_United_States_merits_1993-01-13.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

No separate writing is published.

#### 1993-01-19 — Dobbs v. Zant

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Dobbs_v_Zant_summary_merits_1993-01-19.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, accepts that refusal to consider the transcript was erroneous and concurs in reversal. He regards the correction as unlikely to alter the earlier conclusion because counsel's failure to present mitigation had been justified by an investigation and tactical assessment, and the transcript still reflects an argument against capital punishment. A mistaken recollection about an impulsiveness argument does not itself prove false testimony. He would ordinarily reserve summary error correction for errors with a realistic chance of affecting conviction or sentence. These views neither decide Strickland prejudice nor limit the lower courts' examination on remand. White's noted preference is for plenary review rather than immediate summary disposition.

#### 1993-01-25 — Herrera v. Collins

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Herrera_v_Collins_merits_1993-01-25.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

O'Connor, joined by Kennedy, emphasizes in a separate concurrence that execution of a person established to be factually innocent would be constitutionally intolerable, but that the difficult question of a freestanding federal remedy need not be decided on this submission. The plurality's account of ordinary habeas practice and Texas's late-new-trial and clemency procedures does not attract White's join and supplies no new five-Justice holding on categorical cognizability or the adequacy of clemency.

White concurs in the judgment alone. Assuming that a persuasive showing of innocence could bar execution despite an expired new-evidence deadline, he would require at least a showing that no rational trier could find guilt beyond a reasonable doubt upon the old and new evidence together. He expressly agrees that Herrera's proof falls short for the reasons in the plurality. This does not adopt an established freestanding claim or a definitive burden.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, also joins the plurality and separately rejects a constitutional entitlement to reopen a lawfully completed conviction on newly discovered innocence evidence alone. Townsend, historical procedural practice and finality support his position; the arguendo disposition does not cause him to accept a new constitutional right. His categorical position has two votes.

Blackmun dissents, joined by Stevens and Souter in the legal discussion and proposed remand. He would recognize an independent Eighth and Fourteenth Amendment claim because execution of a person shown to be innocent is itself a constitutional injury. Ford and Johnson show that a valid judgment does not resolve every later objection to execution; Beck connects capital punishment to reliable guilt determinations. He would require the prisoner to show probable actual innocence on the whole evidence, exceeding a mere likelihood that a juror would entertain reasonable doubt. He rejects leaving that constitutional claim conclusively to clemency and would remand for district-court consideration of threshold factual and procedural requirements, without declaring Herrera innocent. Blackmun alone adds his broader criticism of restrictions on capital review; Stevens and Souter do not join that concluding commentary.

Stone-Zsela dissents separately. He would vacate only the categorical exclusion of a properly presented capital-innocence claim, preserving exhaustion and then-governing abuse-of-the-writ rules. His proposed merits burden is clear and convincing affirmative proof, on the relevant old and new evidence together, that the prisoner did not commit the crime for which execution is sought. Ford and Beck support extending substantive protection; Penry's exception for substantive limits on capital punishment would extend to that protected class. This is a proposed extension, not a claim that those cases already decided freestanding innocence. Clemency is additional protection, not a conclusive answer to an established constitutional prohibition. Allegations provisionally credited must be capable of satisfying that burden before factual development becomes material. Townsend and Keeney then retain deliberate bypass, while ordinary counsel negligence alone does not bar a necessary hearing. Any temporary stay requires an express Barefoot/Delo showing; no indefinite stay follows. If innocence is proved, execution for that crime is barred and another sentencing hearing cannot cure the defect. His proposal neither orders acquittal nor disturbs independent lawful custody, revives the Brady claim, changes the trial burden or resolves all noncapital claims.

#### 1993-01-25 — Graham v. Collins

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Graham_v_Collins_merits_1993-01-25.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

White, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, would affirm because reasonable jurists in 1984 could read Jurek, Lockett and Eddings as allowing Texas to channel this mitigation through deliberateness and future dangerousness. In their view a new instruction requirement is not dictated by prefinality law; it neither removes a class of conduct or persons from punishment nor supplies a watershed accuracy procedure, so neither Teague exception saves it. They distinguish Penry's disabling evidence from Graham's evidence supporting rehabilitation and do not adopt the Court's as-applied merits rule.

Thomas also writes separately. He regards Penry's expansive account of mitigating effect as inconsistent with Furman's requirement that capital discretion be structured against arbitrariness and discrimination. He would permit reasonable state definitions of relevance and sentencing criteria while allowing defendants to present constitutionally relevant information. He would overrule Penry to that extent; the Court does not do so. His view is separate from White's sufficient Teague ground.

Stevens concurs while joining the controlling opinion. He regards narrowing eligibility, avoiding vague aggravators, proportionality limits and individualized mitigation as complementary protections. Requiring a sentencer to act on relevant individual circumstances reduces resort to irrelevant considerations such as race; it does not demand an unreasoned preference. His discussion responds to Thomas without creating an additional test or changing the conditional remedy.

Souter's Part IV, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and O'Connor, explains the limited character-evidence disposition. Ordinary chores, religious observance and support for children can inform future dangerousness through the second special issue. On the asserted theory here, their separate bearing on culpability for this offense is insubstantial. The four do not decide the value of such traits apart from the submitted culpability/future-danger arguments. Stone-Zsela joins only the denial of independent relief on that component.

#### 1993-01-25 — Zafiro v. United States

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Zafiro_v_United_States_merits_1993-01-25.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

Stevens concurs only in the judgment. The defendants’ lack-of-knowledge accounts could both be true; accepting Soto’s or Zafiro’s testimony did not logically require convicting Garcia or Martinez. The lawyers’ contrary accusations were argument rather than evidence, and instructions made that distinction clear. Stevens would reserve genuinely irreconcilable evidence and the situation where a weak prosecution case gains decisive support from a codefendant acting as a second prosecutor. His Richardson dissent supports caution about instructions against powerful incriminating evidence. He rejects a categorical severance command, but declines to join a general preference that might restrict the trial judge’s traditional discretion. This concurrence does not decide Gloria’s omitted personal claim.

#### 1993-01-25 — Spectrum Sports v. McQuillan

Source: published writings preserved in the [Decision Record](records/Spectrum_Sports_v_McQuillan_merits_1993-01-25.md); each separate position remains noncontrolling beyond the propositions adopted by the controlling coalition.

No separate writing is published.

These are published positions, not current controlling law. Earlier-term public positions remain available through terms/OT1991/output/ when material. No private Stone core or modeled petition poll is projected here.

#### Lockhart v. Fretwell — 1993-01-25

Thomas concurs separately, joining the Court in full. A state court is not bound by the constitutional interpretation of a lower federal court merely because that court sits in the same circuit. He rejects the Eighth Circuit's contrary hierarchy premise; that clarification supplies an additional observation, not a separate holding joined by five.

Stevens dissents, joined by Blackmun. They would sustain relief because counsel's deficient failure to invoke a professionally available argument had a reasonable probability of changing the original sentence. Strickland supplies that inquiry; Kimmelman treats adequate advocacy as an independent Sixth Amendment protection, and Nix's refusal to count perjured evidence does not equate lawful advocacy with wrongdoing. Later rejection of Collins does not repair the original adversarial failure. They object to allowing the State a retrospective advantage while Teague ordinarily denies a prisoner new favorable rules. Their position supports the judgment granting relief and does not join Stone-Zsela's conditional remand disposition. Stevens also considers, without making it necessary to his dissent, whether such profound failure on the central sentencing question can resemble the presumptively prejudicial breakdowns addressed by Cronic and Holloway.

Stone-Zsela dissents separately. He would vacate and remand for ordinary Strickland prejudice under the correct hierarchy. Collins was persuasive, not binding on Arkansas. The lower court must identify the professionally supportable objection, test it against then-controlling state and Supreme Court law, and reconsider whether the existing probability finding remains supported without assuming obedience to circuit precedent. He rejects a free-standing entitlement screen that automatically erases a lawful advocacy opportunity because its reasoning is later rejected. He equally excludes nullification, fabricated evidence, rulings forbidden by controlling authority, and a bare numerical probability test. If prejudice is established, conditional relief must permit lawful resentencing under then-applicable law, subject to any independent restriction on renewed aggravating findings or punishment. He neither awards life without parole automatically nor revives Collins, authorizes a rejected aggravator without inquiry, invents findings, or treats remand as permission to execute before the claim is resolved. No other Justice joins his writing.

#### United States v. Nachtigal — 1993-02-22

#### United States v. Louisiana, supplemental decree — 1993-02-22

No separate opinion, dissent or noted statement is issued. No private approval poll is part of the public decree.

#### Growe v. Emison — 1993-02-23

Stone-Zsela agrees that the federal plans must be set aside and joins the Court's state-priority, live-plan and statutory holdings. He would allow the district court to defer to the state plans while retaining jurisdiction if a genuine election deadline or properly presented, proved federal defect justifies continued authority. Such retention is permitted, not required. Any implementation safeguard must respond to that actual need; federal preference cannot justify recreating the minority district without statutory proof. His disagreement concerns mandatory dismissal, not the validity of the federal maps or a categorical entitlement to federal supervision.

#### Itel Containers International Corp. v. Huddleston — 1993-02-23

Scalia joins the treaty and regulatory holdings but agrees only in the judgment on the constitutional grounds. He would enforce a negative Commerce Clause, as a matter of precedent, against facial discrimination and laws indistinguishable from a type previously invalidated, without extending open-ended balancing tests. This tax fits neither prohibition. His Import-Export analysis preserves Richfield's textual protection but treats these empty containers as not yet exports when delivered for use in domestic loading; he therefore does not reach Michelin's broader test. His criticism of allowing an Executive litigation position to determine state taxing authority does not alter the Court's independent constitutional holding.

Blackmun would construe the Conventions in light of their purpose and the signatories' practice to protect these recurring transfers as integral to international container movement. He distinguishes a tax on container leases from a value-added tax on imported goods and regards the local-transaction distinction as insufficient. Independently, he would find that the state tax threatens the uniform treatment protected by Japan Line. The Executive's brief cannot itself authorize a tax, and congressional silence does not supply unmistakably clear authorization to override Commerce constraints. Those treaty and foreign-commerce positions support his proposed reversal; they are not Court law.

Scalia's prospective doctrine is limited by his assumption, rather than decision, that precedent's facial-discrimination rule applies equally to foreign commerce. His writing retains the separate textual Import-Export inquiry and does not reduce it to nondiscrimination. Blackmun's treaty-purpose construction is independently sufficient for his proposed reversal, and his foreign-commerce ground remains additional. All these propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Fex v. Michigan — 1993-02-23

**Blackmun, joined by Stevens, dissenting on Article III(a).** The proper custodial submission completes the act the compact requires from a prisoner. Article III(b) channels the request through officials whom the prisoner cannot supervise; reading Article III(a) together with that prescribed channel and Article IX places transmission risk on the participating governments. Houston v. Lack supplies the analogous protection for a prisoner who has surrendered the paper to the mandatory intermediary. Blackmun's Carchman opinion recognizes the serious burdens associated with unresolved detainers, which explain why prompt disposition matters independently of ultimate conviction. The actual-receipt rule permits an indefinite official delay after the prisoner has done what the compact asks. The prosecutor's interest in adequate trial preparation is substantial, but a necessary or reasonable continuance remains possible upon Article III(a)'s actual good-cause showing in open court with the prisoner or counsel present. These Justices would reverse and remand under the custodial trigger, preserving the compact's actual exclusions rather than granting an automatic extension. They do not decide whether a continuance can first be obtained after the period has expired.

**Stone, separately dissenting on Article III(a).** Stone would begin the period when the prisoner gives the responsible custodial official the required written notice and final-disposition request for transmission through Article III(b). The custodian's later preparation of the required certificate or forwarding does not defer that date. The claimant must establish both a qualifying request and its delivery date under ordinary evidentiary rules: an oral inquiry, a writing missing the required notice or request, or an unexplained delivery to an unrelated official does not suffice. The prosecutor and court remain entitled to receive the papers. Stone acknowledges that their actual receipt offers a clear administrative date and preserves their entire preparation period, but reads the mandatory custodial channel and Article IX to assign transmission risk to the governments that operate it. Under his proposed rule the responsible custodian must record receipt, forward request and certificate promptly, and preserve delivery records. He would reverse and remand to apply the September 7 submission date and decide any Article III good-cause continuance or Article VI exclusion on its own predicates. If the lawful period expired before trial, Article V(c) requires dismissal with prejudice. Delay creates no automatic extension; bad faith is not required; no damages remedy or constitutional override is proposed.

#### United States v. Dunnigan — 1993-02-23

None.

#### Negonsott v. Samuels — 1993-02-24

No separate writing is issued. Scalia and Thomas join the text-based opinion; no legislative-history ground is made a condition of their joins or a controlling alternative holding.

#### United States v. A Parcel of Land Known as 92 Buena Vista Avenue — 1993-02-24

**Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** They agree that Goodwin may assert the defense and that gift status alone is not disqualifying. Their analysis starts with the conventional operation of forfeiture, reflected in Stowell: the Government must complete the forfeiture process, after which its title reaches back to the offense. Section 881(d)'s incorporation of customs procedures confirms that Congress retained an adjudicative process and did not make §881(h) an instantly completed transfer immune to the other statutory conditions. A claimant may invoke the conditions that prevent forfeiture during that process. The concurrence therefore rejects the premise that the Government has already conclusively eliminated Goodwin's ownership before her defense can be considered. It does not accept the Court's full account that the subsection (a)(6) exception, by defining the property described in subsection (a), alone resolves what subsection (h) covers; ordinary relation back and the statutory procedures supply the necessary explanation. Nor would these Justices turn statements in Goodwin's submissions into an unlitigated estoppel or a binding election of the date at which knowledge must be measured. The Court's express reservation of that date is consistent with their conclusion, but does not remove their distinct structural objection. Their concurrence preserves the Government's retrospective title once forfeiture is validly completed. It establishes no immunity for a donee who cannot prove the statutory defense, no exemption for fictitious ownership, and no rule under the separate real-property provision.

**Kennedy, joined by White, dissenting.** They would give greater effect to the Government's offense-date claim to forfeitable property. A recipient ordinarily cannot receive a better title by gift than the donor possessed. In their view the donor's title was already subject to the Government's superior forfeiture claim, and the gift did not displace that claim merely because the recipient lacked knowledge. Stowell's avoidance of intermediate transfers and Caplin & Drysdale's application of offense-date governmental title support that conclusion. This is a property-title argument, not an insertion of the words purchaser for value into §881(a)(6) as an independent defense requirement. They distinguish an innocent purchaser who gives value, and a person succeeding to a genuinely protected title, from a donee taking directly from the holder of forfeitable property. They would reverse the categorical defense-eligibility ruling and leave room below to examine governing property law and any legally sufficient consideration or other protected title Goodwin could establish. They do not adjudge those possible factual predicates on this record. They reject the Court's sequencing because they regard the Government's superior claim as limiting the ownership that can support the defense, even though formal forfeiture proceedings remain necessary. These views are noncontrolling.

#### Voinovich v. Quilter — 1993-03-02

None. Every participating Justice joins the entire opinion; there is no published separate position.

#### Reves v. Ernst & Young — 1993-03-03

Stone's March3,1993 concurrence in part/dissent in part: under the accepted directional test, supported evidence of independent valuation/acquisition choices requires factual testing; ordinary audit negligence is insufficient and all remaining RICO elements must be proved. Souter's dissent, joined by White: the full participation language and liberal-construction clause support coverage without direction; independently, the record supports actual management even under the Court's test. Blackmun's legislative-history portion, joined by Stevens,O'Connor,Kennedy, supplies additional historical confirmation with only four supporters. These positions are published but do not alter the two controlling holdings.

#### Smith v. United States (Antarctica) — 1993-03-08

Stevens's March 8, 1993 dissent: foreign-country exclusion requires another sovereign; private-person parity and ordinary conflicts can supply a legal rule in Antarctica, while maritime history defeats an exclusively domestic assumption and valid resident venue is not defeated by gaps affecting others. Stone's concurrence: joins statutory holding but declines additional presumption rationale; the FTCA's maritime history prevents an absolute offshore bar. Their reserved observations do not authorize an Antarctic FTCA suit or decide another channel.

#### Ortega-Rodriguez v. United States — 1993-03-08

White, joined O’Connor/Thomas, would affirm because actual delay and severed codefendant appeals sufficiently disrupt appellate administration; no unlimited judicial-defiance sanction. Source: dissent, March 8, 1993; noncontrolling.

#### Reiter v. Cooper — 1993-03-08

Blackmun dissents without opinion; no attributable public doctrinal proposition beyond opposition to the judgment.

#### Delo v. Lashley — 1993-03-08

Stone-Zsela/Souter concurrence reserves unpresented innocence theory and separates counsel claim from instruction ruling. Stevens/Blackmun dissent favors instruction/retaining relief given presumed innocence of other crimes and actual sentencing circumstances, and opposes summary reversal. All noncontrolling beyond the named majority propositions.

#### Reno v. Flores — 1993-03-23

O'Connor/Souter: substantial institutional liberty, judgment rests on protective agreement-compliant care, no arbitrary or punitive confinement. Stone: core liberty, narrower mandatory-barrier challenges and Mathews inquiry into effective review; tailored assistance or automatic review may be necessary on a supported record, no universal schedule. Stevens/Blackmun: needless confinement despite responsible willing adults violates constrained statutory purposes and liberty; neutral hearings required. All are noncontrolling beyond any Court opinion separately joined.

#### Saudi Arabia v. Nelson — 1993-03-23

White/Blackmun intentional: retaliatory employment can be commercial but actionable conduct is abroad; White alone extends no-domestic-basis judgment to warnings. Souter/O'Connor/Scalia/Thomas warning plurality: warning label does not recharacterize sovereign abuse. Kennedy/Blackmun/Stevens: independent domestic omission supports warning proceedings; duty, causation and HCA preclusion reserved. Stone: warning path requires genuinely independent defendant-attributable undertaking and substantial US contact, disputed jurisdictional facts resolved before merits; no entity collapse. Stevens: intentional commercial employment and domestic recruitment suffice too.

#### Cincinnati v. Discovery Network — 1993-03-24

Blackmun: full protection for truthful, noncoercive lawful commercial speech, while deception/coercion, unlawful transactions and valid time/place/manner rules remain regulable. White/Thomas: accepted commercial distinction permits incremental rack removal; Fox and Metromedia leave municipalities more discretion. Neither separate view replaces the seven-Justice fit rule.

#### Pioneer Investment Services Co. v. Brunswick Associates Limited Partnership — 1993-03-24

O'Connor/Scalia/Souter/Thomas: a sufficiently excusable reason is required before favorable equities; no prejudice or good faith cannot cure ordinary neglect; absent lower finding that unusual notice caused omission, appellate allowance is improper and any necessary fact development should be remanded.

#### United States v. McDermott, 1993-03-24

Source: [record’s published writings](records/mcdermott_merits_1993-03-24.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | I: priority and remedy. Joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Kennedy, Souter. | Reversal |
| Dissent | Thomas | Entire. Joined by Stevens, O'Connor. | Would affirm |

Thomas, joined by Stevens and O’Connor, would affirm. A docketed, enforceable and noncontingent judgment lien is sufficiently perfected under Vermont even though particular property enters its reach later. The dissent rejects making identification of each future asset an additional priority event; alternatively, simultaneous attachment supplies no reason to favor the federal lien. This is a disagreement about federal priority, not state ownership, and is noncontrolling.

#### Arave v. Creech, 1993-03-30

Source: [record’s published writings](records/arave_merits_1993-03-30.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | I–II: facial validity; III: limited remedy. Joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Reverse in part and remand |
| Dissent | Blackmun | Entire. Joined by Stevens. | Would affirm facial invalidity |
| Dissent | Stone-Zsela | Entire. No other join. | Would affirm facial invalidity on the stated construction ground |

Blackmun, joined by Stevens, would affirm because the overlapping descriptions in Osborn do not provide a coherent boundary that a reviewing court can enforce. Godfrey, Maynard and Blackmun’s Walton dissent demand a State’s actual limiting principle, not a federal reconstruction of rhetoric. The violent-rage finding illustrates the instability but is not converted into an independent as-applied holding. Stone-Zsela separately would affirm on the same limited facial question: objective proof of mental state is possible, but that possibility does not define the line among emotional detachment, lack of sympathy, calculated intent and furious violence. Pitilessness does not itself narrow. His dissent preserves judicial narrowing as a permissible method and valid mental-state aggravators as a category. Both dissents leave the conviction and independent resentencing relief undisturbed. None controls.

#### Delaware v. New York, 1993-03-30

Source: [record’s published writings](records/delaware_original_exceptions_1993-03-30.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | I–III: debts, priorities, proof and remand. Joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter. | Partial exceptions disposition |
| Dissent | White | Entire. Joined by Blackmun, Stevens. | Would follow the Master’s contrary allocation |

White, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, would accept the Master’s equitable approach. In their view, the issuer and the location of principal executive offices provide a more meaningful connection to these abandoned distributions than the incorporation of a securities intermediary. The dissent objects that the majority’s formal rules can distribute funds by corporate happenstance. It does not establish a competing controlling allocation or decide the private ownership claims reserved by the Court.

#### Conroy v. Aniskoff, 1993-03-31

Source: [record’s published writings](records/conroy_merits_1993-03-31.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | I: statutory command, context and remedy. Joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Reversal; Scalia concurs in judgment |
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | II: legislative-history confirmation. Joined by White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Additional explanation; Stone does not join |
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | III: response defending legislative-intent inquiry. Joined by White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter. | Additional explanation; neither Stone nor Thomas joins |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Entire. No other join. | Same tolling result from enacted text |

Scalia concurs in the judgment because the enacted command resolves the case. He objects that searching legislative history after finding an unambiguous statute invites selective reliance and needlessly burdens litigants. Stevens’s additional discussion, joined as specified in the topology, regards the enactment history as confirmation of the statutory distinction and rejects the claim that this result is absurd. Thomas does not join the separate defense of legislative-intent inquiry; Stone joins only the operative statutory analysis. These methodological differences create no exception to the tolling rule.

#### United States v. Texas, 1993-04-05

Source: [record’s published writings](records/texas_merits_1993-04-05.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | White | I: common-law obligation; II: non-displacement and remand. Joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Reversal |
| Dissent | Stevens | Entire. No other join. | Would affirm |

Stevens would affirm. North Carolina and Board of Commissioners demonstrate that state interest liability was not a long-settled general rule when Congress excluded States in 1982. West Virginia came five years later. He regards today’s extension to a strict reimbursement obligation as an affirmative development rather than merely preservation of established state liability, and would honor the statutory exclusion accordingly. His view is not sovereign immunity against the United States and does not deny the undisputed principal debt. The majority acknowledges the temporal objection but disagrees about displacement.

#### Hazen Paper Co. v. Biggins, 1993-04-20

Source: [record’s published writings](records/hazen_merits_1993-04-20.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | I: liability; II: willfulness; III: remand. Joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Unanimous vacatur |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Entire. Joined by Thomas. | Joins the Court; stresses unresolved disparate impact |

Kennedy, joined by Thomas, concurs while emphasizing that Biggins brought disparate treatment alone. He regards importing Title VII disparate-impact doctrine into the ADEA as presenting substantial unresolved objections and understands the Court to decide none of them. The Court’s express reservation supports his join; his skepticism is not a holding. Stone does not join this additional theory.

#### Newark Morning Ledger Co. v. United States, 1993-04-20

Source: [record’s published writings](records/newark_merits_1993-04-20.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Blackmun | I: governing rule; II: proved asset and remand. Joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas. | Reversal |
| Dissent | Souter | Entire. Joined by White, Scalia. | Would affirm |

Souter, joined by White and Scalia, would affirm on two grounds. First, the regulation categorically denies depreciation of goodwill, and at-will subscribers’ expected continued purchases are that goodwill; redefining it as whatever remains after measurable assets are removed changes the accepted rule. Second, even under the Court’s test, measuring how long existing subscribers continue does not establish how long the purchased pre-sale goodwill lasts rather than being sustained by post-sale editorial and business efforts. The conditional stipulation concedes neither issue. These independent objections are noncontrolling; the majority accepts the trial findings under the proof-based construction.

#### Brecht v. Abrahamson, 1993-04-21

Source: [record’s published writings](records/brecht_merits_1993-04-21.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | White | I: constitutional trial error. Joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Unanimous classification |
| Opinion of the Court | White | II–III: Chapman for this claim; vacatur and conditional-remedy directions. Joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O'Connor, Souter. | Five-Justice controlling rule |
| Concurrence | White | A: broader continuity argument. Joined by Blackmun, Souter. | Souter joins without taking a position on Powell’s merits |
| Concurrence | White | B: objection to rigid structural/trial taxonomy. Joined by Blackmun. | Noncontrolling methodological reservation |
| Concurrence | O'Connor | Entire. No other join. | Reliability and administration grounds; joins the Court |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Entire. Joined by Scalia, Thomas. | Would affirm using Kotteakos |
| Dissent | Stevens | Entire. No other join. | Would affirm using demanding whole-record Kotteakos review |

White, joined by Blackmun and by Souter in Part A with an express reservation of Powell’s merits, concurs that section 2254 and the Court’s constitutional practice more broadly resist giving the same error different remedial force solely because direct review has ended. White and Blackmun alone also question the reliability of a rigid trial-error/structural-defect taxonomy; that reservation does not undo their join in the classification of this Doyle error. O’Connor concurs on the importance of confidence in verdict accuracy and the administrative costs of multiplying harmless-error categories. She accepts that direct and collateral review can differ, but finds no sufficient justification for diluting Chapman here.

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would affirm. Finality, comity and the limited collateral role justify asking whether the violation substantially and injuriously affected the verdict under Kotteakos. Lawful prewarning impeachment and the physical and circumstantial evidence make the improper references cumulative on this record. The dissent does not recast Doyle as nonconstitutional or treat structural defects as ordinary trial errors. It leaves open, without adopting an exception, whether an especially egregious deliberate error or a pattern of misconduct could independently undermine the proceeding’s integrity despite no substantial verdict effect. Stevens separately would affirm under Kotteakos, as anticipated by his Greer concurrence. He emphasizes a demanding independent review of the entire record, the prosecution’s obligation to explain harmlessness and the error’s actual influence on the factfinder rather than a bare sufficiency-of-evidence test. His reasoning and the principal dissent remain noncontrolling.

#### Withrow v. Williams, 1993-04-21

Source: [record’s published writings](records/withrow_merits_1993-04-21.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | I–II: Miranda review and corresponding remand. Joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy. | Six-Justice holding |
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | III: notice and opportunity on the independent theory. Joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. | Unanimous holding |
| Concurrence in part and dissent | O'Connor | Entire. No other join. | Joins III; would extend Powell |
| Concurrence in part and dissent | Scalia | Entire. Joined by Thomas. | Joins III; would assess exceptional equitable grounds before relitigation |

O’Connor concurs in the notice reversal and dissents on Miranda review. Following her Duckworth concurrence, she would extend Powell to a claim involving voluntary but unwarned statements after a full and fair state opportunity. She regards involuntariness review as the appropriate means to remedy genuine coercion and the added deterrent value of later Miranda review as insufficient to outweigh finality and retrial costs. This does not reject collateral review of actual compelled statements.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, also joins the notice ruling but would remand for a determination whether unusual equitable circumstances justify renewed Miranda litigation after the opportunities already afforded. He treats prior full and fair litigation as a powerful equitable consideration under sections 2241 and 2243, subject to considerations concerning fairness or accuracy, rather than a jurisdictional defect. The separate writing urges a broader approach beyond this case; the Court does not adopt it. It does not cite an unentered historical Brecht rule or convert the simulated Herrera’s limited record holding into a categorical innocence bar.

#### CSX Transportation, Inc. v. Easterwood, 1993-04-21

Source: [record’s published writings](records/easterwood_merits_1993-04-21.md).

| Writing | Author | Parts and exact joins | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | White | I: section 434 coverage; II: warning devices. Joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. | Unanimous warning disposition and governing coverage rule |
| Opinion of the Court | White | III–IV: ordinary speed application and disposition. Joined by Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy. | Six-Justice speed holding |
| Concurrence in part and dissent | Thomas | Entire. Joined by Souter. | Joins I–II; would allow speed claim |
| Concurrence in part and dissent | Stone-Zsela | Entire. No other join. | Joins I–II; would vacate speed ruling for the bounded inquiry |

Thomas, joined by Souter, concurs on warning devices and dissents on speed. He reads the track-class maximum as addressing track safety rather than substantially subsuming every crossing-specific speed duty. The contemporaneous rulemaking’s exclusion of population and crossing variables, and the reservation of other slowing duties, defeat broader coverage in his view. He would let the pleaded speed claim proceed; he does not announce liability.

Stone-Zsela concurs on warning devices and the statutory coverage method but would vacate the categorical speed ruling for a limited inquiry into the duty actually pleaded and preserved. A demand merely to substitute a lower ordinary operating-speed limit remains preempted. A distinct preserved crossing-condition duty may proceed only if its subject is not substantially covered by operative regulations; general time-and-place allegations do not establish that distinction. The remand may not manufacture a new theory or treat separately pleaded vegetation or hump issues as an unestablished speed claim. If no qualifying theory exists, speed preemption remains. He leaves the specific-individual-hazard question reserved and requires independent satisfaction of every local-hazard condition for any reliance on that saving clause. His remand position is neither the six-Justice affirmance nor the two-Justice position allowing the speed claim outright, and it is noncontrolling.

#### United States v. Olano — 1993-04-26

Kennedy joins the Court. He emphasizes that the defendant’s burden is the usual framework, not necessarily the formulation for an error noticed by the court on its own initiative. Rule 24(c) remains independently obligatory; trial judges should not put counsel in the position of resisting a proposed departure from the prescribed jury structure. A preserved objection would place the burden of showing harmlessness on the Government, which could face substantial difficulty. None of those observations permits withholding an objection and demanding automatic reversal later.

Stevens, joined by White and Blackmun, would affirm. Drawing on Vasquez v. Hillery and Gray v. Mississippi, he regards the privacy of deliberations as protecting the tribunal’s integrity independently of measurable verdict prejudice. He nonetheless distinguishes satisfaction of substantial rights from discretionary relief. A reasonable judge could decline reversal after considering the nature of the violation, counsel’s conduct and likely effect; his conclusion is that the Ninth Circuit did not abuse its broad supervisory discretion by granting relief here.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/olano_merits_1993-04-26.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### Edenfield v. Fane — 1993-04-26

Blackmun joins the opinion and judgment but maintains that truthful, nonmisleading commercial speech deserves more protection than the intermediate framework affords; that broader view is not adopted. O’Connor dissents. Her Shapero and Peel positions support attention to the culture and responsibilities of a profession, not simply the sophistication of a particular prospective client. She would allow Florida to protect independence and prevent pressure through a prophylactic restriction without waiting for proven abuse. In her view the Court understates experience-based risks and permits an injunction extending to business solicitation generally on a record centered on Fane’s proposed conduct. She would reverse; no Justice joins her writing.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/edenfield_merits_1993-04-26.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### United States v. Idaho ex rel. Director, Idaho Department of Water Resources — 1993-05-03
