Stone, joined by Stevens, agrees that the assessments cannot be imposed. Idaho finances the adjudication by substituting the challenged assessment for conventional costs; Congress expressly excluded judgments for costs. That textual ground suffices. The concurrence would not turn the result into a general rule that every procedural fee requires a separately phrased waiver. Nor would it permit the Government to avoid ordinary state procedures or the comprehensive adjudication itself. The concurrence supports the cost-characterization ground and judgment, but does not join the Court’s broader monetary-waiver reasoning. Stevens’ position is consistent with his Nordic Village dissent because the present statute contains an express exclusion.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/idaho_merits_1993-05-03.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### Stinson v. United States — 1993-05-03

Stone concurs in the judgment, independently reading the applicable guideline to exclude felon possession alone. He preserves Williams’ rule for departure policy statements and the statutory role of commentary in assessing considered departure factors. Those rules do not establish mandatory deference to every later interpretation of a classification provision. Guidelines undergo congressional review under §994(p), while commentary changes do not; judicial interpretation must remain independently justified. Under a Skidmore-type approach the Commission’s expertise, consistency, text and reasoning receive substantial weight. Stone does not limit crime of violence to offenses with force elements: the serious-risk clause must be read with burglary of a dwelling, arson, extortion and use of explosives. Possession alone does not establish comparable inherent risk merely because a prohibited possessor might later misuse the firearm. That legal error, rather than a request simply to reconsider commentary already considered, supports vacatur. An explanation can illuminate what original text meant without retroactively creating a rule; a substantive later change must satisfy its own temporal conditions. The lower court must identify the governing version, address the preserved temporal argument and any properly supported alternative ground, and apply Williams to the error’s consequences. No particular sentence or final-case reopening follows. No associate joins this writing; its persuasive-weight framework is noncontrolling.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/stinson_merits_1993-05-03.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### Professional Real Estate Investors, Inc. v. Columbia Pictures Industries, Inc. — 1993-05-03

Souter joins the Court. Objective probable cause provides a useful threshold, but the decision does not incorporate every detail of malicious-prosecution law or Rule11 practice into antitrust immunity. Stevens, joined by O’Connor, agrees that this tenable copyright action cannot sustain the counterclaim. He regards the broader formulation as unnecessary to that result and warns that objectively plausible proceedings can sometimes be used as part of abusive conduct. A pattern, imposed burdens or conduct distinct from a genuine attempt to obtain relief may require a different inquiry. The concurrence would decide this record without foreclosing those possibilities. Neither separate writing changes the seven-Justice rule governing the single suit and theory actually reviewed.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/pre_merits_1993-05-03.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### Cardinal Chemical Co. v. Morton International, Inc. — 1993-05-17

Scalia, joined by Souter, joins PartsI–III and the judgment. He agrees that the independent validity counterclaim is not automatically moot and that the jurisdictional premise of the Federal Circuit’s practice fails. He does not join PartIV. Because both parties oppose the automatic practice, the scope of appellate discretion has not received the same adversary development. He would remand for the Federal Circuit to exercise genuine discretion in the first instance, while rejecting a conclusion that jurisdiction itself forces every validity question to be decided. The seven-Justice PartIV nevertheless governs this record, with its express preservation of unusual case-specific grounds.

Source: published writings in [Decision Record](records/cardinal_merits_1993-05-17.md); all separate propositions are noncontrolling except for the Court joins expressly identified in Current Law.

#### Deal v. United States — 1993-05-17

**Dissent — Stevens; joined by Blackmun, O'Connor:** Stevens, joined by Blackmun and O’Connor, reads the phrase as a recidivist provision addressing renewed offending after a prior conviction. The long-established operation of repeat-offender statutes and the development of §924(c) matter more than abstractly counting findings of guilt. Bell, Simpson and Busic support resolving the remaining uncertainty against multiplying the aggravated tier in the first prosecution. The dissent objects that the Court’s construction makes the enhancement depend on the prosecutor’s decision to collect counts in one case and produces repeated enhanced punishment without an intervening opportunity to conform conduct after conviction. It would preserve the underlying convictions and consecutive base penalties. This view does not control. Stevens’s discussion invokes an earlier conviction becoming final; Stone’s distinct dissent expressly leaves appellate finality undecided and is not a join in that language.

**Dissent — Stone-Zsela; no other joins:** Stone-Zsela would require a qualifying conviction before commission of the later offense. He acknowledges that the Government’s ordinary ordinal reading is substantial, but reads the phrase in its recidivist setting and statutory development. Bell, Simpson and Busic support disciplined construction without themselves deciding the present phrase. Residual uncertainty is resolved through lenity after considering those tools. The proposed remedy removes only the aggravated tiers, retaining all six convictions, the consecutive-service command and unrelated sentencing components. He does not decide that the prior conviction must be final on appeal and does not adopt a proportionality limit. His separate writing is noncontrolling.

#### Commissioner v. Keystone Consolidated Industries, Inc. — 1993-05-24

**Dissent — Stevens; no other joins:** Stevens would affirm because income-tax treatment does not necessarily settle the different prohibited-transaction provision. Both voluntary property contributions and transfers satisfying minimum funding can produce income-tax gain; borrowing that classification selectively gives insufficient weight to ERISA’s context. The statutory emphasis on encumbered transfers and the distinction between genuine plan funding and transactions extracting plan assets support the narrower reading. Trustees remain responsible for deciding whether to accept suitable property, which can benefit a plan rather than threaten it. Atlantic Cleaners cautions that identical words may bear different meanings in different settings. This disagreement concerns statutory classification, not a finding that Keystone’s properties were misvalued, a general exemption for every employer transaction, or an order computing tax. The dissent does not control.

Scalia and Stone withhold the Court’s supporting policy portion while joining the ordinary-exchange rule; no separate policy rule or additional opinion is implied.

#### Keene Corp. v. United States — 1993-05-24

**Dissent — Stevens; no other joins:** Stevens reads the pendency language in light of the statute’s purpose of preventing simultaneous pursuit of duplicative claims. Once the other action has ended, that purpose no longer requires closing the remaining forum. He objects that a rigid filing-time rule can defeat adjudication even though neither court is then considering a competing suit and each forum initially had different jurisdictional limits. The lower decisions permitting a plaintiff to remove the obstacle before adjudication reinforce his treatment of the statute as an election rather than an irrevocable trap. He would not approve duplicate recovery or concurrent adjudication; his disagreement concerns the ability to proceed after the competing action ends. The dissent supplies no current exception to the Court’s filing-date rule.

#### Smith v. United States — 1993-06-01

**Concurrence — Blackmun; no other joins:** Blackmun joins the Court’s construction and its application to the offered exchange. He emphasizes that this firearm was tendered as the consideration for the cocaine, so its relationship to the charged trafficking was established without deciding every marginal form of facilitation. He does not understand the opinion’s minimum formulation to make facilitation alone sufficient in every case. That reservation preserves the separate relation-to element and leaves its outer boundary open; it does not withhold a vote from the result or from the Court’s rule for this transaction.

**Dissent — Scalia; joined by Stevens, Souter:** Scalia, joined by Stevens and Souter, reads the entire phrase in its ordinary setting: using a firearm means employing it as a weapon, not merely doing something useful with it. The argument does not restrict weapon use to firing; threats or striking can be weapon employment. The broader transaction language in the forfeiture provision addresses different objects and does not establish the criminal element in §924(c). Treating the gun as merchandise lacks the contextual weapon use charged here, even if trading it assists the drug transaction. The separate relation-to requirement cannot cure that missing element. The dissent would reverse the firearm-use conviction and cannot sustain it on carrying, which was not charged. It does not control.

**Dissent — Stone-Zsela; no other joins:** Stone-Zsela accepts the force of the Government’s instrumental-use reading but finds that the criminal phrase in context addresses employment as a weapon. Perrin and Timbers require contextual ordinary meaning; Bass and Moskal call for lenity only after the ordinary tools leave genuine uncertainty. The independent connection to trafficking does not answer whether the gun was used in the charged sense. Threats, intimidation or striking may qualify without firing, whereas merchandise-only barter does not. He would remove the firearm count and its thirty-year mandatory term and remand for the resulting sentence correction. The separate drug convictions remain, and the remand would not authorize conviction for uncharged carrying or a new trial to cure the rejected legal theory. This position is noncontrolling.

#### Mertens v. Hewitt Associates — 1993-06-01

**Dissent — White; joined by Stevens, O'Connor:** White, joined by Stevens and O’Connor, reads equitable relief in the particular trust-law setting that ERISA incorporates. Firestone makes that background central, and Clews illustrates the traditional power of equity to restore trust losses through a monetary award, including against a knowing participant. The dissent regards the majority’s general legal-versus-equitable categories as an insufficient account of this particular remedy. It argues that the equitable limitation still matters because punitive relief and remedies arising only from the later merger of law and equity were not traditional trust restoration. Russell does not decide the different §502(a)(3) issue. The express redress language and §502(l)’s treatment of knowing participants further support a claim against a participating nonfiduciary without making every professional a fiduciary. This position would remove the categorical dismissal, not establish liability or an amount of recovery. It does not control.

**Dissent — Stone-Zsela; no other joins:** Stone-Zsela would permit traditional monetary restoration for losses caused by actual knowing participation in an ERISA fiduciary’s breach. Firestone supports consulting trust law, and Clews identifies this restorative remedy as equitable; the argument is not that every award once obtainable incidentally in equity becomes available. Russell’s respect for the statutory enforcement structure remains, but does not settle the distinct redress provision presented here. Section502(a)(3)’s express authority to redress the fiduciary violation supplies the bounded cause; §502(l) offers context without creating an independent private action. Hewitt’s disclaimer is a presentation limit, not a jurisdictional flaw. On remand the participants would still need to prove breach, actual knowing participation and causal loss, and the court would determine the proper amount and recipient within equitable limits. No professional-negligence or punitive claim is authorized. Fixed nonfiduciary status, unchallenged restitution, unappealed parties and claims, and the separate malpractice disposition remain closed. His conclusion on statutory coverage is a dissenting proposition, not an answer supplied by the Court.

#### Musick, Peeler & Garrett v. Employers Insurance of Wausau — 1993-06-01

**Dissent — Thomas; joined by Blackmun, O'Connor:** Thomas, joined by Blackmun and O’Connor, distinguishes the accepted private action of an injured investor from a new action by a wrongdoer seeking reimbursement. Northwest Airlines and Texas Industries require congressional authorization rather than a judicial preference for fair allocation. The existence of contribution in other express securities sections shows that Congress knew how to provide it; it does not itself enact a contribution right under §10(b). Lampf’s task of defining a limitation period for a received action does not necessarily justify creating a distinct claimant and claim. The dissent would reject the asserted federal contribution right without deciding whether these professionals committed securities violations or fixing anyone’s share of loss. Its disagreement concerns remedial authority, not a general rule immunizing advisers from their own otherwise established liability. It is noncontrolling.

#### FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc. — 1993-06-01

[Source writing and exact joins](records/beach_merits_1993-06-01.md). Stevens, concurring in the judgment, accepts a concrete distinction between an owner’s private use of property and reaching beyond that property to participate in a regulated market. He doubts that hypothesized system size or competitive advantage, detached from the actual statutory distinction, provides a satisfactory account. Fritz’s presumption of legitimate, impartial legislative action does not in his view make any imagined relationship sufficient regardless of context. His narrower explanation supports reversal but is noncontrolling. Stone’s partial joins are recorded in the topology; no separate Stone opinion is published.

#### Nobelman v. American Savings Bank — 1993-06-01

[Source writing and exact joins](records/nobelman_merits_1993-06-01.md). Stevens joins the Court and separately explains that favorable treatment of residential lenders can encourage the flow of capital into the home-lending market. That explanation supports the statutory design but is not an independent holding, an expansion to all secured lenders or a substitute for the principal-residence and remaining-secured-value predicates.

#### Lamb's Chapel v. Center Moriches Union Free School District — 1993-06-07

[Source writing and exact joins](records/lambs_chapel_merits_1993-06-07.md). Kennedy concurs in part and in the judgment. He accepts the viewpoint holding and the absence of establishment where the school merely permits nonsponsored private use. He declines to endorse the Lemon/endorsement formulation, which is unnecessary to his conclusion; his narrower ground is noncontrolling as a separate writing. Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurs in the judgment. They regard equal access as a permissible neutral accommodation and agree that the district cannot discriminate against a religious perspective. They reject reliance on Lemon and distinguish government establishment of a sect from neutral permission for religious expression. Neither writing supplies five votes to replace the inherited test. Souter joins the Court without a separate opinion.

#### Minnesota v. Dickerson — 1993-06-07

[Source writing and exact joins](records/dickerson_merits_1993-06-07.md). Blackmun, joined by Thomas, agrees that the case is live and that touch can supply probable cause within a lawful frisk. They would vacate and remand because findings made under a categorical no-touch theory may not precisely establish when lawful contact supplied probable cause. They do not vote to uphold the seizure on the current record or authorize manipulation after excluding a weapon. Their proposed remand is noncontrolling. Scalia joins all portions of the Court opinion and separately questions the historical foundation of Terry’s frisk authority. Because the initial stop and frisk are not challenged here, he accepts that premise and does not supply a vote to overrule Terry or a new historical test for later cases.

#### Gilmore v. Taylor — 1993-06-07

[Source writing and exact joins](records/gilmore_merits_1993-06-07.md). Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, joins the Court and emphasizes that Eddings and related capital-sentencing cases do not automatically dictate the sequencing of murder and affirmative-defense instructions in a noncapital trial. Patterson and Martin allow distinctions that the proposed analogy would obscure. Their concurrence adds no holding that every defense-instruction error is constitutionally irrelevant. Blackmun, joined by Stevens, dissents. Cupp’s protection against a fundamentally unfair charge, in their view, reaches instructions that permit jurors to return the greater verdict without evaluating the only basis for a lesser offense. The accused’s admission to the killing cannot fairly be separated from his offered explanation by a misleading route through the charge. They also regard the protection as within Teague’s watershed exception even if the rule is new, because preventing that bypass bears directly on reliable adjudication of the grade of guilt. They would preserve the Seventh Circuit’s conditional writ requiring Illinois to elect retrial within 120 days; that remedy is not the Court’s mandate.

#### Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala — 1993-06-07

[Source writing and exact joins](records/good_samaritan_merits_1993-06-07.md). Souter, joined by Stevens and Scalia, dissents on the statutory text. Clause (ii) speaks of methods that produce inadequate or excessive aggregate reimbursement for a provider and period, not merely estimates pending final settlement. Section 1395g already authorizes adjustment of interim payments, so assigning that same work to clause (ii) deprives its distinct language of effect. Clause (i)’s no-cost-shifting requirement reinforces a final reimbursement obligation. Aggregate treatment prevents a right to reimbursement for every isolated cost item, but it still requires correction of methods producing an inadequate total. On that reading the statute is unambiguous and Chevron cannot authorize the Secretary’s contrary construction. The dissent does not rest on a categorical refusal to defer to agencies that change position. It would reverse the Eighth Circuit’s denial of the asserted adjustment right; that proposed result is noncontrolling.

#### Church of the Lukumi Babalu Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah — 1993-06-11

[Source writing and exact joins](records/lukumi_merits_1993-06-11.md). Kennedy’s Part II-A-2, joined by Stone-Zsela and Stevens, treats the sequence of enactment and contemporaneous official statements as corroboration of the objectively established discriminatory object under Arlington Heights and Feeney. Public hostility is not converted into a finding that every speaker’s intent belonged to the council. That discussion has three joins and supplies no controlling motive test. Scalia concurs in part and in the judgment. He regards neutrality and general applicability as substantially overlapping and rejects the attempt to identify a single subjective motive in a collective legislature, consistent with his Edwards dissent. A law objectively targeting religious practice fails even if its sponsors profess only humane concerns; he joins all of Kennedy’s opinion except II-A-2. Souter concurs in part and in the judgment, joining I, III and IV. He distinguishes the settled prohibition on persecution from Smith’s broader claim that genuinely neutral and general laws need no free-exercise justification. In his view Smith’s relation to Sherbert, Yoder and the unemployment cases, its briefing history and its textual foundation warrant reconsideration in a case that requires it. He neither overrules Smith here nor claims novelty alone permits abandoning precedent; the entered Casey approach to stare decisis remains a separate, contextual discipline. Blackmun, joined by O’Connor, concurs in the judgment. They would require compelling justification and the least restrictive available means for substantial burdens on religious exercise even from general laws. They regard discrimination of this kind as necessarily failing that standard, while preserving the seriousness of animal-welfare concerns and reserving whether a sincere general anti-cruelty law must provide an exemption. These additional approaches remain noncontrolling.

#### Wisconsin v. Mitchell — 1993-06-11

[Source writing and exact joins](records/mitchell_merits_1993-06-11.md). No separate writing is published. Stone’s limited join and disposition-only agreement on the distinct overbreadth component are shown in the topology; they supply no competing public theory.

#### United States Department of Treasury v. Fabe — 1993-06-11

[Source writing and exact joins](records/fabe_merits_1993-06-11.md). Kennedy, joined by Scalia, Souter and Thomas, dissents from protecting any of the competing Ohio priorities against the United States. National Securities, Royal Drug and Pireno, in their view, concern the contractual transfer and spreading of risk between insurer and insured. Distribution after the company fails regulates relations among creditors, including policyholders as creditors, rather than that insurance transaction. The repeated phrase business of insurance should retain a consistent meaning across §1012(b), even though the two clauses address laws and activities differently. They object that the majority’s partial protection selects an appealing distribution without establishing why this priority statute regulates the insurance relationship in one class but not another. They accept that one broader enactment can have genuinely different regulatory functions, as National Securities illustrates, but deny that the competing priorities here supply that distinction. States remain free to provide liquidation procedures because insurers are excluded from the ordinary federal bankruptcy regime; those procedures must nevertheless honor federal priority. Their proposed full reversal is noncontrolling as to policyholders and administration, while reaching the same result as the Court for ordinary wages and general creditors.

#### Northeastern Florida Chapter, AGC v. Jacksonville — 1993-06-14

[Published positions in the record](records/Northeastern_Florida_Chapter_AGC_v_Jacksonville_merits_1993-06-14.md).

O’Connor, joined by Blackmun, would treat the substantially revised ordinance as a new controversy. The changed eligibility categories, goals and mechanisms require their own record; similarity in a general preference is not enough. They would vacate the old judgment and permit litigation over the new program. Their objection concerns mootness and the proper vehicle, not a holding that a ready competitor must prove a winning bid.

#### South Dakota v. Bourland — 1993-06-14

[Published positions in the record](records/South_Dakota_v_Bourland_merits_1993-06-14.md).

Blackmun, joined by Souter, dissents. Dion requires evidence that Congress considered the disputed treaty right and chose abrogation. Building a dam and assuring recreation can coexist with conservation regulation; loss of absolute exclusion does not prove loss of every incident of territorial sovereignty. Wheeler and Indian-law construction principles support retention absent congressional action or necessary incompatibility. The compensation settlement does not identify every surrendered governmental power. Superior federal project law remains controlling.

Stone-Zsela separately dissents on the treaty ground. He would sustain compatible hunting-and-fishing regulation on former trust land because public acquisition removes exclusive possession without clearly removing this distinct power. He does not require express words in every statute, treat retained personal privileges as a new delegation, or decree exclusive tribal control of access. He preserves the fee-land Montana inquiry and adjudication of concrete conflicts with federal access requirements.

#### Local 144 Nursing Home Pension Fund v. Demisay — 1993-06-14

[Published positions in the record](records/Local_144_Nursing_Home_Pension_Fund_v_Demisay_merits_1993-06-14.md).

Stevens, joined by White and Blackmun, concurs only in the judgment. Section302(c)(5) expressly permits pooled funds to benefit employees of other contributing employers. Retention thus creates no structural violation even if courts can enforce continuing compliance with the express statutory standards. Their reading of Arroyo, Amax and Robinson preserves that enforcement power without authorizing free-ranging fiduciary reasonableness review. They regard the Court’s broader supervision holding as unnecessary.

#### Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California — 1993-06-14

[Published positions in the record](records/Concrete_Pipe_and_Products_of_California_Inc_v_Construction_Laborers_Pension_Trust_for_Southern_California_merits_1993-06-14.md).

O’Connor concurs. Her Connolly position requires a basis in the employer’s conduct for attributing benefit expectations to it. She reserves whether pre-MPPAA entry, the original thirty-percent cap, or entry before ERISA could support a different as-applied case; regulation alone cannot answer every expectations claim. Those broader issues are not decided here.

Thomas joins the assessment, actuarial and economic holdings but concurs only in the judgment on §1401(a)(3)(A). He reads preponderance as the standard of proof and unreasonable or clearly erroneous as what must be shown, rather than mutually inconsistent probability measures. He objects to removing those enacted terms through avoidance and finds the resulting statutory burden permissible. Scalia joins the Court’s construction but not its legislative-history discussion; he does not join Thomas’s separate writing.

#### Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills School District — 1993-06-18

[Published positions in the record](records/Zobrest_v_Catalina_Foothills_School_District_merits_1993-06-18.md).

Blackmun, joined by Souter, concurs in the remand. If constitutional decision becomes necessary, direct public transmission of inseparably sectarian instruction presents a concern under Meek, Wolman and Ball distinct from private use of a general benefit. Neither Justice would treat neutrality alone as conclusive on attribution. This conditional discussion supplies no establishment holding.

White, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, dissents. The district chose the constitutional ground below and its new alternatives should not postpone decision. Mueller and Witters support a neutrally available service delivered to a child through parental choice. An interpreter translates rather than independently teaches or sponsors religion, and the aid reaches the school only because the parents chose it. They would reverse the federal constitutional ruling, without necessarily adjudicating every independent reimbursement entitlement.

#### Helling v. McKinney — 1993-06-18

[Published positions in the record](records/Helling_v_McKinney_merits_1993-06-18.md).

Thomas, joined by Scalia, dissents. The Cruel and Unusual Punishments Clause concerns punishment imposed through the criminal process, and existing conditions cases do not justify extending it to uncertain future injury in these circumstances. Their restrictive position continues the punishment-boundary concern in their Hudson dissent. They would not constitutionalize every hazardous prison condition through a civil-rights action. The Court retains the deliberate-indifference requirement but rejects their categorical objection to this future-risk claim.

#### Reno v. Catholic Social Services, Inc. — 1993-06-18

[Published positions in the record](records/Reno_v_Catholic_Social_Services_Inc_merits_1993-06-18.md).

O’Connor concurs in the judgment. She regards the request to extend an already expired application period as ripe: closure has occurred, and its effect is not contingent. But §1255a(a)(1)(A) requires a timely application. Promulgating an erroneous benefits regulation, without more, is not the affirmative misconduct needed to justify this broad equitable extension. Irwin, Hansen and Pangilinan preserve statutory conditions. She agrees that actual front-desking may support processing a timely application and further inquiry; she does not adopt the Court’s ripeness analysis or a categorical total-review bar.

Stevens, joined by White and Blackmun, dissents. The rules concretely discouraged eligible persons from applying during a finite statutory opportunity. Under Abbott and McNary, the policy challenges and class relief should not depend on proving an individual futile visit to the front desk. They would preserve an effective opportunity to apply while leaving individual eligibility for administrative determination.

#### Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation — 1993-06-18

[Published positions in the record](records/Harper_v_Virginia_Department_of_Taxation_merits_1993-06-18.md).

Kennedy, joined by White, concurs in the judgment and joins the background and remedial portion. Davis was sufficiently grounded in existing federal tax-immunity law that its application to these claims is required without eliminating every role for Chevron Oil. They decline the broader general retroactivity formulation.

Scalia joins the Court and explains that judicial decisions declare the governing law; policy-based temporal lawmaking is inconsistent with that role. His broader theoretical account does not enlarge the Court’s stated trigger or reopen final judgments.

O’Connor dissents. Her Beam position preserves Chevron Oil’s attention to a genuinely new rule, reliance and disruption in civil cases. She would sustain Virginia’s prospective treatment, regarding Michigan’s conceded refund as insufficient to compel the general retroactivity conclusion. Her position does not command the Court.

#### Sale v. Haitian Centers Council — 1993-06-21

[Published positions in the record](records/Sale_v_Haitian_Centers_Council_merits_1993-06-21.md).

Blackmun dissents. The protection against return speaks to the threatened destination and the Government’s act, and the removal of the territorial qualifier reinforces that reading. The ordinary meaning and protective treaty context rebut the majority’s territorial inference. The dissent does not convert withholding into an entitlement to enter the United States.

Stone-Zsela separately dissents on the statute. The combined deletion, use of return in addition to deportation, and coordinated American immigration action supply the indication Aramco requires. The Attorney General’s responsibility cannot be avoided simply by assigning physical performance to another component. Stone would affirm rejection of categorical offshore exclusion and remand for actual preliminary-injunction requirements, identified claimants and responsible officers, and meaningful determination before compelled return. The statutory exceptions remain: ordering, inciting, assisting or otherwise participating in persecution on a protected ground; conviction by final judgment of a particularly serious crime and danger to the United States community; serious reasons to consider that the alien committed a serious nonpolitical crime outside the United States before arrival; and reasonable grounds to regard the alien as a danger to United States security. An aggravated-felony conviction is statutorily treated as a particularly serious crime. The separate §243(h)(1) exclusion for aliens described in §1251(a)(4)(D), concerning Nazi persecution or genocide, also remains. The protection concerns threats to life or freedom because of race, religion, nationality, membership in a particular social group or political opinion. No blanket refugee finding, right of admission, worldwide rescue obligation, fixed interview duration or personal presidential injunction follows. Lawful interdiction, maritime safety, lawful offshore screening and safe-destination arrangements remain possible. Independent treaty self-execution is reserved.

#### Brooke Group v. Brown & Williamson — 1993-06-21

[Published positions in the record](records/Brooke_Group_v_Brown_and_Williamson_merits_1993-06-21.md).

Stevens, joined by White and Blackmun, dissents from the sufficiency application. The documented discipline campaign, industry pricing history, subsequent increases and expert evidence permit a jury inference of recoupment when taken together and viewed favorably to the verdict. They accept a competitive-injury requirement and reject automatic liability from intent, but regard the Court as resolving competing factual inferences itself. They would reverse the judgment as a matter of law.

Stone-Zsela separately dissents. He would vacate and remand because the appellate judgment must be reconsidered without a categorical oligopoly obstacle and with favorable reasonable inferences from the whole record. He does not decide that every required recoupment link has been proved. Excess capacity, entry, Reynolds’ incentives, discounts, duration and the need to recover the predator’s own losses remain serious contrary evidence. The reviewing court must test the mechanism, and intent documents cannot fill a missing link. The district court’s separate antitrust-injury and causation grounds remain for consideration; defense judgment may still follow an independently sufficient ground. No automatic damages reinstatement, monopoly-only condition, numerical market-share safe harbor or exclusive cost measure is proposed.

#### Darby v. Cisneros — 1993-06-21

[Published positions in the record](records/Darby_v_Cisneros_merits_1993-06-21.md).

Scalia and Thomas join the statutory construction and judgment but not the legislative-history discussion. No separate opinion issues; text and structure independently support the Court’s holding.

#### Johnson v. Texas — 1993-06-24

[Published positions in the record](records/Johnson_v_Texas_merits_1993-06-24.md).

Kennedy, joined by White, Scalia and Thomas, dissents. Youth’s transience permits mitigating effect through future dangerousness, and the instruction to consider all evidence gives an adequate route in their view. They acknowledge that the Court’s Graham holding rejects the premise that partial relevance always suffices; they would restrict or reconsider that holding while acknowledging its present binding force. They distinguish Penry’s disabling evidence and object to requiring an additional route whenever mitigation has independent culpability significance. They do not invoke Teague to bar a direct appeal.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, separately dissents. Their objection concerns the coherence of Penry’s mitigation requirement with Furman’s demand for structured capital discretion. Thomas’s published Graham position supplies the same objection. They would reconsider that expansion, but neither their view nor the four-Justice principal dissent changes the governing rule.

#### Godinez v. Moran — 1993-06-24

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Thomas | Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter | Opinion of the Court: common Dusky capacity rule, independent informed and voluntary waiver, and reversal/remand. | Reverse and remand |
| Thomas | White, O’Connor, Souter | Additional explanation comparing the demands of pleas, counsel waiver and decisions during trial; four-Justice reasoning only. | Supports reversal; not a separate controlling rationale |
| Kennedy | Scalia | Concurrence in part and in the judgment: one competence standard through the criminal proceeding, grounded in historical practice; does not join the comparative-demand explanation. | Reverse and remand |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence: preserve actual-waiver, renewed-inquiry and reliable retrospective-review questions; no automatic validation from the existing findings. | Reverse and remand |
| Blackmun | Stevens | Dissent: capacity to act without counsel requires its own inquiry; this record warrants conditional relief. | Affirm |

Kennedy, joined by Scalia, accepts the common standard and separate waiver requirements but relies on the longstanding application of one competence standard throughout the proceeding rather than comparisons of decisional difficulty. That historical rationale is noncontrolling. Thomas, joined by White, O’Connor and Souter, additionally regards the choices involved in a plea as no more demanding than the combined decisions during a trial; four votes do not make that additional rationale controlling. Stone-Zsela emphasizes that prior reports and express findings do not automatically resolve a later informed-choice or renewed-inquiry claim; he would assess reliable retrospective review and pre-AEDPA treatment of findings before selecting further relief. Blackmun, joined by Stevens, would sustain conditional relief: capacity to assist a lawyer does not adequately test capacity to proceed alone, and Westbrook, Massey and Rees require a context-specific inquiry. The recent suicide attempt, depression, medication and answers at the colloquy make reliance on the earlier reports insufficient in their view.

#### Heller v. Doe — 1993-06-24

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Souter | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor | Opinion of the Court on unequal proof burdens and limited remedial consequence. | Affirm proof invalidation |
| Kennedy | Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Opinion of the Court on rational family-party distinction and procedural due process; separate treatment from proof burdens. | Reverse participation invalidation |
| Kennedy | White, Scalia, Thomas | Dissent on proof burdens: diagnostic history, prediction and treatment differences each justify the different standards. | Would reverse proof invalidation |
| Souter | Blackmun, Stevens | Dissent on family-party equal protection: information can be obtained without adding an adverse party with appeal rights; due process not reached. | Would affirm participation invalidation |
| Blackmun | No other joiners | Separate dissent on participation, also concurring in the proof judgment: preserves preference for heightened review of disability classifications; that tier is not adopted. | Supports proof invalidation and would invalidate participation |

Kennedy, joined by White, Scalia and Thomas, would sustain the proof distinction. They regard easier diagnosis, a longer behavioral history supporting prediction, and generally less intrusive treatment as independently sufficient rational grounds. Addington’s minimum is met, and ordinary review permits imperfect generalizations. Souter, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, would also invalidate family-party status: useful information can be obtained through witnesses, while party status adds an adversary able to call and cross-examine witnesses and appeal a refusal to confine. They find no rational connection between that additional burden and the different condition and therefore do not reach due process. Blackmun separately preserves his view that disability classifications and substantial restraints on liberty warrant heightened review, while accepting that the proof distinction fails the ordinary standard used here. O’Connor joins the proof and participation majorities on their separate grounds; no additional writing is needed.

#### Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — 1993-06-24

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stevens | Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court on functional method, genuine advocacy preparation and press statements. | Unanimous press reversal |
| Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Opinion of the Court on the pleaded preliminary investigation and remand. | Six-Justice fabrication reversal |
| Scalia | No other joiners | Concurrence: immunity requires historical support; mere preparation of evidence without constitutionally cognizable harm may not state a §1983 claim. | Joins Court |
| Kennedy | White, Souter | Concurrence in part and dissent in part: expert consultation is trial preparation even before probable cause; rejects press immunity. | Would affirm fabrication immunity only |

Kennedy, joined by White and Souter, agrees that press statements lack absolute immunity but views the expert consultation as an advocate’s preparation for presenting evidence. Imbler protects preparatory evaluation as well as the eventual use of testimony; denying that protection, in their view, allows an immunity challenge to be relabeled as a challenge to preparation. They reject probable cause as a universal line between investigation and advocacy and would affirm the fabrication ruling. Scalia joins the Court but emphasizes the claimant’s obligation to establish historical immunity. He also distinguishes immunity from an actionable deprivation: mere manufacture of evidence, without constitutionally harmful use, may not establish a §1983 claim. His observation does not decide that remaining question.

#### Shalala v. Schaefer — 1993-06-24

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scalia | Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court on sentence-four finality, prevailing status and timeliness under then-effective Rule 58. | Affirm |
| Scalia | White, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Six-Justice portion limiting Hudson’s administrative-fee route to qualifying retained-jurisdiction proceedings; Stone does not join this additional portion. | Supports statutory distinction |
| Stevens | Blackmun | Concurrence in the judgment: retain Hudson’s broader integrated administrative-fee rule and final fee judgment after remand proceedings. | Affirm |

Stevens, joined by Blackmun, concurs in the judgment but would preserve Hudson’s broader administrative-fee approach. Representation before the agency after a judicial remand may be essential to obtaining the benefits sought; in their view, EAJA’s purposes support retaining authority to enter the final fee judgment after those proceedings. They regard the distinction drawn for immediate appealability in Finkelstein as insufficient to eliminate Hudson’s protection and would reject the conflicting language in Melkonyan. Their position supports affirmance here but does not control the sentence-four rule. Stone-Zsela joins the finality, prevailing-status and missing-entry holdings without joining the additional categorical administrative-fee limitation.

#### United States v. Edge Broadcasting Co. — 1993-06-25

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| White | Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Common introduction, Central Hudson framework and judgment. | Reverse and remand |
| White | Stone-Zsela, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: actual meaningful reduction in local lottery advertising and reasonable fit on this record. | Five directly adopt the controlling as-applied ground |
| White | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Additional category-level advancement and fit analysis, including sufficiency despite merely marginal individual advancement; four votes only. | Noncontrolling broader reasoning |
| White | Scalia, Thomas | Additional concern that successive border exceptions would erode the State-based scheme; three votes only. | Noncontrolling |
| Souter | Kennedy | Concurrence: unnecessary to decide a more lenient level of generality because this application satisfies the test. | Reverse |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence: lawful out-of-state audience is a real burden; federalism identifies the interest but does not replace scrutiny. | Reverse and remand |
| Stevens | Blackmun | Dissent: truthful information about lawful activity in a neighboring State cannot be suppressed on this asserted derivative interest and fit. | Affirm |

White, O’Connor, Scalia and Thomas additionally accept review of general advancement and reasonable fit despite marginal advancement in a particular instance. That four-Justice analysis is not law. White, Scalia and Thomas also warn of serial border exceptions; three votes establish no rule. Souter, joined by Kennedy, accepts the actual application and declines the broader level-of-generality question. Stone agrees and emphasizes both the burden on the lawful Virginia audience and the continuing availability of materially different as-applied challenges. Stevens, joined by Blackmun, dissents under Bigelow, which protects truthful information about lawful out-of-state activity, and Discovery Network, which requires a demonstrated fit. They regard paternalistic suppression and the overwhelmingly Virginia audience as fatal to the justification, not merely matters answered by federalism. The Court neither adopts their broader interstate-information rule nor forecloses the preserved alternative claim.

#### TXO Production Corp. v. Alliance Resources — 1993-06-25

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stevens | Blackmun | Plurality on substantive excessiveness and potential harm; judgment announcement. | Affirm |
| Stevens | Blackmun, Kennedy | Procedural discussion: reject review and notice objections; decline distinct wealth/additional-compensation instruction attack as not properly presented. | Affirm; three formal joiners |
| Kennedy | No other joiners | Concurrence in part and in judgment: rational punishment for proven deliberate misconduct, not appellate potential-harm arithmetic. | Affirm |
| Scalia | Thomas | Judgment concurrence: traditional jury procedures and actual judicial reasonableness review satisfy procedural due process; reject substantive numerical review. | Affirm |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Dissent: inadequate assurance against arbitrary punishment requires record-based review; no numerical cap or automatic retrial. | Vacate punitive component and remand |
| O’Connor | White | Dissent: disproportionate amount, prejudice risk and inadequate trial/appellate safeguards warrant relief. | Vacate punitive component; new punitive determination on bias ground or at least adequate review |
| O’Connor | Souter | Souter joins only the portions addressing the jury’s actual instructions, wealth/outsider prejudice, deficient posttrial review and the need for relief; not the entire comparative-proportionality analysis. | Vacate punitive component |

Stevens, joined by Blackmun, would sustain the amount under Haslip’s substantive reasonableness inquiry. They regard the small realized loss as an incomplete measure of a deliberate scheme aimed at valuable royalties; threatened harm, repeated misconduct and financial resources support punishment. They adopt no mathematical ceiling. Kennedy joins their procedural discussion but rejects reliance on reconstructed potential-harm calculations not established as the jury’s actual foundation. His separate ground is that the whole trial record supports rational deterrence and retribution for deliberate malicious conduct rather than demonstrated bias. Scalia, joined by Thomas, rejects an unenumerated substantive right to a correct amount and finds the traditional jury process and judicial review sufficient. Their ground is not a logical subset of Stevens’s substantive review or Kennedy’s motive-focused inquiry; no unified substantive Marks rule follows. Stone dissents: the charge’s additional-compensation language, wealth and outsider appeals require meaningful examination of objective support and constraints on the amount. He accepts supported potential harm and general verdicts, supplies no cap, and would require another punitive determination only if adequate correction cannot occur on the existing record. O’Connor, joined by White throughout, finds both the amount and safeguards deficient; she would order a new punitive determination for the prejudice risk or at least constitutionally adequate review. Souter joins her portions addressing actual jury guidance, prejudice, deficient review and relief, not her whole comparative-proportionality discussion. Stevens/Blackmun/Kennedy regard the specific instruction attack as unpresented; O’Connor/White/Souter treat it as an argument supporting preserved due process under Yee. Stone reaches the preserved review ground without making that disputed waiver disposition necessary. Neither side’s three-vote preservation characterization becomes law.

#### St. Mary's Honor Center v. Hicks — 1993-06-25

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Opinion of the Court only on rejection of automatic liability from falsity alone, evidentiary sufficiency without a mandatory additional-proof category, and common appellate vacatur/remand. | Vacate/remand |
| Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Four-Justice further opinion retaining plaintiff persuasion throughout and directing clear-error review of the district court’s ultimate finding. | Would reverse/remand on that allocation |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence in appellate vacatur and dissent from any retention of the old allocation after his defined trigger; separate proposed Title VII rule and additional remedial directions. | Vacate/remand with a new allocation and district-court reconsideration |
| Souter | White, Blackmun, Stevens | Dissent: Burdine and the structured indirect method require judgment for Hicks after proven prima-facie case and disproof of all offered reasons. | Affirm the liability mandate |

Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, would retain the employee’s ultimate persuasion burden throughout. Rule 301 and Burdine distinguish evidence production from credibility, so a rejected explanation does not revive a compulsory presumption. They regard Aikens as requiring an actual discrimination finding and would review the district court’s finding for clear error under Rule 52(a), without a pretext-plus demand. Souter, joined by White, Blackmun and Stevens, would affirm. Their reading of Burdine gives operative force to the employer’s obligation to identify the reasons the employee must contest; after all are disproved, the indirect route is complete. They object to allowing a factfinder to search for a different unexplained motive after trial and invoke statutory stare decisis. Stone proposes a distinct middle rule, not joined by another Justice. The employee must prove the applicable prima-facie case and affirmatively establish that every material nondiscriminatory explanation offered was not an actual reason. Inaccuracy alone, peripheral discrepancies, or an actual remaining material reason do not trigger the shift; none prevents an otherwise sufficient discrimination claim. After the trigger, the employer must prove absence of the prohibited cause by a preponderance, using the whole admissible record. A merely possible lawful reason or unsupported personal hostility is insufficient; hostility itself may be racial. No fixed documentary form is required, and the employee receives notice and an opportunity to contest any decisive factual theory. Equipoise after the shift favors the employee on causation, while other elements, defenses and relief retain their own sources. Stone openly departs from Burdine’s perpetual persuasion assignment as a Title VII-specific proof construction; Rule 301 supplies no authority for that change, and Price Waterhouse’s different predicate is illustrative rather than controlling. He would retain valid findings, set aside the Title VII judgment, and permit a focused supplemental presentation only where fairness under the changed allocation requires it, with an equivalent response opportunity. The rule grants no routine retrial, successive explanations, same-decision defense or 1991 Act retroactivity determination. Stone would separately reconsider Long’s §1983 judgment without automatically importing the Title VII allocation. The two four-Justice groups do not join this departure: one insists on continuing employee persuasion, the other on completed indirect proof requiring judgment. Their theories and Stone’s are not logical subsets yielding a common affirmative allocation. No Marks conversion establishes the proposed shift.

#### Shaw v. Reno — 1993-06-28

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stone-Zsela | White, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter | Opinion of the Court on the limited state-claim injury rule and resulting dismissal. | Affirm |
| Stone-Zsela | White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court on the separate federal-official disposition. | Affirm |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence elaborating equal political membership, distinct constitutional/statutory protections, and limits on the broader justification framework. | Affirm on the Court’s narrow ground |
| White | Blackmun, Stevens | Concurrence: UJO and the absence of impaired electoral participation support dismissal; racial awareness differs from political exclusion. | Affirm; no adoption by Stone of aggregate racial representation as a defense |
| Stevens | No other joiners | Concurrence: arbitrary districting remains reviewable, but a remedial attention to racial voting patterns is not necessarily forbidden segregation. | Affirm |
| Souter | No other joiners | Concurrence: common territorial representation without exclusion differs from legally separated participation; hypothetical representative behavior supplies no established deprivation. | Affirm |
| O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Dissent as to state claim: pleaded racial separation itself warrants strict scrutiny, with actual justification and narrow tailoring examined on remand. | Reverse state dismissal; no finding that the map is already invalid |

Stone’s concurrence explains why equal political membership concerns actual personal treatment rather than aggregate racial officeholding. Constitutional amendments and lawful statutory and judicial changes have expanded participation without an uninterrupted progression or a guarantee of proportional officeholding. Harper corrects a wealth barrier; Reynolds protects equal voting power; the Reconstruction enforcement cases permit properly supported preventive legislation without a completed local constitutional violation in every covered application. Those authorities do not prove that North Carolina needed these lines. Stone preserves Shelley’s personal-rights principle for white and Black residents alike and rejects historical racial dominance as an answer to an actual deprivation. Croson presents a serious contrary analogy because classification can itself injure; his distinction concerns a common territorial electorate, not a supposed requirement of monetary or electoral loss. Wright left proven racial design unresolved, UJO is fractured, and Whittaker’s Gomillion concurrence advances a broader separation theory Stone declines to extend. Anderson’s ballot-label injury and McLaurin’s restrictions within shared facilities refute any universal claim that formal eligibility suffices. An operative racial designation or discriminatory denial of an otherwise available participation opportunity remains examinable; Knight supplies no general right to force officials to listen. Allen’s personal-treatment standing requirement and Heckler’s noneconomic equality injury remain distinct from success on the merits. Gingles’s behavioral evidence, independent § 2 requirements and Beer’s nonretrogression principle remain intact; neither coalition, crossover nor influence duties are newly decided. Congress’s supported preventive categories and a State’s independent authority remain different. Stone’s broader corrective-permission framework is not adopted as a replacement scrutiny tier, statutory duty or universal defense. Ordinary Rule 15/Foman amendment and preclusion rules remain; he directs no amendment and invents no additional facts.

White, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, finds UJO and the lack of an alleged participation impairment decisive, while recognizing that constitutional injury is personal. Their further account of districting practice is not an independent majority rule. Stevens separately distinguishes a State’s effort to recognize demonstrated racial voting conditions from a command that citizens vote or be represented according to race; his Karcher concern with arbitrary districting does not make shape alone dispositive. Souter distinguishes geographic constituency assignment from segregated access to public institutions and declines to infer that elected representatives will deny service to residents of another race. O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, dissents on the state claim. They would recognize the alleged separation itself as a personal constitutional injury and require a sufficiently supported compelling justification and narrow tailoring under Croson. They treat unusual shape as evidence of racial design, not as an aesthetic constitutional rule. They would reverse dismissal and allow examination of justification, not hold the map invalid on disputed facts or require it to be maximally compact.

#### United States v. Dixon — 1993-06-28

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| White | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: nonsummary punitive contempt triggers criminal jeopardy; private initiation does not categorically defeat protection. | Applies to all components; Blackmun withholds the order-versus-community conclusion |
| White | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter | Opinion of the Court: an already prosecuted necessarily included offense cannot be prosecuted again; Dixon and Foster I are barred. | Affirm these two components |
| Souter | Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens | Opinion of the Court: Grady is retained; successive prosecution is not governed exclusively by Blockburger. | Precedent treatment; Blackmun does not join application barring these contempts |
| Scalia | Kennedy | Plurality explanation of judgment permitting Foster II–V: separate elements and rejection of Grady; incorporation explanation for Dixon/I. | Reverse II–V, affirm Dixon/I |
| O’Connor | Thomas | Partial dissent and judgment concurrence: compare contempt’s abstract order/disobedience elements; would permit every later count. Joins Scalia/Kennedy’s proposed Grady overruling only. | Dissent Dixon/I; reverse II–V |
| Blackmun | No other joiners | Partial dissent and judgment concurrence: vindicating the court’s order differs from punishing an offense against the community; permit all counts while retaining Grady for ordinary substantive prosecutions. | Dissent Dixon/I; reverse II–V |
| White | Stevens, Souter | Additional concurrence/dissent on substantive-offense comparison and finality: bar all later counts. | Affirm all |
| Souter | Stevens | Additional concurrence/dissent: Nielsen, Harris, Vitale and Grady protect against successive prosecution of the previously tried conduct; oppose II–V result. | Affirm all |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence/dissent: conduct-based application of retained Grady bars each later count; reject manageability challenge on this record. | Affirm all |

Scalia, joined by Kennedy, compares the offenses incorporated by the orders for Dixon and Foster I, but considers Foster II–IV’s specified threats and V’s intent to kill additional elements absent from the contempt charges. Each contempt also requires an order and its known violation. They would overrule Grady, preserve Ashe’s independent protection and allow those additional charges. O’Connor, joined by Thomas, instead compares the abstract contempt elements to every substantive offense and would allow all later prosecutions; they agree with Scalia/Kennedy on overruling Grady but not on the incorporated-offense bars. Blackmun would permit all counts for a different reason: Young’s judicial-order enforcement serves interests distinct from the community’s ordinary criminal law. He retains Grady and objects to its abandonment for ordinary successive substantive prosecutions. His special contempt ground is not a logical subset of a Blockburger-only rule.

White, joined by Stevens and Souter in his additional discussion, would compare the substantive offenses actually prosecuted and bar every renewed charge. He regards adding intent to kill as no answer to the finality of an already tried assault and emphasizes acquittal finality on the threats. Souter, joined by Stevens in his further discussion, reads Nielsen, Harris and Vitale as a continuing protection against serial prosecution of the same criminal conduct, distinguishes multiple punishment from repeated trials, and opposes the II–V judgment. Only his limited Grady-retention section has the five named Court joins. Stone separately applies retained Grady count by count: Dixon’s drug conduct, Foster’s November assault, all three acquitted threats and the May assault are precisely the conduct the later charges would require proving. He treats Felix as a separate-agreement case and does not impose compulsory joinder of every charge from an episode. He would affirm all bars and therefore would not need an additional Ashe determination. That last procedural preference does not eliminate the Ashe inquiry preserved by the actual remand.

#### Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals — 1993-06-28

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Blackmun | Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: Frye displaced and judgment vacated. | Vacate and remand |
| Blackmun | Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: reliable scientific foundation and fit, Rule 104(a) preponderance, flexible considerations, other evidentiary/sufficiency safeguards and limits. | Eight direct joins |
| Stevens | No other joiners | Concurrence in the judgment and in displacement: further abstract scientific-method prescriptions should await concrete application. | Vacate and remand |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence: data quality and inference explain fit; civil-rights merits and legislative-fact questions are not resolved here. | Vacate and remand |

Stevens concurs in displacement and the vacatur but declines the further scientific-method discussion. He accepts some judicial screening responsibility yet considers abstract guidance insufficiently tied to a decision admitting or excluding a particular proffer; lower courts should develop the issue on concrete records. Stone concurs in the Court’s operative standard and emphasizes that an unpublished reanalysis must explain its data, choices and inference, while animal studies require a defensible bridge to the human proposition. Data quality is relevant rather than a universal extra element; litigation origin and scientific minority status do not establish unreliability. He distinguishes expert admissibility from legislative facts and substantive civil rights standards: no consensus prerequisite for constitutional enforcement, universal statistical significance level, mandatory regression model or retrospective change to earlier voting cases follows.

#### Austin v. United States — 1993-06-28

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Blackmun | Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Common statutory punishment/coverage and judgment sections; each join limited to the actual statutes and remand. | Reverse and remand |
| Blackmun | White, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter | Opinion of the Court: historical partly punitive understanding and civil-label analysis. | Five Justice full historical rationale |
| Blackmun | Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter | Opinion of the Court: remand without an exclusive instrumentality constraint; other relevant factors may be considered. | Six direct joins |
| Scalia | No other joiners | Concurrence in part and in judgment: taking lawful property is partly punitive; excessiveness should depend on the property’s traditional instrumentality connection, not relative value. | Reverse and remand; different test |
| Kennedy | Thomas | Concurrence in part and in judgment: actual statutory forfeiture covered; reserve whether all historical in rem forfeitures punished owners. | Reverse and remand; does not join Scalia’s exclusive test |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence: proposed nonexclusive value, seriousness, culpability and role considerations; no comprehensive formula. | Reverse and remand |

Scalia agrees that taking lawful property under these provisions is partly punitive and constitutes a fine. He rejects the need to establish owner blameworthiness across all historical forfeitures and would measure excessiveness through the property’s traditional closeness to the offense, treating value as irrelevant to that particular instrumentality inquiry. His exclusive test is not the Court’s rule. Kennedy, joined by Thomas, accepts the actual statutory conclusion but cautions against anachronistically imposing one personal-punishment theory on every historical forfeiture. He reserves whether such forfeitures invariably punish their owners and the constitutional question involving a truly innocent owner; he does not adopt Scalia’s exclusive remand test. Stone accepts coverage from the actual culpability-sensitive and deterrent statutory operation. He would examine the interest and supported value taken, seriousness and extent of wrongdoing, relevant owner culpability and the property’s actual role as nonexclusive considerations. He adopts no mandatory ratio, exhaustive checklist or later gross-disproportionality formula. The common remand leaves that choice oftest open rather than converting Stone’s proposals intolaw.

#### Hartford Fire Insurance v. California — 1993-06-28

| Writing / author | Joined by | Exact scope | Judgment relationship |
|---|---|---|---|
| Souter | Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: activity-specific exemption and domestic component dispositions. | Partial affirmance/partial reversal |
| Scalia | Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Opinion of the Court: collateral transaction boycott boundary and pleading application. | Permit California I–IV/Connecticut I–II; exclude VII/VI |
| Souter | White, Blackmun, Stevens | Concurrence in domestic judgment: broader enforcement-activity interpretation of boycott; primary insurers’ enlistment of reinsurers suffices without a categorical collaterality requirement. | Same domestic dispositions |
| Souter | Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens | Opinion of the Court: substantial intended U.S. effects and absence of the asserted conflicting duty support adjudication here; broader comity questions reserved. | Affirm No.91-1128 |
| Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Dissent on foreign claims: prescriptive comity and Charming Betsy restrict substantive reach despite federal subject-matter jurisdiction. | Dismiss London-only claims |
| Stone-Zsela | No other joiners | Concurrence: §6a claim connection where applicable, distinction between judicial power/substantive coverage/comity, and no universal no-impossibility rule. | Concur in component-specific judgment |

Souter, joined by White, Blackmun and Stevens on the boycott question, would recognize primary insurers’ enlistment of reinsurers to enforce their desired market terms as coercive enforcement beyond ordinary cooperative drafting. Barry and South-Eastern Underwriters do not, in their view, confine every boycott to formally collateral transactions. They agree that the principal counts survive and that the model umbrella/excess counts lack any refusal allegation. Scalia, joined by O’Connor, Kennedy and Thomas, dissents from allowing the London-only claims. He accepts federal subject-matter jurisdiction over nonfrivolous statutory claims and Congress’s power, but reads Charming Betsy, Lauritzen and Romero as requiring source-based prescriptive comity. The foreign actors, center of activity and British regulatory interest favor nonapplication even without an incompatible legal command. He would dismiss for failure to state a claim rather than invoke lack of judicial power. Stone’s concurrence preserves the actual §6a claim connection requirement where applicable and separates that statutory limit from discretionary comity. The American-market operation alleged here supports continued adjudication; he adopts no power over wholly foreign injury lacking the required connection and no general rule that comity is irrelevant whenever two laws can both be obeyed.

#### Alexander v. United States — 1993-06-28

Source: [published writings in the decision record](records/Alexander_v_United_States_merits_1993-06-28.md).

**Kennedy, joined by Blackmun and Stevens, and by Souter as to the protected-inventory discussion, concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.** All four join Part II's corrective remand. They would go further and hold unconstitutional forfeiture of expressive inventory not adjudged obscene or otherwise unprotected when imposed here as punishment for obscenity. Marcus and Quantity of Books protect each work rather than permit one unlawful title to taint neighboring lawful works. Arcara involved temporary closure for prostitution while the books remained available elsewhere; it did not authorize destruction of the inventory. Kennedy, Blackmun and Stevens also would invalidate the speech-enterprise and influence-property forfeiture under the principles underlying Near and Vance. Their concern is suppression of future expression, not merely the amount of punishment. Souter does not join that broader enterprise rationale or the rejection of Part I's classification; he joins Parts I–III of the principal opinion. The three broader writers do not join Parts I or III, but accept the Excessive Fines remand as alternative relief. Their preferred categorical inventory rule has four votes, and their broader enterprise rule three. The common remand does not adopt either as controlling law.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins Parts I–III and explains his proposed method for the unresolved First Amendment inquiry. Ordinary sanctions for nonexpressive misconduct do not require special scrutiny merely because a publisher loses assets. Where expressive predicates draw the sanction and lawful inventory is removed, a genuinely content-neutral incidental restriction must satisfy O'Brien: constitutional authority, an important or substantial interest unrelated to suppressing expression, and a restriction no greater than essential to that interest. Suppression because of protected content requires compelling justification and narrow tailoring. He distinguishes transfer of forfeitable economic interests from gratuitous elimination of lawful works and would examine whether lawful realization of the assets can accomplish the authorized punishment. There is no universal least-restrictive-means requirement or categorical inventory immunity. The judicial order, administrative destruction, causation, reviewability and available correction remain distinct; destroyed stock cannot be returned by this appeal and no damages action is created. On excessiveness, the interests and supported value taken, seriousness and extent of wrongdoing, culpability and property's role are nonexclusive proposed considerations, not a binding numerical formula. These additional specifications are his alone, not added to the Court's holdings.

**White, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** They join Parts I and III but oppose Part II and would reject the First Amendment challenge. In their view the adjudicated RICO violation and established nexus permit a generally applicable forfeiture without converting the assets' expressive character into constitutional immunity. Fort Wayne Books accepts substantial punishment for obscenity, and Arcara does not require special scrutiny of every sanction affecting a bookseller. They regard the statutory forfeiture and its deterrent consequences as punishment rather than a governmental system controlling future publication. They preserve objection to targeted censorship or a future-publication prohibition, but see no basis for the additional judicial-forfeiture remand on this record. They agree that excessiveness is independently reviewable under the Eighth Amendment. Their First Amendment approval has four votes and does not control.

DeBoer A-64 publishes no separate writing or legal rationale. Delo remains unadjudicated and adds no public position.

### 5. Current Procedure and Institution

Current roster after the June 28 decisions: Chief Stone-Zsela; associates Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, in seniority order. White participated through Alexander and retired after the complete date group; Ginsburg is not yet seated. Quorum six; affirmative full-Court relief requires five of eight; certiorari still requires four. Earlier nine-member decisions retain their recorded participation. Continuing allotments: Stone D.C./Fourth/Federal; Souter First; Scalia Second/Third; O'Connor Fifth; Stevens Sixth/Seventh; Blackmun Eighth; Kennedy Ninth; Thomas Eleventh. No Justice is silently assigned the Tenth Circuit; an intervening application there requires a recorded reassignment. Listed interim applications continue to use full-Court referral. DeBoer A-64 is complete; Delo A-69 remains stopped and has no new Court order.

Grubbs's administrative stay remains operative pending further Court order. Its application requires prompt examination of the actual application and State response; no long-term stay, merits disposition or automatic October 21 dissolution is inferred. Coleman v. Thompson is not an OT1992 matter; it is resolved within OT1991 by post-close correction. Zatko, Wyoming and Reynolds retain the opening states above. Martin's lead petition is closed; its companion fee motion is complete, with the underlying petition still open pending a verified compliance or subsequent Court event. Each completed merits remand retains the case-specific remedy and unresolved issues in its record; remand does not imply a new Supreme Court filing or continuing retained jurisdiction.

- **Grubbs v. Delo:** Administrative stay operative; actual application and State response must be examined promptly; continued relief requires lawful review authority and the ordinary stay showing.
- **Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott:** The November 2 order grants IFP and denies certiorari in No. 92-5584. That petition proceeding is closed; the D.C. Circuit judgment remains undisturbed. In No. 92-5618, only the fee motion is decided. Martin may pay the Rule 38 docketing fee and submit a petition complying with Rule 33 by November 23, 1992. Ordinary petition processing requires that compliance; the order neither denies the petition nor commands an automatic later denial. No payment, default, extension or subsequent Court action is established. The Delaware judgment is not vacated, affirmed on the merits or remanded. No prospective filing restriction issues. At the continuing term cursor, November 23 has passed, but calendar passage alone supplies no terminal event; No. 92-5618 remains an open compliance-dependent matter.
- **Montana v. Imlay:** Supreme Court writ terminated; later sentence untouched.
- **Church of Scientology of California v. United States:** Supreme Court review complete; Ninth Circuit summons-enforcement appeal restored on remand.
- **Hadley v. United States:** Supreme Court review concluded by DIG; Ninth Circuit judgment remains undisturbed.
- **Parke v. Raley:** Sixth Circuit conditional writ reversed; district rejection of present habeas challenge stands; no new hearing required.
- **Richmond v. Lewis:** Federal district conditional habeas order on remand; Arizona must cure sentence within reasonable period or vacate death/impose lawful lesser sentence.
- **Soldal v. Cook County:** Supreme Court merits review complete; seizure claim remanded for further proceedings below. No retained Supreme Court matter or later filing is established.
- **Farrar v. Hobby:** Supreme Court review complete; fee judgment vacated and amount review remanded. No retained Supreme Court proceeding or later filing established.
- **District of Columbia v. Greater Washington Board of Trade:** Supreme Court review complete; affirmed appellate judgment governs further district-court proceedings in the preemption action.
- **Mississippi v. Louisiana:** Supreme Court merits review complete; interstate complaint must be dismissed below; remaining title proceedings remanded. No new original action or retained Supreme Court matter.
- **Republic National Bank of Miami v. United States:** Supreme Court merits review of the jurisdictional dismissal complete; forfeiture merits appeal remanded to the Eleventh Circuit. No retained Supreme Court matter or actual payment order.

- **Puerto Rico Aqueduct & Sewer Authority v. Metcalf & Eddy, Inc., 1993-01-12:** Supreme Court review of appellate jurisdiction is complete. The First Circuit judgment is reversed and the matter remanded for proceedings consistent with the collateral-order ruling, including consideration of the asserted immunity. The Court does not direct that PRASA be declared immune, dismiss the contract action, award damages, or order any particular result on the contract merits. No retained Supreme Court proceeding or new filing deadline is created.
- **Bath Iron Works Corp. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 1993-01-12:** Supreme Court merits review is complete. The First Circuit judgment is affirmed; Brown retains the award.
